# Revised frozen neutral packet — California Democratic Party v. Jones

Fresh neutral validation completed November 3, 2026. This packet supplies no vote, commitment, target historical Supreme Court result or Stone position. It supersedes the generic and stale framing of this case's runtime Section I for independent modeling; the approved runtime source remains unchanged. Sources are copied below without retyping their substantive passages.

## Event, scope and participation

No. 99-401; merits review of the Ninth Circuit on June 26, 2000, following April 24, 2000 argument. The question is whether California may compel political parties to select their official nominees through the described blanket primary. The nine seated Justices are Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No case-specific nonparticipation is identified in the authorized materials. Independent modeling addresses only the eight Associates.

The opening law is the OT1998-close set, supplemented by effective current-term law. The chronology cursor is June 26, 2000. Uncoordinated June 26 decisions share entering law and do not govern one another. No supplied dependency makes this case a coordinated sequel. The current projection records no institutional change.

## Fresh framing and completeness determinations

The State's law selects an official nominee for each party; party-committee voting remains limited to that party's registrants. The surviving private party functions, electoral access interests and empirical findings must all be considered without turning the lower court's constitutional characterization of the burden into an established fact. Outsider influence, sincere crossover voting, strategic voting and organized raiding are distinct. Popular support is not party consent. The four parties' opposing bylaws are part of the record. The packet preserves the qualified predictive nature of crossover and participation evidence and does not invent California totals.

Both the parties' claim of compelled control over a standard bearer and the State's claim of authority over a public nominating process receive substantive treatment in the copied source extraction. A decision may sustain the law on actual burden and proportionate interests, apply strict scrutiny to a severe burden, or find inadequate fit even without declaring the burden severe. Neither the classification nor the outcome is predetermined. Party-authorized participation, a conventional open primary, nonpartisan candidate winnowing and an official party nomination are separate arrangements; this case need not adjudicate them all.

The focused entering-law slice requires one addition: Reform Party of Allegheny County, decided April 10, 2000. Its exact sanitized public projection is copied below. Its common rule concerns unequal access to a timely pre-primary cross-nomination opportunity, not a free-standing right to every nomination mechanism. Its explanation that endorsement does not replace the legal nomination opportunity is relevant to the parties' claimed burden here, while its equality premise, timing and confined relief limit the analogy. It neither predetermines Jones nor changes Anderson–Burdick scrutiny. Any source-map statement that no relevant current-term nomination decision exists is superseded by this explicit addition.

The controlling law and source extraction preserve statutory party-committee limits; state and congressional scope; the changed presidential reporting issue; absence of a granted Elections Clause initiative question; and the difference between prospective relief, reopening completed elections and judicial creation of a new ballot system. No damages award, automatic redesign, or adjudication of unpresented presidential-delegate rules is supported.

## Validated objective source extraction

## Event and exact judgment below

No. 99-401. Certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Ninth Circuit's March 4, 1999 judgment, 169 F.3d 646, affirming after independent constitutional review the Eastern District of California's judgment, 984 F. Supp. 1288 (November 17, corrected November 24, 1997). Rehearing was denied June 4, 1999. The Ninth Circuit (B. Fletcher, Hug and Trott) adopted the district court's opinion in full; no appellate dissent appears in the reproduced opinion. The district court decided after a four-day bench trial, not a pleading dismissal or a stipulation that no burden existed. Plaintiffs sought declaratory and injunctive relief against implementation of Proposition 198. The approved granted question concerns compulsory outsider voting in a party's nominee selection; the petition also phrased a broader private-association comparison, which does not enlarge the approved grant.

## Governing enactment and material findings

Proposition 198 was adopted in March 1996. Elections Code §2001 gives otherwise qualified electors, including unaffiliated voters, the right to vote for candidates regardless of political affiliation, subject to other law. Conforming amendments affect §§2151, 13102, 13203, 13206, 13230, 13300, 13301 and 13302. Each voter receives the same ballot, candidates appear with party identification, and the voter can cross party lines office by office. Section 15451 makes each party's highest vote recipient its nominee at the general election. Thus this is a partisan nomination system, distinct from a nonpartisan first round advancing the highest candidates without choosing an official nominee for each party. Party committee elections remain limited to registrants of the relevant party. All four plaintiff parties—the Democratic, Republican, Libertarian, and Peace and Freedom parties—had bylaws opposing outsider voting in their primaries.

The trial court found a significant but nonsevere associational burden. That is a constitutional characterization to be reviewed, not a binding historical fact deciding the present legal issue. Its underlying findings included:

- Crossover voting would increase. Evidence from Washington suggested roughly ten percent per office, sometimes twenty to twenty-five percent; some studies distinguished independents from members of competing parties. The California prediction was necessarily uncertain. Do not mix study denominators or turn those estimates into established California election totals.
- Some races over time probably would be decided by crossover voters, especially where competition was asymmetric. The possibility could influence campaigns and officeholders even without changing a particular winner. Campaign costs could rise, discipline could weaken, and activists' morale could decline.
- Organized raiding to nominate a weak opposing candidate was not shown likely to be important. Sincere voting for a preferred candidate and strategic voting for a second choice were separate phenomena. Rare raiding does not establish absence of outsider influence.
- Major parties remained vigorous in Washington. The court did not expect serious organizational debilitation; endorsements, campaigning, financial support, platforms, registration and turnout work remained available. Nominees' participation in governing committees could be addressed by party rules. Those alternatives do not literally restore control of the legal nomination.
- The court found the special effects alleged for minor parties inadequately proved, noting infrequent contested primaries and the different incentives of major-party voters. This was a finding on the trial showing, not a rule that minor-party autonomy has no protection.
- Proposition 198 received substantial voter support, but the court expressly rejected the contention that polling of party registrants constituted the parties' consent. Party rules prescribe their own deliberative amendment procedures; inferred popular approval is not their replacement.

## Reasoning below and both sides

The lower courts applied the Anderson–Burdick balancing inquiry, relying on the actual system and evidence rather than treating every outsider vote as automatically fatal. They characterized primaries as the public first stage in selection of officeholders, distinguished party governance from state election machinery, and treated compulsory registration as already an imperfect measure of political association. They found the burden significant but short of severe and the aggregate state interests sufficient: representativeness, broader debate, participation of independents and minority-party voters in safe districts, greater choice, competition and turnout, perceived fairness, political stability, and privacy. Their description of certain voters as effectively disenfranchised meant limited practical influence in safe districts, not legal ineligibility to register or vote. Empirical support for increased competitiveness and turnout was qualified. The courts rejected mere state paternalism about what would help a party, yet accepted electorate-centered representativeness and participation interests.

The parties' supported argument is that selection of the official standard bearer defines their political message and is a core associational act. Compulsory participation by people who retain an opposing affiliation can change that act even when their votes are sincere. Tashjian, Eu and La Follette protect a party's choice of participants; ordinary election regulation and the actual Timmons fusion restriction do not necessarily transfer the selection decision to outsiders. Endorsing a different candidate cannot undo the statutory nominee's status. The change in the nominating electorate is the asserted injury, so lack of proof that parties will collapse is not dispositive. A government desire to moderate a party's positions can itself be the burden challenged. The parties acknowledge lawful administration and primary requirements, subject to constitutional limits; their private-association analogy does not erase the white-primary cases.

California and the defending intervenors argue that public, state-financed nomination is an integral governmental electoral function carrying ballot-access advantages, not simply a private club's meeting. State authority to structure that function and electors' participation interests deserve weight. Tashjian addressed a party's invitation to independents, Eu internal governance and endorsements, and La Follette national-convention delegate rules; none automatically decides this blanket primary. California stresses the trial findings, the survival of party advocacy, the small practical differences among blanket, open and easily changed registration systems, the nondiscriminatory application to parties, and privacy without a public party declaration. Its merits brief asserts later participation improvements; those are litigation assertions unless their evidentiary basis is independently established, not substituted trial findings. The trial court's rejection of implied party consent must accompany any reliance on popular approval.

Analytical possibilities under the entering law include sustaining the system after actual-burden review; finding severe compelled association and testing precise interests and fit; or finding an insufficient fit even on a less-than-severe classification. These are available arguments, not vote predictions. The lawful distinction between electoral administration and control of a party's own official nominee must be addressed whichever path is selected. A source showing conventional open primaries or nonpartisan systems exist is not their constitutional adjudication in this case.

## Scope, remedy and source limits

The case concerns state-office and congressional nominations. California's merits brief identifies amended presidential-preference reporting provisions (§§15151, 15375, 15500, amended May 4, 1999) permitting national parties to apply their own rules; do not decide national-delegate treatment on the old trial assumption. A ruling can be limited to compulsory blanket selection of official party nominees without deciding a party-authorized open primary, a conventional one-party-ballot open primary, nonpartisan elections, or the validity of every registration rule. An independent Elections Clause objection to initiative enactment is not part of the supplied granted question; do not decide it without an established preserved basis. The current materials establish no need to invalidate completed elections or specify an entirely new ballot system. A reversal requires relief fitted to the actual unconstitutional operation and an ordinary conforming remand; affirmance leaves the existing defense judgment in place. There is no damages determination to invent.

No missing transcript or JA fact is certified absent. The collection holds and this preparation saved the petition, both merits briefs, joint appendix and reply. The extraction read the lower-court opinions reproduced in the petition and relevant merits-summary/argument passages; it has not certified a line-by-line review of all 316 KB of JA OCR. Any proposed holding depending on an exact transcript concession, an additional empirical number, an omitted amicus proposition or later presidential change needs that particular original passage checked. The existing supported judicial recitations suffice for the framed associational question under Engine §14(2), subject to fresh validation.

## Date-eligible Justice source map

The controlling simulated Timmons merits opinion is Thomas's, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Breyer. It validates the particular nonfusion restriction through contextual burden and fit, preserving party association, actual nomination alternatives, and a distinction from internal-party control. This supplies each of those five Justices' own adopted position, not a vote on Jones. Stevens's public Timmons dissent, joined by Ginsburg and in Parts I–II by Souter, emphasizes the actual standard-bearer choice and the inadequacy of substitution by endorsement; Stevens/Ginsburg reject protection of established parties' competitive position. Souter's own separate dissent reserves a demonstrated stability interest linked to effective government. The exact controlling holdings and these public prior positions are copied into the focused law slice.

All eight Associates joined Souter's simulated Hurley opinion protecting an actual private expressive composition, with limits for ordinary services and without treating use of public streets as government expression. Its attributable-expression premise requires application rather than automatic transfer to primaries. The surviving pre-divergence Tashjian opinions also matter. O'Connor joined Scalia's separate dissent; Scalia joined Stevens's separate dissent, which concerns federal/state legislative voter qualifications. The exact attribution, grounds and qualifications appear in the sanitized source correction copied below. La Follette's national-convention holding has an expressly bounded institutional setting. These older writings supply different analytic considerations; no Jones prediction is encoded here. The fresh model may retrieve their whole separate writings when a particular fork depends on their exact scope.

Source: `state/holdings/election-law.md`; exact heading: Burdick v. Takushi, 504 U.S. 428 (1992)

#### Burdick v. Takushi, 504 U.S. 428 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-535.
**Decided:** June 8, 1992.

##### What level of constitutional scrutiny applies to a restriction on voting or candidate access

**Holding:** A court must weigh the character and magnitude of the injury to First and Fourteenth Amendment rights against the State's precise asserted interests and the extent to which they require the burden. Severe restrictions must be narrowly drawn to advance a compelling interest; reasonable, nondiscriminatory restrictions ordinarily are justified by important regulatory interests, so strict scrutiny does not govern every election rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas; five Justices control the complete rule. Kennedy's dissent, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, and Stone's dissent also accept the calibrated framework without making their contrary application controlling.

**Material application:** A write-in prohibition must be examined with the available routes to ballot access and its role in the selection system. Neither the prohibition alone nor the general need for election administration decides the burden.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary administration remains subject to meaningful scrutiny. The holding neither imposes strict scrutiny on all ballot regulations nor extends *Burson*'s public-sidewalk campaign-content holding to the official ballot.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Anderson v. Celebrezze*, 460 U.S. 780, supplies the burden, precise-interest and necessity inquiry. *Norman v. Reed*, January 14, 1992, preserves actual access review and permissible electorate-specific support requirements, including its protection against cross-component exclusion. *Storer v. Brown*, 415 U.S. 724, recognizes substantial regulation of candidate selection while preserving review of severe barriers. *Burson v. Freeman*, May 26, 1992, concerns ordinary public sidewalks; neither its strict-scrutiny proposition nor its fractured judgment changes this ballot analysis.

##### Whether Hawaii's complete write-in prohibition is valid within the ballot-access system reviewed

**Holding:** Where the complete ballot-access system imposes only reasonable burdens, a write-in prohibition is presumptively valid and may be sustained by proportionate state interests. Hawaii's party and nonpartisan routes provide adequate access, and its prohibition validly supports primary finality, prevention of sore-loser candidacies and party raiding, and its treatment of uncontested primaries.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas; five Justices support both the rationale and affirmance. Stone, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy disagree with this application.

**Current force:** Remains controlling for the complete access system reviewed and its reasonable burdens.

**Material application:** New parties need one percent of registered-voter signatures 150 days before the primary; qualifying established parties retain access. Candidates need 15 signatures for legislative or county offices, or 25 for statewide or federal offices, 60 days before the primary. Nonpartisan candidates advance with ten percent of the primary vote or the votes sufficient to nominate a partisan candidate, whichever is lower; voters choose one party or nonpartisan primary ballot for all offices. These combined routes and restrictions support the limited-burden finding. Lawful selection deadlines need not accommodate every later preference.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Both primary and general-election applications within this system are reached. The Court does not independently validate every surrounding access rule or decide a prohibition within materially more restrictive access rules. Candidate qualifications and reasonable write-in procedures elsewhere remain open; no absolute write-in right or guarantee of competitive races is created.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Ninth Circuit; its reversal of the district court's injunction remains effective. No prospective injunction or replacement election-administration scheme issues.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Jenness v. Fortson*, 403 U.S. 431, permits reasonable demonstrations of support. *Munro v. Socialist Workers Party*, 479 U.S. 189, permits primary winnowing and preventive regulation without proof of a particular past disruption. *Storer v. Brown*, 415 U.S. 724, supports primary finality and restrictions against circumvention. *Anderson v. Celebrezze*, 460 U.S. 780, governs this limited-burden application; the decision creates no exception to its inquiry. *Norman v. Reed*, January 14, 1992, retains protection against unjustified party-name and cross-component disqualification.

##### Whether the Constitution requires the State to count and publish fictional candidates or arbitrary protest messages

**Holding:** The First and Fourteenth Amendments do not require a State to count and publicize write-in votes for fictional figures or arbitrary protest messages as an independent form of expression. Elections select public officials, and a desire to use the ballot for such messages does not create an entitlement to a write-in option.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas, controls. Kennedy's dissent, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, and Stone's dissent expressly agree on rejection of this broader claim; all nine support the component without issuing a joint opinion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A genuine interest in supporting an actual alternative candidate requires the contextual access analysis separately stated above. Political expression outside the ballot and every restriction on eligible candidates in a different system are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reject the arbitrary-message tally claim; it supplies no independent ground for relief from the prohibition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Storer v. Brown*, 415 U.S. 724, supplies the office-selecting function of elections; the ballot is not a general expressive forum. *Anderson v. Celebrezze*, 460 U.S. 780, protects electoral participation without requiring an arbitrary-message tally.

Source: `state/holdings/election-law.md`; exact heading: Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party

#### Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1608.
**Decided:** 1997-04-28.

For the separate questions in Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party, 1997-04-28, see Federal Courts — Mootness and conditional reenactment.

##### This limitation is justified within the complete nomination system

**Holding:** Minnesota may prevent this otherwise qualified minor party from placing its consenting nominee on the ballot under its name when he also seeks or holds the other major party's nomination, where the complete system leaves the candidate available and the party able to nominate other candidates, endorse and campaign. In this application the burden is less than severe and proportionate interests in ballot integrity, defined nomination choices and a stable electoral system justify the restriction under Anderson and Burdick.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Part II, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The challenged 1994 §204B.06 subdivision 1(b) barred an affidavit when another affidavit was on file for any office at the same primary or next general election. Section 204B.04 subdivision 2 barred petition nomination for the same office by a primary candidate, subject to two distinct exceptions. Under §204D.10 subdivision 2, if any major-party primary candidate received ten percent of the average vote for the party's state officers at the last state general election in the relevant district, its highest-vote candidates became nominees; if none met that threshold, none were nominated and all could seek petition nomination under §§204B.07–.09. The relevant state officers were governor, lieutenant governor, secretary of state, state auditor, state treasurer and attorney general. The §204B.13 subdivision 4 nonpartisan-vacancy exception allowed a petition within one week of the vacancy and no later than four calendar days before election; an eligible voter could sign despite voting in the primary or signing another petition. Neither exception applied to Dawkins. The distinct ban on nomination by two major parties is not decided.

The conditional replacement permitted a major-party and one-or-more-minor-party nomination for the same partisan office with written consent of the parties' state chairs; petition nomination also required the candidate's written consent. Losing the major-party primary forfeited the simultaneous minor-party nominations, and a party candidate could not also file as an independent. It retained the nonpartisan-vacancy exception, barred a defeated major-party primary candidate except to fill a §204B.13 vacancy, and repealed the old primary-turnout provision. Its affidavit rule referred to any other office, required petition acceptance and the consent forms, and contained a distinct dual-office exception for a soil-and-water district supervisor outside Anoka, Hennepin, Ramsey and Washington counties and a mayor/council or town-supervisor office in a city or town of no more than 2,500 people wholly or partly within the district. That different-office exception is not fusion. A multiply nominated candidate's name appeared once with party names; major/minor nomination used the major-party position and multiple-minor nomination the first filing party's position. Votes for multiply nominated candidates were excluded from the specified party-status qualification or retention counts. These features neither establish compliance in this case nor receive independent constitutional approval.

The Court recognizes no right to duplicate votes, separate party vote totals or a particular ballot design and adjudicates no independent challenge to consent, counting, format, support or deadline rules.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Anderson and Burdick retain contextual burden, precise interest and fit review. Norman's authorized expansion and independent-candidate protections and Colorado Republican's independent campaign speech remain distinct. Tashjian and Eu protect association without compelling every ballot mechanism; Storer, Jenness and Munro permit reasonable preventive selection rules without proof of past disruption. No voter-incapacity premise, incumbent-protection rationale, pure rational-basis rule or universal anti-fusion approval is adopted.

##### Reversal has the enacted transition consequence, without a retrospective election ruling

**Holding:** Chapter 419 §10 makes its amendments effective for the 1996 state primary and thereafter, suspends them while the McKenna judgment is stayed or its mandate recalled, and provides that reversal causes the amendments to expire and prior law to revive. This reversal engages that expiration-and-revival condition; the remand must respect it without assuming an unproved stay, recall or continuous intervening statutory regime.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Parts I and III, joined by all seven other Associates; eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela agrees to reach the merits without an additional statutory rationale.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand, 5–4, subject to expiration of the amendments and revival of prior law. The completed 1994 election is not reopened, and no intervening filing is adjudicated.

Source: `state/holdings/first-amendment.md`; exact heading: Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston

#### Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-749.
**Decided:** June 19, 1995.

##### Private parade composition and compelled inclusion

**Holding:** Applying a public-accommodations law to compel private parade organizers to include an identified contingent conveying a message they do not choose violates the First Amendment. A parade remains expression despite broad admissions, multiple themes and the absence of a single precise message; the rule does not exempt ordinary nonexpressive goods or services from nondiscrimination law.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices; nine Justices adopt the as-applied private-expression holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Public-street use under a neutral permit does not convert this private parade into a governmental forum. The dismissed and unappealed state-action/public-forum theory against the City is not revived. Independent review of intertwined expression characterizations retains credibility deference. Message misattribution in other settings remains open; no general antidiscrimination exemption or premise that ordinary customer identity is itself a message is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- West Virginia State Board of Education v. Barnette: applies protection against compelled expression.
- Miami Herald Publishing Co. v. Tornillo: applies editorial selection protection.
- Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC: applies existing medium-specific editorial protection, without importing unrelated cable results.
- Roberts v. United States Jaycees; PruneYard Shopping Center v. Robins: distinguish access not materially altering the private expressive composition.
- Bose Corp. v. Consumers Union; Fiske v. Kansas: apply independent review of intertwined First Amendment characterizations while retaining credibility deference.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand with the injunction compelling GLIB's inclusion dissolved. The public-accommodations law remains enforceable where its application does not compel private expression; no fee amount, fine or sanction is decided.

Source: `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`; exact heading: Write-in voting within the complete ballot-access system

### Write-in voting within the complete ballot-access system

**Governing question:** Whether a ban on write-in voting violates electoral-choice or independent expressive rights.

**Current rule:** Under the existing Anderson inquiry, the court assesses the character and magnitude of the actual injury against the State's precise interests and the extent to which those interests require the burden. Severe burdens require narrow tailoring to a compelling interest; reasonable, nondiscriminatory burdens ordinarily may be justified by important regulatory interests. When the complete access system imposes only reasonable burdens, a write-in prohibition is presumptively valid and may be sustained by proportionate interests, including primary finality and prevention of evasive candidacies or party raiding. The ballot need not provide an official count and publication of votes for fictional figures or arbitrary protest messages as an independent expressive entitlement.

**Authority by component:**
- Governing inherited framework: Burdick v. Takushi, “1. Review of election regulations follows the actual burden”, June 8, 1992; White, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas (five), applying Anderson and the earlier simulated Norman rule.
- Limited-burden write-in presumption: Burdick, “2. Hawaii's complete write-in prohibition is valid in its reasonably accessible system,” June 8, 1992; same five Justices.
- Arbitrary-message claim: Burdick, “3. The ballot need not serve as an official tally of arbitrary political messages,” June 8, 1992; same controlling five-Justice opinion, with all nine agreeing in rejection of that component.

**Present operation effective:** Burdick v. Takushi, June 8, 1992, for the write-in presumption and expressive boundary; the underlying Anderson framework is applied unchanged.

**Exceptions and limits:** No absolute validation of all write-in bans follows. Materially more restrictive access systems may impose severe burdens. The rule does not replace strict scrutiny of content-based campaign restrictions on public sidewalks.

**Holdings navigation:** Burdick v. Takushi, 504 U.S. 428 (1992) — Ballot access and political parties.

Source: `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`; exact heading: Private parade composition and compelled messages

### Private parade composition and compelled messages

**Governing question:** Application of a public-accommodations law to an unwanted expressive contingent in a private parade.

**Current rule:** The First Amendment prohibits compelling private parade organizers to include an identified contingent conveying a message they do not choose. Broad admissions, varied themes and absence of a single precise message do not remove a parade’s expressive character. The protection concerns compelled insertion of expression, not an exemption for ordinary nonexpressive goods or services; ordinary customer identity alone is not a message.

**Authority by component:**

- Private parade composition and compelled inclusion: Private parade composition and compelled inclusion, 1995-06-19. Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices; nine Justices adopt the as-applied private-expression holding.

**Present operation effective:** 1995-06-19; the component authorities identified above.

**Exceptions and limits:** Other expressive-association disputes require a comparably material expressive burden rather than a categorical exemption from nondiscrimination law.

**Holdings navigation:** Private parade composition and compelled inclusion.

## Prior public noncontrolling positions: Timmons

Source: `terms/OT1996/output/OT_1996CHUNK7.md`; exact public separate-position paragraphs for Stevens/Ginsburg/Souter, copied without private material.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

Stevens dissents, joined by Ginsburg throughout and Souter in Parts I–II. A willing, sufficiently supported standard bearer is a central associational choice, and endorsing him or choosing someone else does not replace the Party's legally operative nomination. Even without declaring every such burden severe, they find inadequate fit: no defeated candidate seeks reentry, the candidate consents, the other party does not object, support requirements have been met, and another party designation adds no new individual to the race. Norman, Tashjian and Eu require attention to that actual association; Burdick permits reasonable prevention but does not make every general interest sufficient. Stevens and Ginsburg further reject preserving established parties' competitive position as a reason to exclude this consensual alliance. They would affirm at the as-applied scope, without mandating duplicate votes, separate party totals or a court-designed ballot.

Souter also dissents separately. Less-than-severe burden does not end the inquiry into fit. He agrees that the present interests are inadequate, but does not join a categorical rejection of every stability justification. A demonstrated threat to effective governing and a connection between fusion and that threat might justify a restriction; he leaves that question open. His Norman and Burdick positions preserve support requirements and primary finality, neither of which explains this supported nomination of the primary winner.

## Exact additional effective current-term law

### Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections — 2000-04-10

[Record](../records/reform-party-v-allegheny-county_merits_2000-04-10.md)

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Eshenbaugh, No. 97-3359 | Denial of Rule 60(b)(6) relief affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No dissent |
| Childress, No. 96-3677 | Timely pre-primary equal-protection entitlement affirmed; deletion of the independent associational declaration retained, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No dissent |
| Prospective relief within Childress review | Unqualified breadth vacated and remanded for confinement to the timely pre-primary holding, 5–4 | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg would retain the existing breadth |

The lower court must identify and adjust only relief lawfully within the Childress appeal. The separate final Eshenbaugh judgment and obligations outside that review remain intact. This judgment does not automatically restore post-primary enforcement against the Party or reopen completed elections.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg in Parts I and II; Stone-Zsela in Part II; O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas in Part III | Part I: Rule 60 finality, eight Justices. Part II: common timely-filing equality rule, nine. Part III: confined relief, five. |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports Part II and would retain the broader injunction. Stone-Zsela agrees with the Rule 60 disposition without joining Part I's reasoning and adds no ground on that component or on deletion of the independent associational declaration. |

#### Holdings

#### I. A distinct final judgment is not reopened by this Rule 60 motion

**Holding and operative rule:** An intervening decision that does not remove the independent equality premise of a final judgment supplies no extraordinary circumstance requiring reopening under Rule 60(b)(6) on this record. Review of the denial is not a fresh merits appeal, and the separate Childress appeal does not reopen Eshenbaugh's judgment.

**Authority:** Part I of Justice Breyer's opinion, joined by all seven other Associate Justices, controls with eight votes; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela agrees with the disposition only.

**Controlling explanation:** Timmons sustained a particular nomination restriction; it did not erase the independent objection to Pennsylvania's unequal treatment. The Department cannot use the motion as a substitute for further merits review it deliberately forwent. Browder supplies the boundary between a final judgment and review of a later Rule 60 ruling. Rufo and Agostini preserve a distinct route under Rule 60(b)(5) for appropriate prospective adjustment when a qualifying present change warrants it. They do not convert the actual (b)(6) motion into an unpresented (b)(5) proceeding or make every continuing obligation immune from lawful future modification. The abandoned (b)(4) theory is not decided.

#### II. Timely minor-party nomination requires a justification for unequal treatment

**Holding and operative rule:** When Pennsylvania permits major parties to cross-nominate for common-pleas judge, Philadelphia Municipal Court judge, Philadelphia Traffic Court judge, elective school director and justice of the peace, it may not reject an otherwise qualifying minor party's pre-primary nomination solely because the candidate filed major-party petitions or later won those primaries without a sufficient justification for that unequal burden. The court evaluates the actual voting and associational burden, the precise regulatory interests, and their fit; severe restrictions require narrow tailoring to compelling interests, while reasonable nondiscriminatory restrictions ordinarily may rest on important regulatory interests.

**Authority:** All nine Justices join Part II of Justice Breyer's opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** Anderson and Burdick require review of the burden in the actual election system. Unequal access matters to that burden and to the required fit, but does not automatically create a suspect class or a severe restriction. Timmons preserves preventive election regulation in the specific Minnesota system; it does not answer why Pennsylvania permits corresponding major-party cross-nominations while excluding this timely minor-party nomination. Norman protects independently qualified participation while preserving meaningful support requirements.

Childress filed before the primaries and won both major-party nominations. Preventing sore-loser candidacies therefore does not explain her exclusion. Major-party cross-nomination already permits overlapping party support; this additional association adds no individual candidate. Voluntary nomination does not involuntarily divert the Party's votes, and allowing major-party alliances while excluding this one does not itself promote new candidates. The asserted interests fail to explain the distinction actually drawn. Endorsement and the candidate's continued ballot availability do not replace the withheld legal nomination opportunity. Late nonpartisanship and voter-identification arguments provide no preserved factual basis curing that defect. The Commonwealth's absence is not default.

The Court decides no independent associational claim, fixed new scrutiny tier, universal fusion right, duplicate-vote entitlement, party-specific total, or ballot design. Otherwise lawful neutral fusion restrictions, deadlines, support requirements and consent rules remain available.

#### III. Relief in Childress must follow the application proved

**Holding and operative rule:** Childress's timely-filing violation supports relief against that unequal application, not an unconditional entitlement to add a nomination after the major-party primaries. The unqualified prospective relief is vacated only insofar as it lies within Childress review, and the lower court must confine that relief while preserving final obligations outside the appeal.

**Authority:** Part III of Justice Breyer's opinion is joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas and controls with five votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Simultaneous nomination and a nomination added after primary selection can implicate different finality interests. Burdick and the limited Timmons holding preserve room for lawful selection rules. The Court does not enact a new deadline or hold every post-primary restriction valid; it declines to make Childress's proved application an unconditional guarantee of later nomination. Pennsylvania's severability rule preserves valid applications unless they are inseparably dependent on the invalid portion or cannot operate consistently with legislative intent. Confined relief can remedy the timely exclusion without judicially replacing the election code. The uncertain relationship between the original decrees must be resolved in implementing relief lawfully before the lower court, not assumed to dissolve Eshenbaugh's independent final obligations.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Anderson and Burdick are applied for review of actual burdens, asserted interests and fit; their calibrated election review remains in force.
- Timmons is distinguished by Pennsylvania's unequal permitted nomination opportunity. Its particular Minnesota holding remains intact and is not enlarged into universal fusion approval or prohibition.
- Norman is applied to independently qualified party participation and the continuing election controversy; meaningful support rules remain available.
- Storer, Jenness and Munro retain their allowance for preventive election administration; that allowance does not dispense with a justification for the distinction imposed here.
- Browder is applied to the boundary between final merits judgments and review of later Rule 60 rulings.
- Rufo and Agostini retain their Rule 60(b)(5) prospective-modification rules; neither is displaced by the disposition of this (b)(6) motion.

#### Law After Decision

Pennsylvania must afford equal access to the timely pre-primary cross-nomination opportunity described in Part II. Later nominations receive no categorical entitlement from Childress's holding and are not categorically declared invalid. The separate final Eshenbaugh obligations remain effective. The decision changes no general scrutiny tier and recognizes no freestanding fusion right.

#### Source Notes

The [published Third Circuit opinion, including its separate writings](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/174/174.F3d.305.96-3677.97-3359.html), supplies the lower appellate numbers, procedural histories, statutory offices and December 9, 1996 decree description. It does not establish whether that decree combined, replaced, duplicated or supplemented earlier decrees. The original decrees and any consolidation or amendment entries would resolve that implementation question. The limited remand does not presume their relationship. Grant and argument dates follow the Court's scheduled review of this matter.

## Neutral-handoff lineage and source correction

This revised neutral freeze supersedes the original Jones neutral freeze solely for the corrected pre-divergence Tashjian attribution and related source qualifications. The original freeze remains preserved. This is a preparation correction before provisional commitments, not a change to an adjudication. The prior neutral receipt's assertion that all supplied attribution was ready is qualified by this identified error. No Jones outcome, vote or Stone position is supplied by the correction.

## Tashjian attribution and grounds

Source: [Tashjian v. Republican Party of Connecticut, 479 U.S. 208 (1986)](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/479/208), syllabus and complete separate writings, including their footnotes.

The neutral source-map statement that O'Connor joined the majority is erroneous. Marshall's majority was joined by Brennan, White, Blackmun and Powell. Stevens dissented separately, joined by Scalia. Scalia's separate dissent was joined by Rehnquist and O'Connor.

Stevens's dissent, beginning at 230, concerns constitutional equivalence of federal and state legislative voter qualifications. It is not a join in Scalia's separate associational analysis.

Scalia's dissent, beginning at 234, favors contextual accommodation of association and state election administration. It treats members' selection of their own candidate as protected, but finds little associational interest in outsourcing selection to unwilling nonmembers. It permits Connecticut to require a democratic vote by party members, even if party leadership prefers outsiders' participation. Those grounds do not automatically answer compulsory outsider selection.

The majority's footnotes 6 and 13 distinguish invited independents from unwanted participants and reserve different considerations for invitation of other parties' members. Neither writing decides the present blanket-primary question.

## Related bounded source verification

[Eu v. San Francisco County Democratic Central Committee, 489 U.S. 214 (1989)](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/489/214) identifies all participating Justices, including Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy, as joining Marshall; Rehnquist did not participate. Stevens's full concurrence at 233 adopts reservations about formulaic compelling-interest and least-restrictive-means language, while accepting the judgment and opinion with those reservations. It must not be represented as an objection to every associational remedy.

[Democratic Party v. Wisconsin ex rel. La Follette, 450 U.S. 107 (1981)](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/450/107) was opened for its bounded national-convention setting. No additional Justice-specific attribution from that source is proposed by this note.

# Frozen neutral packet — Henerey v. City of St. Charles, School District

Fresh neutral validation completed November 3, 2026. This packet supplies no modeled vote, assignment, Stone position or target historical Supreme Court outcome. It supplements and corrects generic framing in the approved runtime Section I without editing that input. Exact saved source handoffs are copied below.

## Event and current law

The approved simulation grant was March 20, 2000, with argument or submission May 2 and merits consideration June 26. The review concerns the December 29, 1999 Eighth Circuit judgment at 200 F.3d 1128, affirming summary judgment. These Supreme Court calendar placements are supplied scheduling premises, not recovered historical orders. The nine seated Justices are Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; no case-specific nonparticipation is supplied. Independent modeling concerns the eight Associates.

Use the OT1998-close law as supplemented by effective current-term law, including June 19 Santa Fe at its actual scope. The current cursor is June 26. Uncoordinated June 26 decisions share entering law and operate together only prospectively. No current-case legal conclusion follows from the fact that review was granted.

## Independent framing and completeness checks

The first granted issue concerns whether this particular expression is independent student advocacy or part of the school's sponsored educational process; the second concerns the permissible enforcement of genuinely viewpoint-neutral rules. The grant's wording supplies no stipulation that this enforcement was neutral. The court must identify the governing protection before deciding whether the actual grounds and sanction satisfy it.

The record is at summary judgment, with evidence and reasonable inferences viewed favorably to Henerey. The majority's tacit-approval and neutrality conclusions and Wolle's contrary pretext, notice and sanction inferences must remain distinct. No trial found either the asserted pretext or its absence. The principal disqualified Henerey before the completed count revealed his majority; the packet does not invent retaliation for the electoral result. The number of distributed items differs in the reported accounts and does not require an invented reconciliation.

The principal's stated advance-approval ground and the appellate court's independent content-related ground must each be tested. A supported lawful ground may sustain judgment, but a merely imaginable reason is not the actual causation showing. An inference of one impermissible reason is not automatically final liability: Settle preserves the protected-conduct and substantial-or-motivating-factor showing and a separate preponderance same-decision defense. Neither the validity of a general approval rule nor the mere controversial character of a subject settles actual enforcement or sanction.

KJ-R's copied text preserves separate location approval, previous-day-or-earlier approval, additional time for difficult materials, stamping, the listed unacceptable categories, the superintendent-or-assistant fundraising exception, and the residual permission for materials outside the forbidden categories. The policy excerpt is expressly incomplete. It cannot establish a fixed twenty-four-hour decision guarantee, a universal disruption condition for every category, or the validity of an unquoted clause.

The available paths are to apply Hazelwood and sustain a supported legitimate sanction, to apply Hazelwood but vacate for a material factual or legal defect in the actual grounds or sanction, or to apply Tinker to independent expression with its distinct requirements. Framing these paths supplies no conclusion about the factual inferences or final judgment. Settle does not impose universal curricular viewpoint neutrality; Boring addresses a teacher's personal claim over assigned institutional instruction, while Good News/Good Sports concerns actual private access. Santa Fe's official representative religious-choice mechanism does not itself classify every student election as official speech.

The summary-judgment damages controversy must remain separate from any expired office claim. Defendant identity, personal and official capacities, municipal attribution, conduct-date immunity and available relief are not interchangeable. April 1997 governs immunity notice; later Boring and Santa Fe may be merits law without supplying retroactive notice. A vacatur need not compel a trial, discovery, reinstatement, approval of a message or damages. A supported independent lawful disposition remains available on remand.

## Readiness and exact source limits

Ready for independent modeling on the supplied granted questions and source-supported alternatives. The packet does not certify recovery of the complete original Rule KJ-R, trial record, exact damages demand, a waiver, an immunity ruling below or hypothetical Supreme Court briefs. Any result relying on an unquoted policy clause, a particular damages amount, established waiver or a previously decided immunity defense requires that specific source. Their absence is not litigant default. No current factual premise is supplied merely to avoid an inconvenient record dispute.

Research cutoff for merits law is June 26, 2000; the conduct-date inquiry remains separately limited to April 1997. The exact Settle reusable-rule entry and Boring public dissent were requested and supplied before freeze. The lower court's historical Good News/Good Sports disposition is superseded by the actual simulated holding in the copied slice. No unresolved material omission prevents the bounded classification, actual-ground and sanction analysis.

## Validated objective source extraction

# Henerey — objective source extraction for fresh neutral validation

Read with the Henerey section of `runtime/OT_1999CHUNK9_NEUTRAL.md` and `entering-law/OT_1999CHUNK9_HENEREY.md`. This is a control preparation source extraction, not non-Stone commitments. A fresh context must validate the packet before modeling. The grant, May 2, 2000 submission/argument placement and June 26, 2000 decision placement are the approved simulation calendar, not recovered historical Supreme Court proceedings. Research cutoff for law: June 26, 2000; material conduct: March–April 1997. No actual Supreme Court merits outcome is supplied for this matter.

## Judgment, parties and posture

The Eighth Circuit's December 29, 1999 judgment, 200 F.3d 1128, No. 98-3439, affirmed Judge Charles A. Shaw's summary judgment for the defendants in the Eastern District of Missouri. Chief Judge Wollman wrote, joined by Richard S. Arnold; District Judge Charles R. Wolle, sitting by designation, dissented. Adam Henerey sued through his father under 42 U.S.C. §1983. Named defendants were the City of St. Charles School District, Principal Jerry Cook, and Assistant Principal David Watson, the individuals in both capacities. The appeal was submitted April 21, 1999. The summary-judgment standard requires favorable treatment of the nonmovant's supported evidence and reasonable inferences; the majority and dissent disagree over its application here.

The lower court did not decide that the condom distribution lacked any expressive content. It identified a material dispute over protected expression but held the rule constitutional and the sanction reasonably related to pedagogy. The approved grant addresses classification of election speech and the permissibility of genuinely viewpoint-neutral campaign enforcement; it does not supply a factual stipulation that enforcement was neutral.

## Facts and source status

Henerey was a sophomore seeking election as junior class president in March 1997. Candidacy was open to class members subject to meeting the council adviser and signing a contract to obey school rules. A council member told him flyers and posters needed advance approval. The school approved his campaign slogan. The campaign began April 7. After complaints that his posters covered competitors' posters and demeaned others, adviser Mary Stodden told him all materials required administrative approval. The opinion reports these complaints and the warning; it does not establish that the alleged posting misconduct was the actual ground of disqualification.

On April 10, election day, Henerey distributed condoms attached to campaign stickers in the school halls without seeking specific approval for condoms or indicating that the campaign would concern sex. The majority describes about eleven distributed; the dissent describes an offer of about twenty to requesting students. These accounts need not be converted into a single exact quantity. While Stodden was counting votes, a student complained; she informed Cook, who disqualified Henerey for failure to comply with Rule KJ-R. The subsequent count showed he had a majority. Thus do not write that Cook first learned he had won and then removed him in response to the result.

Other candidates distributed candy, gum or buttons without specific advance approval. The school said candy distribution was a longstanding practice with tacit approval. The majority found no record evidence disputing that practice and therefore no evidence of selective content enforcement. The dissent thought a jury could infer that the safe-sex message, rather than the approval process, caused the sanction. No trial resolved this disagreement. The only stated principal's ground identified by the dissent was failure to obtain approval.

## Rule KJ-R and educational function

The official published opinion quotes only relevant portions of the policy, with omissions. Those quoted portions are script-copied into `preparation/OT_1999CHUNK9_HENEREY_RULE_SOURCE.md`; that extract is not represented as the complete policy. The text requires a principal-approved distribution location; approval by the principal or assistant principal the previous day or earlier; more than one school day for material not readily classified or approved; and an approval stamp. It identifies unacceptable hate/hostility/violence material, pornography, obscenity, material unsuitable for school distribution, libel, commercial promotion, unapproved fundraising, and material the principal concludes would materially disrupt classwork, cause substantial disorder, or invade others' rights. The fundraising clause contains superintendent-or-assistant approval as its own exception. All material outside the unacceptable categories is acceptable. Do not flatten these distinct provisions into one general disruption exception or characterize the policy as guaranteeing a decision in exactly twenty-four hours: the lower court used that shorthand, while the quoted text expressly allows longer for difficult material.

The lower court classified the election as a school-sponsored curricular activity in a nonpublic forum. It relied on faculty supervision, a school-defined campaign period, the eligibility/contract requirements, prior approval, and instruction in leadership and democratic participation. It found no policy or practice opening campaign resources to indiscriminate public discourse. It reasoned that observers could attribute approved campaign material to the school. These are the lower court's legal classifications and record-based inferences, not a universal rule that every student campaign message is the school's own speech.

## Grounds below, including the alternative and dissent

The majority's principal ground was ordinary pedagogical enforcement of a known advance-approval requirement. It accepted tacit approval of conventional candy as a reason that different treatment did not establish content selection, rejected the facial prior-restraint and vagueness objections in this secondary-school setting, and found disqualification a reasonable sanction. Its generality analysis invoked the nature of indecency, vulgarity and disruption; it did not hold every unguided restriction on student speech valid.

The majority separately assumed protected expression and disagreement with its content, and still upheld disqualification. It reasoned that condom distribution could be read as encouraging teenage sexual activity and could carry apparent school approval, whereas candy was a conventional modest gratuity. It invoked maturity, decorum, curriculum, avoiding association with controversial sexual topics, and advance notice allowing accommodation of parents' objections. These are an independent appellate rationale and possible legal inferences, not findings that Henerey's actual message urged irresponsible sex, caused disruption, or produced a documented parental confrontation.

Wolle's dissent argued that a jury could find pretext, selective application, insufficient notice that condoms needed approval when other campaign objects did not, and disproportionate disqualification. Approval of the slogan and differential treatment could support the inference that the safe-sex message was the real cause. He rejected controversy over safe sex as enough by itself to suppress discussion in the campaign. He sought reversal of summary rejection so possible damages against one or more defendants could be adjudicated, not automatic election installation or a damages award.

## Strongest supported arguments and lawful paths

Henerey's record-supported position is that his individual campaign advocacy is protected; sponsorship of an election does not necessarily attribute every candidate's message to the institution. He challenges forum classification, prior restraint and discretion, the asserted neutrality of implementation, actual pedagogical grounds, notice, and sanction. Even if Hazelwood governs, genuine school purposes must explain the decision; controversy or a merely imaginable rationale cannot resolve disputed motive and enforcement at summary judgment under the current Settle holding. The school responds that a faculty-supervised election teaching leadership and democratic procedures is part of its program; approval rules protect orderly participation, maturity and attribution; candidates agreed and received warnings; and traditionally approved candy is a different comparator. It may defend the judgment on the principal process rationale without insisting that every controversial topic is prohibited.

Analytical paths include applying Hazelwood and sustaining a supported neutral sanction, applying Hazelwood but vacating for a material inference about actual grounds or permissible sanction, or treating the specific expression as independent student advocacy and applying Tinker. These are alternatives for independent adjudication. The Tinker branch requires its own supported disruption or rights-of-others inquiry and cannot presume one from offensiveness. The content-based appellate alternative must be tested separately under simulated Settle, which preserves genuine academic judgment while denying constitutional immunity to an impermissible categorical exclusion. Settle did not impose universal curricular viewpoint neutrality. Any protection against actual viewpoint discrimination must be framed at the lawful program's actual scope.

## Remedy, unresolved matters and chronology

The historical opinion establishes a money-damages claim and multiple defendants/capacities. No damages amount, municipal-policy determination, immunity holding, ongoing office entitlement, or current mootness finding is established. Graduation or completion of the school election alone does not erase a supported damages controversy. If summary judgment is vacated, the ordinary remand may address actual cause, any properly presented same-decision defense, defendant-specific liability and immunity, and lawful relief. It need not mandate trial, discovery, restoration to an expired office, an award, or censorship/approval of a particular message. Conduct-date immunity must be evaluated using law available in April 1997, separately from June 2000 merits law.

The current-term Santa Fe decision is effective June 19, 2000 and is copied in the law slice. It addresses a board-created official religious-choice mechanism and preserves independent student speech; it does not resolve Henerey's message attribution, forum classification, sanction or disputed grounds. Boring concerns a teacher's claimed personal entitlement over assigned instruction, not a rule stripping students of speech rights. Settle remains the directly relevant genuine-pedagogy and causation rule. The lower Henerey opinion cites the historical Good News/Good Sports ruling; the simulated December 11, 1995 decision supersedes that historical disposition and must be used at its actual private-access scope.

## Own date-eligible Justice source map

All eight Associates joined Souter's simulated Settle Parts I–II, including genuine pedagogical control, rejection of an impermissible categorical religious exclusion, the supported-inference limit on summary judgment, and the distinct Mt. Healthy burdens. Each therefore has an adopted position on the important fork without projecting a current vote. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas joined the Boring majority's limited institutional-instruction rule. Souter's Boring dissent, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer, accepted final school control but demanded actual legitimate pedagogy and preserved cause and defendant-specific defenses; it does not establish that teachers and students have identical interests. Stevens, O'Connor and Scalia joined the pre-divergence Hazelwood majority; the remaining participating Associates were not on that Court. These sources support separate analysis of program classification, actual reason and sanction. The supplied source map contains no prediction or assignment.

## Exact published policy excerpt

Script-copied from the official Eighth Circuit opinion, pp. 7-8. The opinion itself omits portions; this is not the complete original rule. PDF page markers retained.

ADVERTISING IN THE SCHOOLS  (Board Policy KJ-R)

The distribution of such items may take place in a location approved
by principal of the school.
. . . .

The approval must be obtained the previous day or earlier from the

work or involve substantial disorder or invasion of the rights of

All materials not proscribed in “Unacceptable items”.

## Exact focused entering-law handoff
