Source: `state/holdings/first-amendment.md`; exact heading: Settle v. Dickson County School Board

#### Settle v. Dickson County School Board

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit No. 93-6207; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** June 25, 1996.

##### What genuine curricular grounds may limit a religious research topic

**Holding:** A teacher may enforce genuine topic-approval, research, source and academic-method requirements reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns, and a student's religious convictions create no exemption from them. But curricular discretion does not make categorical exclusion of an otherwise permissible religious perspective, or a mistaken blanket legal prohibition on academic religious subjects, a legitimate ground merely because it is described as educational judgment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part I, unanimous.

**Material application:** The teacher-only submission is not presumed publicly attributable to the school or an open forum. Objective inquiry may be distinguished from devotion or personal testimony. Genuine factual or evaluative mistakes can remain pedagogical judgments, while a legally impermissible categorical religious ban does not become valid as an honest academic mistake. The full policy permits objective, respectful, balanced academic religious treatment without promoting tenets or demeaning belief and accommodates student-initiated belief or nonbelief; neither provision guarantees approval.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal curricular viewpoint neutrality, unrestricted topic choice, religion-based exemption, perfect-accuracy duty or independent Free Exercise, Establishment or equal-protection holding.

##### Whether the summary-judgment record establishes lawful cause or the same-decision defense

**Holding:** Summary judgment cannot rest on a merely possible pedagogical justification when supported evidence permits a material inference that a student's work was rejected for an impermissible religious reason rather than legitimate application of assignment requirements. Under Mt. Healthy, the student must establish protected conduct and its substantial or motivating role; defendants may then prove by a preponderance that the same adverse decision would have occurred without that conduct.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part II, unanimous.

**Material application:** Failure to secure permission was supported but its independent causal effect was disputed by the next-day no-penalty alternative, mixed reasons and original-choice answer, which also covered Buddha and Muhammed. Faith alone does not prove inability to research. Familiarity does not prove absence of new inquiry. Source requirements must reflect permitted secondary and outside sources, not a new Bible-only or school-library-only premise. Comparator descriptions are relevant without proving identical circumstances.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No pretext, hostility, final causation, liability or automatic defeat of a proved same-decision defense is found. Waters supplies its shared genuine-ground distinction, not an imported employment investigation code. Graduation proves no universal mootness; actual capacities, municipal attribution, immunity and remedies remain open. Conduct-date immunity notice concerns March–April 1991, not later decisions.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacated and remanded, 9–0, for ordinary proceedings on the free-speech claim. No compulsory approval, reading, grading, credit, removal of the zero, damages, new discovery procedure or trial; another supported lawful disposition remains possible.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier, 484 U.S. 260:** Applies legitimate pedagogical control to this graded research assignment, preserving genuine method and quality decisions and reasonable academic error. Its public-attribution rationale is not automatically transferred to a teacher-only submission.
- **Tinker v. Des Moines Independent Community School District, 393 U.S. 503:** Preserves student expression without creating unrestricted topic choice or displacing genuine assignment requirements.
- **Epperson v. Arkansas, 393 U.S. 97:** Applies the distinction between curricular authority and constitutional immunity, and between objective academic study of religion and governmental religious instruction. No new religion-clause claim is adjudicated.
- **Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District, June 7, 1993; Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, June 29, 1995; and Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, December 11, 1995:** Preserve the distinction between genuine program boundaries and religious-viewpoint exclusion in their actual settings. They do not classify this assignment as a forum or establish a right to a particular grade.
- **Lee v. Weisman, June 24, 1992, and Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, February 12, 1996:** Preserve their limits on official worship and coercive religious participation without creating a blanket prohibition on academic religious subjects.
- **Waters v. Churchill, May 31, 1994:** Applies the genuine-reliance and causation distinction at its shared scope. Its separate investigation approaches and public-employment balance are not adopted as a classroom test.
- **Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle, 429 U.S. 274:** Applies the claimant's protected-conduct and substantial-or-motivating-factor burden and defendants' separate preponderance same-decision defense. A possible lawful reason is not sufficient proof, and partial improper motivation does not automatically establish entitlement to relief.

No precedent is overruled.

Source: `state/holdings/first-amendment.md`; exact heading: Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education

#### Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education

**Docket or dockets:** No. 97-1835.
**Decided:** 1999-02-17.

##### Assigned instructional production and the asserted personal speech right

**Holding:** A public-school teacher's selection, production, and direction of material as part of the assigned school instructional program constitute institutional curricular expression subject to the school system's lawful control; the artistic discretion entrusted to the teacher does not, on this claim, create a personal First Amendment right against curricular and related employment control. This rule concerns the assigned school production, and does not authorize punishment of separate citizen speech or an independently unconstitutional classification or exclusion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's complete opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, supplies five direct rationale votes.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier: applies the distinction between personal student expression and school-sponsored educational activity; its student-speech rule is not converted into an instructor's personal control over institutional content.
- Settle v. Dickson County School Board, June 25, 1996: preserves genuine curricular authority and the prohibition on categorical religious exclusion masquerading as pedagogy; distinguishes the student's limited interest within an assignment from the teacher's asserted interest in administering the program.
- Jeffries v. Harleston, November 17, 1997: distinguishes the unaffiliated citizen address expressly outside assigned official work; its protection, actual-cause rule, and limited mandate remain intact.
- Brewster v. Board of Education, August 17, 1998: distinguishes the particular internal attendance-error report and conduct-date immunity question; no general rule withdrawing protection from workplace reports follows.
- Waters v. Churchill, May 31, 1994: preserves its limited majority propositions on genuine lawful grounds and mistaken accounts of protected employee speech; supplies no personal curricular entitlement or universal investigation procedure.
- Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle: preserves the claimant's protected-conduct and substantial-or-motivating-factor showing and the employer's separate preponderance same-decision defense; no defense is found proved here.
- Pickering v. Board of Education, Connick v. Myers, and Givhan v. Western Line Consolidated School District: retain contextual public-employee speech protection, including private speech; the Court adopts no categorical workplace or private-concern exclusion.
- Epperson v. Arkansas and Keyishian v. Board of Regents: preserve independently applicable constitutional limits on educational authority; the curricular label does not establish immunity from them.

No precedent is overruled.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The teacher's delegated selection and direction of this school instructional production do not supply the personal speech entitlement claimed against curricular and related employment control. That limited institutional-content rule leaves student expression, separate citizen speech, independent constitutional prohibitions, and other sources of employment protection in place. The decision adopts no universal rule for government employees' assigned speech and no constitutional safe harbor based on any conceivable pedagogical reason.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Affirmed, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.
**Opposing:** Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

The dismissal of the granted First Amendment claim remains in force. The Court orders no further remedy and does not reopen claims outside the grant.

Source: `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`; exact heading: Assigned school productions and teachers’ personal speech

### Assigned school productions and teachers’ personal speech

**Governing question:** Whether a teacher has a personal First Amendment right over an assigned school instructional production.

**Current rule:** Selection, production and direction of material as part of an assigned public-school instructional program are institutional curricular expression subject to the school system’s lawful control. Delegated artistic discretion, public controversy and prior approval do not themselves give the teacher a personal constitutional right against curricular and related employment control or make approval an irrevocable constitutional delegation. A protected personal interest must be established before justification and same-decision rules apply.

**Authority by component:**

- Confined instructional-expression classification and antecedent personal-interest requirement: Canonical Decision Record `boring_v_buncombe_county_board_of_education_merits_1999-02-17.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / Assigned instructional production and the asserted personal speech right, 1999-02-17; five direct joins.

**Present operation effective:** 1999-02-17.

**Exceptions and limits:** Separate citizen speech, independently forbidden classifications or exclusions, and statutory, contractual, tenure and state-law limits remain. This is not a rule for every duty-related statement or university academic freedom, and a curricular label does not establish an actually lawful motive.

**Holdings navigation:** [Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education — Assigned instructional production and the asserted personal speech right](HOLDINGS.md#assigned-instructional-production-and-the-asserted-personal-speech-right-1).


## Current-term addition

Source: `terms/OT1999/workspace/neutral-projection.md`; Santa Fe controlling-law block only.

### Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe — 2000-06-19

[Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe](../records/santa-fe-independent-school-district-v-doe_merits_2000-06-19.md)

#### The school may not delegate its official religious choice to a student majority

**Holding and operative rule:** A public secondary school may not organize an invocation as a representative component of its own football pregame ceremony and delegate to a student-majority election whether that official component will include religious exercise. The challenged board-created election mechanism violates the Establishment Clause under both its unrestricted and nonsectarian/nonproselytizing versions; relief reaches that mechanism while preserving genuinely voluntary private prayer, independent student expression, lawful secular messages and genuine neutral access.

**Authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, supplying seven direct votes for the rule, explanation and limited affirmance. The particular government-created representative religious choice is essential to all seven joins; the Court adopts no independent religious-pressure holding.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini requires identification of the governmental act and its function, using purpose, effect and institutional relationships as evidence within the modified Lemon inquiry. Here the board creates an invocation or message slot within its own ceremony; the student council conducts a majority election under the principal's advice and direction; the electorate selects a single volunteer; and the speaker must serve the board's prescribed purposes. This is an official representative component, not generally available access for independently chosen private speakers. Lee forbids official school organization of worship, and Ingebretsen explains why student initiation or delivery cannot alone make that exercise private. A majority vote cannot privatize the board's delegated religious choice.

The District stresses secular purposes, student authorship, no required prayer, optional attendance and the possibility of silence or a secular message. Those features do not remove the existing governmental mechanism. The Court assumes no official textual preapproval and no delivered October prayer; it does not rest on a finding that every secular purpose is a sham. The ability to decline use of the delegated religious authority does not transform it into neutral access. Pinette and Bronx Household protect actually private expression, where government has not adopted the devotion as its own event component. Tanford likewise distinguishes official organization from private composition, without making its adult setting identical to this school policy.

The backup's religious-content limits retain the same official mechanism and do not cure it. The Court does not direct officials to edit private religion into acceptable language. Nor does it prohibit every election, invited student speaker or religious message. The existing mechanism suffices to affirm; Lee and Ingebretsen's separate protection against substantial practical religious pressure remains intact without requiring legal compulsion or compulsory attendance. No additional pressure finding, universal facial-challenge rule, new forum entitlement, graduation ruling or damages award is necessary.

- **Agostini v. Felton:** Applied as the governing governmental-act/function inquiry; purpose, effects and entanglement remain evidence within that inquiry, not independent surviving prongs.
- **Lemon v. Kurtzman:** Continues in the form Agostini adopted; the Court revives neither an independent purpose test nor an independent endorsement test.
- **Lee v. Weisman:** Its official-organization rule applies to this school-created invocation mechanism. Its independently sufficient substantial-pressure rule remains, without a compulsory-attendance prerequisite; no new pressure holding is made here.
- **Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District:** Its rejection of automatic insulation by student delivery and its protection of lawful private expression apply. Its provision-sensitive interim ruling is not recast as a universal rule invalidating every future religious message.
- **Tanford v. Brand:** Preserved for its established official devotional program and its distinction between independent wording and private access; the Court does not transplant every feature of university commencement to football.
- **Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette:** Its actual generally available private forum is distinguished from this selective representative event component; religious content and government location remain insufficient by themselves to establish attribution.
- **Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10:** Its independently organized private use remains protected; the school's own representative ceremony presents a different governmental function.
- **Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District:** Its protection of religious viewpoints within genuine comparable private access remains intact; no schoolwide private forum is inferred here.
- **Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia:** Its independently directed private-expression relationship remains intact; it does not authorize government-organized worship.
- **Chabad of Southern Ohio v. City of Cincinnati:** Its contextual attribution inquiry remains; neither a forum label nor location alone supplies the answer.
- **Strout v. Albanese:** Its distinction between genuine private expression and governmental performance or purchase of a religious function is preserved; no football-specific rule is imported from its tuition setting.

The official character of the challenged representative religious-choice mechanism supplies the constitutional defect even without a delivered October prayer or official editing of its words. Government cannot make its own religious choice private by delegating it to a student majority. Agostini's unified governmental-act/function inquiry governs; no independent purpose or endorsement test returns. Genuinely private religious expression retains its protection. The independent substantial-pressure rules of Lee and Ingebretsen continue without requiring legally compulsory attendance; this decision need not decide that additional ground.

Source: `state/holdings/first-amendment.md`; Good News/Good Sports private-access holding.

#### Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue

**Docket or dockets:** Eighth Circuit No. 93-2148; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied; judgment below, 28 F.3d 1501.
**Decided:** December 11, 1995.

##### Religious instruction on an admitted moral-development subject

**Holding:** A school opening an after-school period to comparable private moral- and character-development instruction and activities may not exclude an otherwise eligible club because its approach is religious. The rule applies to this actual forum even if nonpublic; genuine subject, activity, scheduling, supervision, safety and access limits remain permissible when applied without viewpoint discrimination.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight direct votes.

**Material application:** Both the Scouts and Club concern moral development. Prayer and Bible instruction do not transform the religious viewpoint into a disqualifying subject; express exclusion even of Scout religious speech confirms the viewpoint line. Access after 6 p.m. does not cure discrimination in the comparable 3–6 p.m. period.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Schools need not open every facility to every religious activity or retain this forum permanently. Neutral prospective forum changes remain available; standalone worship in a different forum and the facial validity of every religious-use rule are not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lamb's Chapel supplies the subject/viewpoint distinction; Perry and Cornelius permit genuine, reasonable and viewpoint-neutral management without reclassifying an admitted religious perspective out of the forum.

##### Establishment consequences of these actual private-use arrangements

**Holding:** Equal access on these forum terms does not establish religion: the forum serves a secular purpose, the expression remains private in context, and access creates neither governmental advancement of religion nor excessive entanglement. Private sponsorship must be real; selection, control, adoption, preference or obscured attribution could alter the conclusion, and neutral availability or a disclaimer alone cannot cure official religious activity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer; five direct votes for the contextual explanation. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join Part I's concrete rejection of the establishment defense but not Part II's complete rationale.

**Material application:** Parent leadership, voluntary attendance with parental permission and notices of private sponsorship establish actual separation. The 11–15-year-old audience, ongoing extracurricular day and available late bus do not on this record convert the private program into school speech; earlier involvement of a former board member establishes no current control.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new constitutional checklist, fixed disclaimer, universal monitoring duty, official prayer, compelled participation or public-funding entitlement is created. Independent religion-clause claims are unnecessary. No Equal Access Act holding is made.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Eighth Circuit's reversal and remedial remand, 8–1; lower courts determine nondiscriminatory declaratory or injunctive relief while lawful neutral forum management remains. No damages, deadline or new hearing is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lemon supplies purpose, effect and entanglement; Lamb's Chapel applies equal access to private school-forum expression. Chabad demands actual attribution inquiry without a universal sign rule. Pinette supplies no comprehensive forum exemption; Rosenberger's printing benefit establishes no devotional-funding entitlement. Mergens distinguishes private expression from school sponsorship within its own statutory setting.

**Current-force modification:** Effective June 23, 1997, Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test as modified in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform a single inquiry with three routes: controlling specific prohibition or distinct doctrine, established permissible relationship, and residual attribution and function. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553, June 23, 1997, Holdings I–II; First Amendment — Public instruction on religious-school premises.


Source: `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`; exact reusable rule.

### Genuine pedagogy and religious perspectives in graded assignments

**Governing question:** A student’s challenge to rejection of a religious research topic within a teacher-assessed curricular assignment.

**Current rule:** Teachers may enforce genuine topic-approval, research, source and academic-method requirements reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns; religious conviction creates no exemption. But categorical exclusion of an otherwise permissible religious perspective, or a mistaken blanket legal ban on academic religious subjects, is not legitimate pedagogy merely because so labeled. Schools retain authority to select knowledge, method or perspective taught and to distinguish objective inquiry from testimony or devotional advocacy; no universal curricular viewpoint-neutrality rule follows. Summary judgment cannot rest on a merely possible academic justification where supported evidence permits a material inference of impermissible religious causation. The student must establish protected conduct and a substantial or motivating role; defendants may prove by a preponderance that the same decision would have occurred absent it.

**Authority by component:**

- Legitimate-pedagogy boundary and actual causal proof: I. Curricular authority must rest on genuine, legitimate pedagogy and II. The affirmative record leaves a material causal dispute, 1996-06-25; Souter’s unanimous opinion.

**Present operation effective:** Settle, 1996-06-25.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary genuine factual or evaluative errors may remain within pedagogical discretion; the rule creates no classroom forum, automatic topic approval, compelled grade, independent investigative duty or conclusive liability from an impermissible factor where the same-decision defense is proved.

**Holdings navigation:** Settle v. Dickson County School Board.

Source: `terms/OT1998/output/OT_1998CHUNK4.md`; Boring public separate writing, copied.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer, would vacate the dismissal and remand. The dissent accepts the school's final authority over instruction but recognizes a limited personal expressive interest against adverse action unrelated to genuine legitimate pedagogy. A teacher need not have a right to insist on a chosen play to object that officials punished an allegedly approved expressive contribution for an impermissible nonpedagogical reason. The dissent would extend Hazelwood's legitimate-pedagogical-relation approach to this claim, informed by Settle's distinction between real educational authority and constitutional immunity. It does not assert that either precedent already decided a teacher's entitlement.

Officials could alter content and take related personnel action reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns. They would not have to satisfy strict scrutiny, prove actual disruption in every case, open a forum, or guarantee Boring's preferred production. But approval, compliance with the requested edits, and punishment for unpopular expression are pleaded; insubordination or a merely conceivable legitimate reason cannot replace an actual supported ground. Waters and Jeffries preserve that genuine-ground and causal distinction. Brewster reinforces attention to the actual employee claim and individualized defenses without treating every internal disagreement as protected.

The dissent would permit ordinary adjudication of protection, actual pedagogical grounds, and cause. Boring would have to show protected conduct was a substantial or motivating factor; defendants could prove by a preponderance that they would have made the same decision without it. Liability, municipal attribution, and individual immunity would remain distinct; immunity notice would be judged as of the 1991–1992 conduct. The proposed remand orders no play, reinstatement, damages, discovery, or trial and permits another supported lawful disposition. These four Justices reject the majority's absence-of-personal-interest conclusion while preserving broad final school control.

# Frozen neutral packet — Mobil Oil / Marathon v. United States

Fresh neutral validation completed November 3, 2026. No modeled vote, assignment, Stone position or target historical Supreme Court disposition is supplied. Exact saved facts, operative terms, entering law and statutory copies follow.

## Event and independent completeness assessment

Nos. 99-244 and 99-253; argument March 22, 2000; merits consideration June 26, 2000. The judgment under review is the Federal Circuit's revised May 13, 1999 reversal, not its superseded 1998 opinion or a liability affirmance. Both petitioners' remaining North Carolina contract claims must be accounted for; earlier settled parties, a takings claim and separate CZMA administrative litigation are not adjudicated here. The nine seated Justices are Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; no case-specific nonparticipation is supplied. Independent modeling concerns the eight Associates.

Use effective pre-June-26 law; unrelated June 26 cases do not govern this event retroactively or as coordinated precedents. Winstar's actual assumed-risk holding, not its noncontrolling broader theories, supplies the contractual baseline. An authorized promise must be identified before breach, materiality, repudiation, election, restitution and amount are examined separately. The case cannot begin with Section I's conclusory assertion that a material replacement of the bargain is already an established breach.

Lease section 1's named statutes, existing regulations, specified future waste/conservation/correlative-right regulations and other applicable laws remain distinct. Sections 9–10, 13 and 19 qualify operation, suspension, cancellation and reserved rights without automatically resolving the contested risk allocation. The promised process is not a guarantee of a permit, state concurrence, a successful well or profits. Interior's favorable review does not eliminate North Carolina's independent objections, while those objections do not themselves decide whether depriving the separately purchased process is material. The fifth lease has no submitted exploration plan; its theory is anticipatory repudiation rather than a fabricated application.

The lower majority's independent-permit and no-operative-causation rationale, Newman's process bargain analysis, the Government's incorporated-suspension account, and its independent waiver/election defense are all preserved. Seeking performance or extensions can bear on election, but the legal consequence and record preservation remain contested. No litigant concession, waiver or recovery amount is supplied by inference.

The exact OBPA text is added to cure an abbreviated qualification in the initial handoff. It prohibits six listed activities in the defined North Carolina area until the later of October 1, 1991 or forty-five days of continuous congressional session after the specified certification/explanation report. Continuity breaks only at adjournment ending the session; days when either House is absent because of an adjournment longer than three days to a day certain are excluded. The scientific panel advises and supplies findings; the Secretary may disagree with detailed explanation and justification. A fixed calendar-day interval or absolute panel veto would misstate the law. The panel's own reporting/termination provisions and the Secretary's study/consultation duties remain as quoted. Congressional findings mention a July 14, 1989 memorandum; the supplied MOU recitation dates the actual agreement July 12. No two-day timing issue is decided from that discrepancy.

The exact OCSLA section 1334(a) text preserves the lessee-request condition for national-interest/proper-development/transportation suspension and the separate serious-harm suspension ground. Equivalent term extension has a gross-negligence/willful-violation exception; no such conduct is found. Cancellation requires the Secretary's post-hearing findings of probable serious harm, persistence beyond a reasonable time and comparative advantages, plus five continuous suspension years or a shorter period requested by the lessee. The distinct compensation formula, pre-September-18-1978 exception and joint-lessee due-diligence provisions do not establish any satisfied cancellation predicate here. The statutory compensation route and common-law contract restitution are separate.

The actual June 2, 1995 suspension letter requires rent and caps the new suspension at five years. It qualifies the Government brief's broader rent-free description. Neither continued suspension through June 2000 nor a later renewal is established; earlier rent-waived periods and the independent value of term extension remain available at their proved scope. No current lease expiration, waiver or offset is inferred simply from the absence of another research source.

Lawful paths include sustaining a supported no-undertaking/no-breach/no-material-breach or independent-election ground, or finding contractual liability and a lawful restitution entitlement with genuinely unresolved amount issues remanded. The precise legal ground determines the remedy. Bonus consideration, annual rentals, cognizable benefits/returned rights, profits and interest are different items. The exact award is not established by rounded $156/$158 million references; interest requires its own legal authority. No drilling order, CZMA override, legislative injunction or automatic evidentiary hearing follows.

## Readiness and exact source limits

Ready for independent modeling of the consolidated contract questions. The packet uses supported recitations rather than certifying complete line-by-line reading of the joint appendix. Exact judgment amounts, benefits evidence, later suspension/renewal/payment history and disputed preservation must be verified if they determine a chosen ground. A bounded entitlement/remand disposition may preserve those matters without pretending they are established. The supplied statutory cancellation predicates remain unproved; missing material cannot be turned into default. The research cutoff is June 26, 2000, with conduct and statutory-change dates maintained separately. No additional reconstruction premise is adopted.

## Validated source facts

# Mobil Oil / Marathon — objective source extraction for fresh neutral validation

Read with the Mobil/Marathon section of `runtime/OT_1999CHUNK9_NEUTRAL.md`, `entering-law/OT_1999CHUNK9_MOBIL.md`, and `preparation/OT_1999CHUNK9_MOBIL_OPERATIVE_SOURCE.md`. This control source extraction contains no current-case Supreme Court disposition or vote. Fresh neutral validation is required before modeling. Nos. 99-244 and 99-253; argument March 22, 2000; decision placement June 26, 2000. Research cutoff for decisional law and procedural facts: June 26, 2000; modern retrieval November 3, 2026.

## Review posture and decisions below

Certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) reviews the Federal Circuit's revised May 13, 1999 opinion, 177 F.3d 1331, No. 97-5146. It supersedes the October 15, 1998 opinion at 158 F.3d 1253. Plager wrote for himself and Rader; Newman dissented. The revised judgment reversed the Court of Federal Claims' liability judgment at 35 Fed. Cl. 309 (1996) and subsequent July 24, 1997 restitution judgment. The actual ruling under review is not an affirmance of contractual liability with only arithmetic left.

On cross-motions for summary judgment, with neither side identifying a material factual dispute for that decision, the trial court found an undertaking to consider properly submitted exploration plans timely and fairly, a material breach/repudiation through the Outer Banks Protection Act (OBPA), and no sovereign-acts defense. After a benefits hearing it awarded return of the bonus payments, exceeding $156 million for both plaintiffs, but denied annual rental recovery. It found no legally cognizable offset established by the Government. The Federal Circuit did not reach the Government's challenges to calculation of restitution because it found no breach warranting relief. Reported rounded figures of $156 million and $158 million should not be converted into an exact final award; the precise judgment and any proper offsets govern.

The Court of Federal Claims litigation originally involved other lessees and areas; settlements left these plaintiffs' North Carolina OBPA claims. An originally pleaded takings theory is not before the Court. A separate District of Columbia action reviewing Commerce's CZMA override decision is not this contract appeal. Do not decide that administrative challenge or require Commerce to override North Carolina as contract relief.

## Lease and statutory obligations

Mobil and Marathon each acquired approximately one-third interests in five North Carolina leases and paid more than $78 million in bonuses, with annual rentals separately due. Four leases were part of the Manteo Unit; the fifth had no submitted exploration plan. Each lease had a ten-year primary term, continued production/drilling provisions, and conditional exclusive exploration/development rights. It was not a guarantee of discovery, profitable production, unconditional plan approval, or every other permit.

Section 1 incorporates the Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act (OCSLA), §§302–303 of the Department of Energy Organization Act, regulations issued under those statutes existing when the lease took effect, future regulations under those statutes for prevention of waste, conservation of OCS natural resources and protection of correlative rights, and other applicable statutes and regulations. Preserve the named sources and future-regulation qualifications; do not paraphrase this as immunity from all future regulation. Sections 9–10 require operation under approved plans and compliance with exploration/development/production regulations and orders. Section 13(a) permits suspension or cancellation pursuant to OCSLA §5 and compensation where the Act provides it. Section 13(b) separately addresses wartime/national-emergency suspension on the Defense Secretary's recommendation with just compensation. Section 19 reserves ungranted rights, with particular noninterference and national-defense qualifications. Those reservations do not themselves answer whether the specified contracted approval process could be replaced without contract consequences.

Under 43 U.S.C. §1340(c)(1), an exploration plan must comply with OCSLA, its regulations and lease provisions; following necessary modifications, approval is due within thirty days of submission unless the statutory serious-harm condition, not avoidable by modification, requires disapproval. The harm categories include life (including aquatic life), property, minerals, national security/defense, and marine, coastal or human environment. Separate provisions authorize suspension/cancellation on their own predicates. Section 1334(a)(1)(A)'s national-interest/proper-development/transportation suspension is at the lessee's request; §1334(a)(1)(B) addresses threatened serious, irreparable or immediate harm. Do not remove the lessee-request condition from (A) or make every general environmental concern a finding satisfying (B). The Government also relies on the environmental-analysis/NEPA regulation, former 30 C.F.R. §250.10(b)(4), later recodified §250.110(b)(4), and the inordinate-permitting-delay provision (b)(6). Whether these incorporated powers authorize the actual disputed postponement is contested.

Approval of a plan is distinct from permission to carry it out. Under OCSLA §1340(c)(2) and CZMA §307(c)(3), operations requiring federal licenses/permits and affecting the coastal zone need state consistency concurrence, conclusive presumed concurrence under the actual statutory conditions, or a Commerce override based on consistency with CZMA objectives or national security. No presumed concurrence is established here. Discharges separately require an EPA NPDES permit and the applicable CZMA process; a drilling permit and later development/production approval also remain necessary. The plan-approval claim cannot silently become an entitlement to those permits. The statutory cancellation-and-compensation route is conditional; the Federal Circuit found it inapplicable and said the lessees did not claim its predicates were met, while Newman's dissent invoked it by analogy/support. No actual statutory cancellation finding is supplied.

## Chronology and competing governmental determinations

In July 1989 Mobil, North Carolina and Interior entered an MOU for additional environmental review of a draft exploration plan and lease suspensions. The MOU promised additional suspensions upon application for specified state objections, administrative/judicial challenges, appeals or regulatory delays, with two drilling seasons after their resolution. The draft plan was submitted in 1989; Interior's extensive report was completed in June 1990. Interior found the proposed well had no significant environmental impact under its review and deemed the plan approvable under its criteria. Those determinations do not erase North Carolina's distinct data and coastal-consistency objections.

North Carolina formally objected to the NPDES consistency certification in July 1990. Some sources date the letter July 16 and the lower opinion says July 17; no decisive one-day timing question is presented. On August 18, 1990 Congress enacted OBPA. Mobil submitted its final plan August 20. North Carolina objected to the plan's CZMA certification November 19. Both state objections remained unresolved during the material period, and Commerce declined override on September 2, 1994 because the requisite findings were unsupported. Whether OBPA materially deprived the companies of the separately purchased process, despite those barriers, is the contract question, not a settled causal fact.

OBPA §6003(c) prohibited new lease sales/issuance, exploration-plan and development/production-plan approval, drilling-permit approval and drilling on the defined North Carolina OCS. It lasted until the later of October 1, 1991, or forty-five days of continuous congressional session after an Interior report, following consideration of the scientific panel, certifying sufficient information for OCSLA responsibilities and explaining/justifying differences from the panel. Continuous session has its own adjournment exclusions; this was not simply forty-five calendar days. The panel recommended studies and information; it was not granted final permit authority. The Act required further environmental studies and consultation, but allowed the Secretary to disagree with panel recommendations through the stated explanation process.

The panel reported in January 1992. Interior's April 1992 report certified information sufficient for the first proposed exploratory well while withholding approval pending two additional studies. The lower majority treated the statutory bar for that plan as lifted after the required congressional period and later work as the Secretary's own initiative. Petitioners dispute that later work was independent of OBPA and emphasize future stages still needing statutory certification. The Government stresses that no other plans were before Interior and denies an indefinite ban on every future proposal. Studies were transmitted in July 1994. A general suspension was terminated in November 1994 after the studies and Commerce decision. OBPA was repealed in 1996. Repeal is a subsequent event, not proof that any prior breach was or was not material or retracted in time.

## Lower judgment and strongest litigation positions

The Federal Circuit agreed that the catchall lease language did not generally incorporate a future enactment such as OBPA; it declined an interpretation making promised rights illusory. It nonetheless held that North Carolina's independent, unresolved objections prevented exploration, that the lessees assumed the original permit risks, and that OBPA was not the operative cause of their inability to drill. It concluded no breach or basis for restitution had been established. It rejected a general OCSLA right to compensation whenever exploration proves unavailable. That is the complete lower path: its agreement on the future-statute clause is not a concession of material breach.

Newman dissented because the purchased timely-and-fair consideration entitlement had been negated, and ordinary contract/restitution principles did not permit the Government to retain the consideration after preventing the promised performance. She distinguished exploratory failure after performance from withholding the contracted process, and did not assert that every proposed plan must be approved. Her opinions' original and revised versions must not be conflated; the revised May 1999 dissent is the operative one.

Petitioners argue that the timely original approval process was a material part of what the bonuses bought; OBPA imposed new prerequisites across all six regulated activities, initially barring performance for at least thirteen months and practically prolonging it. They seek restoration of payments for a repudiated bargain, rather than expectation profits requiring proof that oil would have been found or permits ultimately obtained. Independent state objections, the possibility of a losing bargain and post-suit repeal therefore do not automatically defeat restitution. On the fifth tract, they invoke anticipatory repudiation of the same approval undertaking rather than a fiction that an application was submitted.

The Government argues that the leases already permitted environmental study and suspension, that Congress merely required the contracting agency to use existing powers, and that the priority to seek eventual development—not an inflexible timetable for one administrative step—was the essential bargain. It separately argues no material deprivation because the CZMA/NPDES barriers would have blocked operations regardless of OBPA. It distinguishes breach/materiality from causation of expectation damages and argues that restitution cannot follow every technical delay. Its reliance on broad national-interest suspension must be tested with the lessee-request qualification above. The source packet does not convert either party's construction into an operative rule.

## Waiver, continued performance and remedy defenses

The Government's independent waiver/election defense relies on submission of the final plan after enactment, administrative and judicial efforts to overcome the CZMA objections, and requests for continued suspensions and extensions. It says these demanded and accepted performance, induced continued governmental expense and affirmed the contracts, leaving at most partial-breach damages. Petitioners distinguish urging performance/retraction from acceptance of materially useful performance, invoke the preexisting MOU obligation, deny that an unapproved plan or unsuccessful override delivered the promised approval process, and identify their 1992 suit as an election once the scope of the breach was clear. Mobil also disputes preservation of some newly framed waiver grounds and reliance on correspondence outside the trial record. These are arguments requiring resolution at the available record's actual scope, not an established forfeiture or a presumed intentional waiver.

The suspension benefits require particular care. The Government's January 2000 brief broadly characterizes the extensions as continuously rent-free. Its own appended June 2, 1995 MMS letter, however, approves new suspensions effective November 10, 1994 pending final nonappealable judgment, caps them at five years under §250.10(e), and expressly requires rentals under §218.154(b)(2). Those express terms qualify the narrative characterization. Do not assume that the 1995 letter itself established continuing suspension or rental relief through June 2000. A distinct renewal or actual payment history would be needed if that proposition determines waiver or an offset. Earlier directed suspensions did waive rentals, and extensions of term may have value independently of rent. The distinction can be reserved if the legal waiver ground fails even treating the documented requests/extensions as given.

If breach and restitution entitlement are found, the Government expressly requests remand for its unaddressed challenges to the amount. Restoration must account for actual returned rights and legally cognizable benefits; the trial court's bonus-only award and rental denial are distinct. No profits, interest, automatic return of all rentals, guaranteed permit, legislative injunction or order nullifying North Carolina's objection follows from the process claim. Interest against the United States requires separate legal authority. A narrow liability or entitlement decision can leave only genuinely unresolved monetary issues; it need not order a new evidentiary hearing if the existing record suffices. Affirmance can rest on a valid no-breach, no-material-breach or independently supported waiver ground, with any remand matched to the ground actually adopted. No such path is selected here.

## Law entering and each Associate's own sources

The focused law slice copies actual simulated Winstar and the reusable rule, effective July 1, 1996. An authorized undertaking and its exact risk allocation are essential; general regulation or fairness supplies no contract. Congress retains regulatory power, while an actual assumed risk may carry monetary responsibility. Causation, loss, terms, duration and remedies remain separate. The majority does not establish a universal unmistakability exemption for money claims or a comprehensive substantial-contractual-effect exception to sovereign acts.

Souter, Stevens, O'Connor and Breyer adopted Winstar's limited unmistakability threshold account; Souter, Stevens and Breyer also adopted its separate substantial-effect account, which is noncontrolling. Scalia's own concurrence, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, applies unmistakability and reads the whole contract to find the particular undertaking; it recognizes that immediate regulatory permission could be a different bargain. Ginsburg's dissent would require contract-by-contract reconsideration of continuing change-of-law risk under unmistakability rather than treating valuable immediate permission as necessarily a longer promise. Seven Associates nevertheless joined the common assumed-risk holding; Ginsburg accepted actual-authorization requirements but disputed the identified continuing promise. These exact prior public positions are appended to the slice and support individual examination without supplying current votes.

## Sources and remaining limits

- [IA Mobil item](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0329) and [IA Marathon item](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0330), located by the collection's own docket/name listing. Saved petition, JA and merits OCR provide the lower judgments and record references. Some nominal individual IA files concatenate additional filings; document cover/title/date control attribution, not the filename alone. The revised Federal Circuit majority and Newman dissent were read in full from the petition. The lease and OBPA source sections were read; relevant petitioner merits and reply arguments were read. The full JA was saved but no line-by-line completeness certification is made.
- [Official United States merits brief](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/mobil-oil-exploration-and-producing-southeast-inc-v-united-states-merits), January 2000, saved as clean text `preparation/sources/chunk9_early/mobil_us_merits_official.txt` and read in full, including all footnotes and appendix letters. It supplies the actual respondent defenses and expressly reserved amount challenge. Its party character and internal suspension-description discrepancy are retained.
- `preparation/OT_1999CHUNK9_MOBIL_OPERATIVE_SOURCE.md` contains script-copied lease clauses, MOU/suspension passages and letter terms, with OCR status and provenance. Source authority is the original filed instrument, not the reading copy. Exact numeric judgment, proof of benefits, any later suspension extension, or contested preservation must be checked if decisive; none is silently supplied as a new finding.

## MOU recitation in United States merits brief

6. Knowing that the State's objection was a potential bar to operations under these leases, Mobil entered into lengthy negotiations with the State and the MMS which resulted in a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) dated July 12, 1989. Under the MOU, Mobil agreed to submit a draft plan of exploration for review by the State and MMS undertook to complete an Environmental Report (ER) on the exploratory well proposed by Mobil. J.A. 79. Mobil also requested and obtained a temporary "suspension" of its leases (J.A. 83) which "put the leases on hold, extended their terms, and suspended [lease] rental payment obligations." C.A. App. 128.3. The MOU further provided that "[i]f the State objects to Mobil's certification of consistency [under the Coastal Zone Management Act], or if any other administrative or judical challenges or appeals arise, or if the issuance of the permits for Mobil's exploration activities is delayed due to any of the other circumstances contemplated by 30 C.F.R. § 250.10," the MMS would "issue additional suspensions of operations, pursuant to 30 C.F.R. § 250.10, to allow for the resolution of such matters and to allow Mobil two drilling seasons under the [exploration plan] thereafter." J.A. 83 (emphasis added).

## June 2, 1995 suspension letter appended to United States brief

JUN 02 1995
Mobil Exploration & Producing U.S. Inc.
Attention: MS. Toni D. Hennike
Post Office Box 650232
Dallas, Texas 75265-0232
Dear Ms. Hennike:
Pursuant to Minerals Management Service's (MMS) letter dated October 9, 1992, suspensions of operations (SOO) were issued for the 21 leases in the Manteo Unit, Agreement No. 752-290001-0, offshore North Carolina. The letter states that the SOO's will expire when the two studies recommended by the Environmental Sciences Review Panel have been accepted by the MMS and when the U.S. Department of Commerce renders a decision on the appeals from the State of North Carolina's Denial of Consistency Certification on the Exploration Plan and the NPDES Permit.
By letter dated July 22, 1994, the MMS Transmitted the two aforementioned studies to the Department of Commerce, and on September 2, 1994, the Secretary of Commerce issued a decision upholding North Carolina's objection to your proposed Exploration Plan. The MMS letter dated November 9, 1994, provided formal notice that the suspensions for the 21 leases in the Manteo Unit were terminated and that rental payments are to be calculated from November 10, 1994.
Your letter dated Febrary 21, 1995, requests, pursuant to 30 CFR 250.10(b)(6) and the Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) of July 12, 1989, with the State of North Carolina and the MMS, the reinstatement of the SOO's for the Manteo Unit leases effective November 10, 1994. Our office did not receive the original of this letter; however, a facsimile was received on April 4, 1995.
Mobil filed suit in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia (Case No. 1:95V00093) January 18, 1995, challenging the Secretary's decision and stating that the time period to seek judicial review had not expired when the MMS terminated the SOO's.
Although it is our position that the terms of the SOO approval letter dated October 9, 1992, were satisfied, we believe new suspensions are appropriate pursuant to 30 CFR 250.10(b)(6) and the MOU while Mobil seeks judicial review of the Secretary's decision.
Therefore, we hereby approve SOO's for the Manteo Unit leases from November 10, 1994, until a nonappealable final judgment concerning the Secretary of Commerce's decision is issued from a court of competent jurisdiction. However, pursuant to 30 CFR 250.10(e), the SOO's shall not exceed five years.
We request that Mobil immediately notify this office of the decision rendered in its present suit, referenced above, by the delivery of a copy of said decision.
The terms of the Manto Unit leases will be extended for a period of time equal to the period the SOO's are in effect.
