# Source attachment E: June 26 Jones public law, verbatim

## Event

California Democratic Party v. Jones, No. 99-401. Plenary merits decision; argued April 24, 2000; decided June 26, 2000. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, reviewing 169 F.3d 646 (1999), which adopted the District Court's post-trial ruling, 984 F. Supp. 1288 (1997).

The question is whether California may require objecting political parties to select their official nominees for state and congressional office through a blanket primary that permits every voter to cross party lines office by office. Proposition 198 gives each party's nomination to its highest vote recipient even though voters unaffiliated with that party participate on equal terms. Elections for party committees remain confined to party registrants. All four plaintiff parties oppose the compelled participation through their governing rules.

The trial record supports likely crossover voting and effects in some contests, while distinguishing sincere crossover from comparatively rare organized raiding. Its estimates vary by race and denominator; the Court does not adopt a universal numerical threshold. The parties retain advocacy and endorsement, but those activities do not themselves select the legal nominee. No separate Elections Clause claim, presidential-delegate judgment, damages claim or retrospective election challenge is decided.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the Ninth Circuit's judgment and remands for proceedings consistent with the invalidity of compulsory blanket selection of the objecting parties' official nominees.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 7-2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Stevens and Ginsburg.

The blanket-primary requirement cannot constitutionally compel these objecting parties to submit their official nominations to equal participation by unaffiliated voters. The lower courts must provide declaratory and prospective injunctive relief fitted to that defect. The Court does not reopen an election, award damages, prescribe a replacement ballot, decide every severability question or invalidate an unpresented voluntary or nonpartisan arrangement.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer | Reversal and remand | Severe burden, strict scrutiny, application, limits and remedy in full |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No additional join | Joins the Court in full | Political autonomy and the distinction between a party's choice to broaden appeal and state-compelled alteration of its nominating constituency |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Public-primary distinction and independently sufficient contextual justification; no independent Elections Clause disposition |

The seven Justices joining the Court adopt all three holdings at the same level of generality. No judgment-only vote or narrowest-grounds synthesis is needed.

## Holdings

### 1. Compulsory outsider selection of an official party nominee imposes a severe burden

**Holding and operative rule:** A State severely burdens political association when it requires an objecting party to give voters unaffiliated with that party equal participation in choosing its official nominee through this blanket-primary mechanism. The requirement therefore must be narrowly tailored to serve a compelling state interest; ordinary authority to administer elections does not alone supply that justification.

**Authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer, controls each holding in this entry by seven direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Tashjian protects meaningful associational choice while preserving the State's role in a workable election system. Eu protects party governance against state direction, and Democratic Party v. Wisconsin ex rel. La Follette rejects state-imposed participation that overrides a party's protected selection rules. Hurley protects the composition of private expression; it does not exempt every association from ordinary regulation. Selecting the official standard bearer is a central party act even when the State supplies the primary machinery. California's system changes who makes that choice, rather than merely regulating its time, ballot or administration. Sincere crossover can alter nominations and candidates' positions without organized raiding. The parties need not prove organizational collapse or await an election that conclusively changes every nominee. Retained endorsement and advocacy do not restore the lost selection choice. Burdick and Timmons require attention to the actual burden: Timmons left the party able to choose a consenting nominee through the available system, while this law makes outsiders participants in the party's own nomination. The public character of elections permits regulation and requires protection of voters' constitutional rights; it does not remove this distinct associational injury. The Court decides neither all conventional open primaries nor every membership or registration rule.

### 2. The asserted interests do not justify this compulsory nomination mechanism

**Holding and operative rule:** The interests advanced for Proposition 198 do not justify its severe interference with the objecting parties' nomination choices. Participation, choice, competition, privacy and stable elections may support appropriate election rules, but the State has not established a compelling justification and necessary fit for imposing equal outsider participation in the party's own official selection.

**Controlling explanation:** The State may seek representative government and robust participation, but it cannot justify this intrusion merely by preferring more moderate party nominees or nominees responsive to voters who have chosen another association. That purpose changes the party's political choice rather than protecting the electoral process from an independent injury. Giving independents or minority-party voters influence in another party's safe-district primary likewise does not establish a right to select that party's standard bearer. Increased choice, turnout and competition are legitimate electoral aims, yet the necessary connection to compelled party nomination is missing. A nonpartisan winnowing election can select candidates without purporting to select each party's official nominee, and a party may itself invite additional voters. Those possibilities show why broader participation does not require this mechanism; their mention decides no unpresented system. A privacy interest in political affiliation also does not establish an entitlement to participate secretly in every other party's nomination. The stability and fairness arguments do not identify a necessity sufficient to overcome this severe burden. The Court does not substitute a numerical prediction for constitutional analysis or treat every participation objective as impermissible. The defect is the asserted purposes and insufficient fit of this particular compulsory means.

### 3. Relief follows the nomination defect and does not decide other election systems

**Holding and operative rule:** The defense judgment must be reversed and the case remanded for declaratory and prospective injunctive relief against compulsory blanket selection of these objecting parties' official nominees. The ruling leaves ordinary election administration and constitutional voting protections intact and reserves the validity of unpresented primary designs and any remaining severability or implementation questions.

**Controlling explanation:** The plaintiffs established a constitutional defect in the compulsory choice mechanism, not a right to have this Court administer elections. The remedy therefore prevents that compelled selection without prescribing a ballot, reopening completed elections or awarding damages. Timmons preserves ordinary regulation that leaves meaningful lawful nomination choices; the white-primary decisions preserve voters' constitutional protection against exclusion. Describing less intrusive alternatives does not hold that every top-two or nonpartisan system, party invitation or registration condition is valid. The same defect governs the state and congressional nominations presented. No independently presented Elections Clause or national-delegate claim supplies an additional judgment.

## Precedent Treatment

- Tashjian v. Republican Party of Connecticut supplies protection for meaningful party associational choices within a regulated electoral system; this decision does not adopt an unlimited power of party leaders to override members.
- Eu v. San Francisco County Democratic Central Committee supplies the protection against state control of party governance and political choice; ordinary election administration remains available.
- Democratic Party v. Wisconsin ex rel. La Follette supplies protection against forced participation inconsistent with protected party selection rules; the present decision applies that principle to the challenged official nominations without deciding national-delegate questions.
- Burdick v. Takushi supplies burden-sensitive review of election regulations. The severe burden here triggers compelling-interest and narrow-tailoring review rather than the lesser justification sufficient for ordinary mechanics.
- Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party remains controlling as to its nonfusion restriction. Its retained means of choosing a consenting nominee distinguish it from compelled outsider participation in the actual choice.
- Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections retains its equal-access holding concerning a timely consensual cross-nomination and its limits on the independent association issue. It does not itself decide the present public-primary question.
- Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston supplies protection for the composition of private expression, with its actual limits; this decision neither exempts ordinary services from neutral regulation nor classifies every regulated association as private expression.
- The white-primary decisions, including Smith v. Allwright and Terry v. Adams, retain their prohibitions on unconstitutional exclusion from electoral processes. Associational protection supplies no exemption from those constitutional commands.

## Law After Decision

California may not enforce the compulsory blanket selection of the objecting parties' official nominees. Election regulation remains subject to the burden-sensitive framework, with strict scrutiny for the severe selection injury established here. Timmons and Reform Party retain their distinct holdings about ballot designation and unequal cross-nomination opportunities. The State may pursue broad electoral participation through lawful, less intrusive designs; this decision does not approve every nonpartisan, top-two, voluntary open-primary or registration system. A party's nomination autonomy remains subject to applicable constitutional voting protections.

## Separate Writings

**Kennedy, concurring.** He joins the Court's complete rule and remedy. Political parties may choose to broaden their own appeal, change their principles or invite participation. The State's effort to achieve moderation by changing the electorate that selects a party's official representative instead directs the association's political development. The distinction is between the party's own adaptation and a compulsory substitution of decision makers, not hostility to broader democratic participation. His concurrence adds no campaign-finance holding or adjudication of another primary design.

**Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, dissenting.** They would affirm on the presented associational claim for both state and congressional nominations. They distinguish a publicly required and administered nominating election from the party's private leadership, endorsement and advocacy decisions. Protecting a party's voluntary candidate choices against exclusion does not necessarily give it a constitutional veto over the qualified electorate in that public primary. Timmons's consensual candidate-access dispute and Hurley's private expressive composition do not erase this boundary; Reform Party's equality holding does not resolve it. Independently, the retained party functions, limited and variable injury supported by the trial record, and participation, choice and representative-government interests adequately justify the law under contextual review. They acknowledge that official nomination differs from endorsement and that sincere crossover can matter; neither observation establishes the severe private-association injury the Court finds. Their position does not depend on implied party consent from a popular vote or on the absence of every crossover effect. The unpresented Elections Clause question remains undecided and supplies no different judgment.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Ninth Circuit for declaratory and prospective injunctive relief confined to the unconstitutional compulsory nomination mechanism and any ordinary implementation questions properly remaining. No new election, damages award or statutory replacement is ordered. State and congressional nominations receive the same disposition; national-delegate arrangements and the amended presidential reporting provisions are not adjudicated.

## Source Notes

The initiative's operation, trial findings and judgment below are reported in 984 F. Supp. 1288 and 169 F.3d 646. The petition, joint appendix and merits submissions are preserved in the [Records and Briefs collection, No. 99-401](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0335). The constitutional authorities and their specific functions are identified in Precedent Treatment. Crossover estimates are record-specific and do not establish one statewide percentage or a requirement to prove malicious raiding. The May 4, 1999 presidential-reporting amendment does not supply a national-delegate issue for decision here.

---

## Independent framing, qualifications and readiness

The challenge concerns Colorado section 18-9-122(3), considered June 28, 2000 after January 19 argument, on review of the Colorado Supreme Court. It is a facial preenforcement challenge seeking declaratory and injunctive relief, not review of a conviction or an injunction aimed only at particular wrongdoers.

The statute's precise trigger is a knowing speaker-initiated approach within eight feet without the approached person's consent, for the listed leaflet, sign or oral communicative purposes, on a public way or sidewalk within one hundred feet of an entrance door to a lawfully authorized health-care facility. Colorado's binding no-retreat construction matters: a stationary speaker need not leave because another person approaches. A knowledge element does not itself decide all clarity or overbreadth objections, and consent limits approach rather than every word, sign or presence in the area. Subsection (2)'s obstruction offense, (5)'s local-law permission and (6)'s civil-liability cross-reference are separate. Nothing here adjudicates every civil remedy or local law.

The State's actual access and treatment interests and petitioners' actual peaceful initiation, leafleting and conversational burdens receive substantive consideration. Legislative testimony about misconduct is not proof that these petitioners committed it. The state court's characterization of adequate alternative communication remains reviewable constitutional reasoning, not a conclusive empirical finding. A benign objective does not settle content classification, and a communicative-purpose category does not mechanically resolve the analysis without examining operation and justification.

Madsen and Schenck preserve the protection of initial peaceful contact and distinct scrutiny of moving restraints, but apply a heightened injunction test and specific remedial records. They do not establish a universal forbidden distance or automatically invalidate generally applicable legislation. Ward supplies the neutral legislative route. The model must separately consider classification, the appropriate scrutiny, actual fit/channels, vagueness and overbreadth and match relief to the adopted ground. A valid no-retreat reading cannot be replaced by a hypothetical duty to flee; neither can statutory knowledge be treated as an answer to every protected-activity burden.

The lower opinion's account of a neutrality concession and the complaint's content-discrimination allegation are both preserved. The state court passed on neutrality. No new waiver is inferred without a source showing its actual scope and the ordinary treatment of an issue decided below. If forfeiture itself would determine the result, current petition/briefing verification is required. The merits alternatives may be considered without inventing a forfeiture. A ruling must retain ordinary remedies and the independent subsection boundaries; no substitute distance, damages or conviction disposition is assumed.

Ready for independent modeling at this bounded scope. Exact current preservation, any unreported enforcement construction and actual elements of an unpresented civil claim remain targeted source conditions only if relied on. No other missing material blocks the identified facial merits paths. All eight Associates have their own prior writings/joins in the copied Madsen/Schenck history, with Breyer's clarification-before-invalidation position and Stevens's distinct equity analysis preserved. Prior public views are not present votes.

## Validated objective source extraction

## Hill v. Colorado - No. 98-1856

**Event and question.** Merits consideration June 28, 2000; argued January 19, 2000. Certiorari to the Colorado Supreme Court's February 16, 1999 judgment in Hill v. Thomas, 973 P.2d 1246. Petitioners seek declaratory and injunctive relief against section 18-9-122(3). Their initial complaint asserted prior restraint, content discrimination, vagueness and overbreadth. The state court treated the challenge as facial because there had been no enforcement. The case is not review of convictions, damages, or a defendant-specific injunction.

**Operative law.** The copied statutory text contains all six subsections. Subsection (3) penalizes a knowing approach within eight feet, without consent, for the listed communicative purposes in public-way or sidewalk areas within 100 feet of any entrance door to a health-care facility. It covers leaflets/handbills, signs, oral protest, education and counseling. Subsection (4) defines the facility by legal authority to administer medical treatment. Subsection (2)'s obstruction offense is distinct; subsection (6) adds civil liability by cross-reference. The constitutional review of (3) does not automatically invalidate (2) or resolve all civil-remedy elements.

**Authoritative state construction and judgment.** The Colorado Supreme Court affirmed summary judgment for the State. It construed the statute to require both knowing conduct and an approach by the speaker. A stationary speaker need not retreat when someone comes within eight feet; a third party's movement does not by itself create liability. The rule does not require advance consent for all words, signs or presence in the 100-foot area. It regulates the specified knowing nonconsensual approach. The court considered ordinary conversation and signs possible at eight feet and regarded stationary, consensual and more distant communication as alternatives. That assessment is part of the constitutional conclusion below, not a conclusive empirical finding that every leaflet can be delivered as effectively.

The court applied Ward's rule for general time/place/manner legislation, distinguishing Madsen/Schenck's closer fit for injunctions and Sabelko's retreat requirement. It treated the law as content neutral and the access, safety and medical-treatment interests as significant. It relied on legislative testimony of blocking entrances, intimidation, crowding and confrontation. These are reported legislative materials; they are not findings that these individual petitioners committed violence. The record supports ordinary sidewalk counseling as petitioners' claimed activity.

**Preservation caution.** The Colorado opinion says petitioners did not contest content neutrality in that state appeal and records a concession about the applicable time/place/manner measure. The initial complaint did raise content discrimination, and the state court itself decided neutrality. This extraction supplies neither a new waiver nor a conclusion that every argument is preserved. If disposition turns on forfeiture, verify the actual current petition and briefing and apply the ordinary rule to the issue passed upon; do not silently discard the granted challenge based on an isolated characterization.

**Competing arguments.** Petitioners can press that peaceful personal leafleting and conversational initiation in a traditional public forum receive protection; an advance-consent approach rule covers protected conduct without obstruction or a prior refusal. The statute selects protest, education and counseling by communicative purpose, and its coverage of passers-by and all health facilities may burden more speech than the demonstrated access problem warrants. The distinct obstruction law and other ordinary conduct remedies are practical alternatives. Madsen's protection of peaceful initial approach and Schenck's moving-zone difficulties are relevant, although their injunction standard and larger distances cannot simply be transplanted.

The State relies on generally applicable legislation regulating the mechanics of close approach, neutral as to views and covering all health-care entrances; its knowledge and approach elements avoid forced retreat and accidental violations. The eight-foot measure and 100-foot setting preserve seeing, hearing, stationary offers and willing encounters while serving access and treatment-related interests. Under Ward a rule need not be the least restrictive imaginable. The State still must justify the actual burden and available channels; neither the word safety nor discomfort at a message answers every application.

**Distinct paths and remedy.** Decide classification before applying the corresponding review. If content neutral, test advancement, fit and actual alternative channels under Ward, accounting for the binding no-retreat construction. If content based, identify the classification and apply the proper stricter protection. Address vagueness and overbreadth separately, preserving knowledge, consent and purpose elements. A listener's lack of consent is not automatically a censorship power over all public speech. Any invalidation must match the actual subsection/application; no substitute distance, new conduct finding, conviction reversal or damages award follows automatically.

**Justice-specific sources.** O'Connor authored simulated Madsen's controlling fit rule; Souter and Ginsburg joined its provision-specific analysis, and Souter separately addressed actual-concert limits. Stevens adopted an individualized equitable analysis and sustained the approach term only for the identified enjoined actors, expressly distinguishing a general rule. Scalia's Madsen and Schenck writings, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, emphasize operation, legally supported relief and protected initiation. Breyer's Schenck separate opinion favored first-instance clarification of the disputed mobile terms, without approving every literal floating zone. The majority joins and precise differences are copied from the earlier public renders. Ward v. Rock Against Racism, 491 U.S. 781 (1989), authored by Kennedy, supplies ordinary regulation review; Stevens joined Marshall's dissent. No target-case reasoning supplies a commitment.

**Sources and limits.** [Colorado Supreme Court opinion and reproduced statute](https://static.case.law/p2d/973/html/1246-01.html); [OpenJurist parallel](https://openjurist.org/973/p2d/1246/hill-v-thomas-1301771). The IA collection's own listing was searched by docket and case name: no matching 98-1856 item was returned; the earlier 95-1905 item is not a substitute for the current merits briefs. Lower judgment, operative construction and statutory text suffice for the basic merits paths; exact current preservation or an asserted argument concession needs a specific source before use. ## Exact case-specific source terms

## Hill - all six statutory subsections reproduced below

Source: [hill_lower.txt](https://static.case.law/p2d/973/html/1246-01.html).

(1) The general assembly recognizes that access to health care facilities for the purpose of obtaining medical counseling and treatment is imperative for the citizens of this state; that the exercise of a person’s right to protest or counsel against certain medical procedures must be balanced against another person’s right to obtain medical counseling and treatment in an unobstructed manner; and that preventing the willful obstruction of a person’s access to medical counseling and treatment at a health care facility is a matter of statewide concern. The general assembly therefore declares that it is appropriate to enact legislation that prohibits a person from knowingly obstructing another person's entry to or exit from a health care facility.
      (2) A person commits a class 3 misdemeanor if such person knowingly obstructs, detains, hinders, impedes, or blocks another person’s entry to or exit from a health care facility.
      (3) No person shall knowingly approach another person within eight feet of such person, unless such other person consents, for the purpose of passing a leaflet or handbill to, displaying a sign to, or engaging in oral protest, education, or counseling with such other person in the public way or sidewalk area within a radius of one hundred feet from any entrance door to a health care facility. Any person who violates this subsection (3) commits a class 3 misdemeanor.
      (4) For the purposes of this section “health care facility” means any entity that is licensed, certified, or otherwise authorized or permitted by law to administer medical treatment in this state.
      (5) Nothing in this section shall be construed to prohibit a statutory or home rule city or county or city and county from adopting a law for the control of access to health care facilities *1250that is no less restrictive than the provisions of this section.
      (6) In addition to, and not in lieu of, the penalties set forth in this section, a person who violates the provisions of this section shall be subject to civil liability, as provided in section' 13-21-106.7, C.R.S.

Cutoff: immediately before June 28, 2000. All four June 28 matters use the same entering-law baseline. ## Standards and Tests selection: Speech burdens in a content-neutral injunction

### Speech burdens in a content-neutral injunction

**Governing question:** How a court tailors a content-neutral injunction regulating speech-related conduct.

**Current rule:** A content-neutral injunction must burden no more speech than necessary to serve the significant governmental interest supporting the remedy. This inquiry is more demanding than the ordinary content-neutral legislative time, place and manner rule, without becoming a universal least-restrictive-means test. Each restriction needs a remedial connection to the established interest and conduct. Public access, medical quiet and residential privacy may support relief, but neither those interests nor prior misconduct justifies territorial restrictions or advance-permission conditions broader than necessary. Removing an invalid restriction also removes its operative predicate; it cannot be restored by relabeling the same conduct under another clause. Within an otherwise valid fixed access zone, an exception permitting nonthreatening initial counseling by up to two people per approached person or group may end when the recipient objectively communicates, verbally or nonverbally, that counseling is unwanted. The counselor must end that close encounter and leave the fixed zone, while retaining lawful advocacy outside it; this supplies neither universal silence nor a mobile personal radius.

**Authority by component:**

- Content classification and five-Justice injunction standard: Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc — The decree as confined is not categorically content based; A neutral speech injunction must burden no more speech than necessary, 1994-06-30. O'Connor's Part II-A(2), joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg: six. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reject this application while accepting the separate actual-concert principle. O'Connor's Part II-B, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five. Stevens's individualized equitable account and the strict-restraint dissent are not joins in this standard.
- Removal of invalidated predicates and lawful remedial discretion: Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc — Relief removes invalidated predicates; Remand permits lawful relief without requiring a personal zone, 1994-06-30. O'Connor's Part IV-B, joined by all eight other Justices: nine at the invalidated-predicate and no-evasion level. This agreement does not settle the permissible content of a new decree. O'Connor's Part IV-C, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight. Stone-Zsela dissents from the refusal to require his bounded post-refusal replacement.

- Coverage boundary retained in applying the speech-injunction rule: Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc — An injunction binds responsible parties and actual concert, not a viewpoint, 1994-06-30. O'Connor's Part II-A(1), joined by all eight other Justices: nine. The three dissenters on operational neutrality join only this distinct coverage principle.

- Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York — The fixed-zone counseling exception may end after a communicated refusal, 1997-02-19; Stone-Zsela with Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six) supplies the confined communicated-refusal operation

**Present operation effective:** Schenck, 1997-02-19, for the fixed-zone counseling qualification; Madsen's governing standard and other components retain their 1994-06-30 authority.

**Exceptions and limits:** The particular distances and hours adjudicated are applications, not universal safe harbors. The decision does not require a personal buffer zone, decide the reserved independent conduct-language claims, or transplant Casey’s abortion-regulation framework into the advocacy inquiry. Coverage remains confined to responsible parties and persons with notice acting in actual concert or participation; viewpoint, association or independent advocacy alone does not establish concert.

**Holdings navigation:** An injunction binds responsible parties and actual concert, not a viewpoint; Relief removes invalidated predicates; Remand permits lawful relief without requiring a personal zone; The decree as confined is not categorically content based; A neutral speech injunction must burden no more speech than necessary. Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York (1997-02-19) — The fixed-zone counseling exception may end after a communicated refusal.

## Hill - controlling prior holdings


Source: `state/holdings/first-amendment.md`. Copied sections.


#### Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-880.

**Decided:** 1994-06-30.

For the other questions in Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 1994-06-30, No. 93-880, see Federal Courts — The limited scope of geographic and paragraph (4) review; Federal Courts — An injunction binds responsible parties and actual concert, not a viewpoint; Federal Courts — The independent conduct-language claims remain undecided; Federal Courts — Relief removes invalidated predicates; Federal Courts — Remand permits lawful relief without requiring a personal zone.

##### The decree as confined is not categorically content based

**Holding:** As authoritatively confined to named responsible parties and actual concert with notice, this injunction addresses established interference with access, traffic, medical care and residential privacy rather than abortion advocacy as such; defendant-specific selection does not make it categorically content or viewpoint based. Each restraint still requires independent constitutional scrutiny, and a materially different viewpoint-based application receives no approval.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II-A(2), joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg: six. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reject this application while accepting the separate actual-concert principle.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Ward v. Rock Against Racism*, 491 U.S. 781: applies justification without reference to content; defendant-specific relief still requires scrutiny.
- *Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC*, June 27, 1994: applies its operation-and-justification distinction without importing its cable-specific result or plurality evidentiary application.
- *Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, Inc.*, March 24, 1993: preserves the rule that a benign objective alone does not cure a speech-selective burden.

##### A neutral speech injunction must burden no more speech than necessary

**Holding:** A content-neutral injunction burdening protected expression must burden no more speech than necessary to remedy the established conduct and serve the corresponding significant governmental interest. Its precise terms require a closer fit than ordinary review of generally applicable neutral time, place and manner rules; neither an injunction label nor past misconduct permits suppression of unrelated lawful advocacy.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II-B, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five. Stevens's individualized equitable account and the strict-restraint dissent are not joins in this standard.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Ward v. Rock Against Racism*: distinguishes generally applicable neutral regulation from this direct injunction; its ordinary rule remains intact.
- *Carroll v. President and Commissioners of Princess Anne*, 393 U.S. 175: applies its demand for precision in speech-burdening injunctive relief.
- *NAACP v. Claiborne Hardware Co.*: applies the relation between unlawful conduct, responsible persons and permissible relief.
- *Califano v. Yamasaki*, 442 U.S. 682: applies the limitation of equitable relief to what the established violation requires.

##### The qualified public access arrangement survives

**Holding:** The public portion of the 36-foot clinic clearance, with its retained south shoulder, east shoulder subject to the five-foot setback and no-embankment rule, and prompt-crossing exception, burdens no more speech than necessary on this record to secure driveway access and traffic after narrower obstruction commands failed. It remains enforceable at that qualified scope, without creating a generally permissible clinic-buffer distance.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III-A, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five on geographic fit. Stevens adds a vote to leave the arrangement intact only against the categorical challenge; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would reverse the expressive exclusion.

**Material application:** The southern boundary is the south edge of Dixie Way's pavement, leaving the unpaved south shoulder available subject to lawful restrictions. On the east, the U.S. Highway One shoulder remains usable, but defendants may not ascend the embankment and must remain five feet from the clinic's east line. Pending painted lines, prompt crossing uses an eight-foot strip directly from Dixie Way's south to north edge, with its western line coextensive with Highway One's western right-of-way line; once marked, the crosswalk governs. Within the buffer no other pavement entry is allowed. Crossing permits no stopping, hesitation, picketing, congregation, demonstration or traffic impediment. This is an existing crossing dimension, not a personal-separation order.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Carroll*: applies precision to the access boundary and actual crossing and shoulder permissions.
- *Claiborne Hardware*: confines the expressive burden to supported access relief.
- *City of Ladue v. Gilleo*, June 13, 1994: applies attention to usable alternatives without creating an entitlement to every preferred location.

##### The private-property extension lacks the necessary remedial connection

**Holding:** The north/west extension of the 36-foot exclusion cannot suppress otherwise lawful expression on adjoining private land without a demonstrated connection between that full exclusion and the access or operational harm to be remedied. Florida's approval of that extension is reversed; removing the government's expressive restriction neither authorizes trespass nor adjudicates owners' property rights.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III-B, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur in reversal on their stricter grounds; Stevens declines distinct geographic review.

**Material application:** The north/west owners and invitees were excepted from the entry prohibition, while the other expressive restrictions still applied when they acted in concert. Removing the governmental expressive exclusion adjudicates neither permission to enter nor private property boundaries.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Claiborne Hardware*: applies the requirement that relief correspond to the established wrong.
- *Carroll*: applies precision to the unsupported geographic extension.
- *Ladue*: preserves practical channel analysis; it supplies no right to use another person's property.

##### The qualified medical-noise restriction survives

**Holding:** Paragraph (4)'s noise restriction is valid in its medical-operation context from 7:30 a.m. through noon, Monday–Saturday, during surgical procedures and recovery periods, as to the listed and other sounds within earshot of patients inside the clinic. Its affirmance rests on the treatment interference established here, not disagreement with a message, and adds no decibel, intent or newly drafted volume threshold.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III-C, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would reverse on strict direct-restraint and adequate-unlawful-predicate grounds, despite accepting fit under the Court's assumed standard; Stevens declines merits.

**Material application:** The covered sounds are singing, chanting, whistling, shouting, yelling, bullhorns, auto horns, amplification equipment and other sounds within the earshot of patients inside the clinic, in the lower court's medical-operation context. Both the 7:30 a.m.–noon Monday–Saturday window and the surgery-or-recovery condition apply; neither is replaced by a newly invented sound threshold.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Ward v. Rock Against Racism*: applies the distinction between controlling sound's physical effects and suppressing its content; the heightened injunction rule governs this remedy.
- *Forsyth County v. Nationalist Movement*, June 19, 1992: preserves the distinction between a neutral harm and burdens resting on hostility to a message.
- *Carroll*: applies precision through the time, treatment and patient-location limits.

##### The prohibition on observable images is too broad

**Holding:** The prohibition on all images observable to patients inside the clinic, even within the stated morning surgery-and-recovery periods, burdens more speech than necessary on this record and must be removed. Patient vulnerability or distress at a message does not establish a general power to prevent visible advocacy; independently lawful threat or intrusion relief remains available.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III-D, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur in reversal on stricter grounds; Stevens declines merits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Erznoznik v. City of Jacksonville*, 422 U.S. 205: applies protection against broad suppression merely because images are visible to unwilling viewers.
- *Boos v. Barry*, 485 U.S. 312: applies the distinction between communicative emotional effects and neutral physical harms.
- *Carroll*: requires precision beyond a categorical visual prohibition unsupported by the demonstrated need.

##### Peaceful initial approach cannot depend on advance invitation throughout 300 feet

**Holding:** Paragraph (5)'s requirement of an indication of interest before any physical approach to a person seeking clinic services within 300 feet burdens more speech than necessary and must be removed. This holding protects peaceful initial advocacy, not a right to accompany, encircle, surround, threaten, obstruct or force communication upon a person who declines it, and approves no universal application of disputed harassment or abuse terms.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III-E, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter and Ginsburg: five. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur in reversal on stricter grounds; Stevens dissents from reversal of this physical-approach condition.

**Material application:** The clause regulates physical approach, not every word or sign within 300 feet. Invited nonthreatening conversation and literature were permitted when a service-seeker approached or inquired, but never inside the 36-foot zone. Protections against accompanying, encircling, surrounding, harassment, threats and physical or verbal abuse of a declining person retain only their independently lawful scope; reversal neither invalidates every conduct protection nor approves every disputed use of those labels.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Claiborne Hardware*: applies protection of persuasion and the connection between misconduct and relief.
- *Boos*: preserves political approach while allowing properly grounded protection against threats and coercion.
- *Carroll*: requires the restriction to distinguish protected initiation from the conduct justifying restraint.

##### Residential privacy does not justify this territorial exclusion

**Holding:** The 300-foot residential prohibition on approaching, congregating, picketing, patrolling, demonstrating and amplification sweeps beyond the supported protection of a targeted home and must be removed. Its advance-invitation qualification does not restore general neighborhood expression; distinct lawful obstruction, trespass, threat and properly focused residential protections are preserved without selecting a replacement distance.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III-F, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg: six on this bounded ground. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur in reversal under their stricter direct-restraint account; the judgment is unanimous.

**Material application:** Paragraph (6)'s separate bans on blocking entrances, exits or driveways and impeding a street providing sole access to the residences' street are distinct from the territorial ban. The separate invited-contact provision allows willing contact outside the clinic buffer, with residential invitation communicated from a distance before contact, not obtained by an uninvited knock or doorbell. Invitation may be revoked, requiring immediate termination and departure. Retained language must conform to reversal and cannot recreate the invalid territorial exclusion.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Frisby v. Schultz*, 487 U.S. 474: applies its distinction between focused home picketing and general neighborhood expression; its narrow residential protection remains.
- *City of Ladue v. Gilleo*: applies practical attention to lost channels while preserving the distinction between one's own home and intrusion on another's.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Florida judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded for a conforming decree. The qualified public-access arrangement and medical-noise restriction remain; the private extension, images prohibition, initial-invitation requirement and residential territorial ban must be removed, including dependent use under paragraph (9). The predecessor and independently lawful unreviewed terms are not automatically dissolved. The court may consider precisely fitted lawful conduct relief, but no replacement injunction, personal distance, new sound threshold, damages or contempt judgment is commanded.




#### Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1065.
**Decided:** February 19, 1997.

##### Fixed access restrictions satisfy the governing injunction standard on this record

**Holding:** A content-neutral speech injunction must burden no more speech than necessary to remedy the established conduct and serve its corresponding significant interest. These fixed fifteen-foot demonstration restrictions at identified doorways, driveway entrances and parking-access points of covered facilities—all facilities where abortions are performed in the Western District of New York—with nonthreatening initial counseling by no more than two counselors per approached person or group, satisfy that requirement on the supported obstruction and crowding record; fifteen feet is not a universal safe harbor.

The injunction binds responsible parties and persons with notice acting in actual concert; shared views, association or independent advocacy alone do not establish that relationship. This coverage boundary neither adjudicates new nonparty liability nor determines whether a particular speech restriction is sufficiently tailored.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's fixed-access holding is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, six Justices. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas also join the responsible-party, notice and actual-concert boundary, giving that distinct proposition nine votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decree regulates demonstration, not mere presence; the counseling limit is per approached person or group, not two counselors for an entire zone. Substantial temporary-order compliance does not erase supported access interference, and no requirement to disobey a speech-neutral predecessor or exhaust every narrower possibility is adopted. The temporary order already contained personal-distance and counseling restrictions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen supplies provision-specific fit; its distances are not safe harbors. Carroll supplies precision and Claiborne Hardware ties relief to responsible misconduct. Ward's rule for ordinary neutral regulation does not replace the heightened injunction standard.

##### The fixed-zone counseling exception may end after a communicated refusal

**Holding:** Within an otherwise valid fixed access zone, the allowance for nonthreatening counseling by up to two people per approached person or group may end when the recipient objectively communicates verbally or nonverbally that counseling is unwanted. The counselor must end that close encounter and leave the fixed zone, while retaining lawful advocacy outside it; the condition establishes neither silence everywhere nor a mobile personal radius beyond the fixed zone.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Unexpressed unwillingness does not trigger departure. This condition is an exception to independently valid fixed access relief, not a general unwilling-listener veto or captive-audience rule.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen's protection of peaceful initial approach remains: permission to begin an encounter within a valid corridor differs from the broad advance-permission requirement invalidated there. Boos preserves the distinction between physical harm and offense at communicative content.

##### The moving-person restriction burdens more speech than the record warrants

**Holding:** The injunction's fifteen-foot demonstration boundary moving with each person seeking access to or leaving a covered facility burdens more protected speech than necessary under Madsen and must be struck, even with its initial counseling exception and communicated-refusal qualification. This record-specific holding does not create a right to threaten, touch, obstruct, crowd or coercively pursue anyone, or invalidate every precisely supported separation rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The initial counseling and refusal qualifications do not cure uncertainty from moving listeners and overlapping zones. The Court invents neither a travel cap nor an overlap rule to sustain the actual restraint. Precisely supported, materially different conduct relief remains open.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen requires independent fit for the additional moving burden; Carroll supplies precision where contempt enforces it, and Claiborne Hardware preserves the misconduct nexus.

##### The moving-vehicle restriction independently fails

**Holding:** The fifteen-foot demonstration radius moving with vehicles seeking access to or leaving covered facilities likewise burdens more speech than necessary on this record and must be struck. The decision preserves traffic safety and fixed access protection without deciding the validity of a materially different, precisely supported vehicle restriction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen requires a separate vehicle-specific fit; fixed entrance protection and traffic interests do not automatically justify a moving demonstration radius.

##### Removal of the moving predicate also removes the dependent retreat distance

**Holding:** Once paragraph 1(b)'s moving-person and moving-vehicle restrictions are invalidated, paragraph 1(c)'s cross-reference cannot recreate an automatic fifteen-foot moving distance outside valid fixed zones. That dependent operation must be removed on remand; independently lawful conduct prohibitions remain, and the Court neither commands a substitute distance nor decides every cessation condition considered apart from the invalidated predicate.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The fixed access and in-zone refusal components are affirmed 6–3; the person and vehicle moving restraints are reversed 8–1; their automatic outside-zone retreat operation is vacated 8–1. Remand requires a conforming preliminary decree, preserving independently lawful unreviewed conduct terms. No replacement distance, travel endpoint, compulsory substitute, contempt judgment, damages or final permanent decree is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen's invalidated-predicate and no-evasion rules require removal of the dependent distance; its refusal to compel a substitute personal zone remains intact.

### Madsen - published source

Source: `terms/OT1993/output/OT_1993CHUNK8.md`.


#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Reaches geographic fit and paragraph (4) | Fair inclusion of geographic tailoring and the confined exceptional paragraph (4) review; eight |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II-A(1) | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Common coverage principle | Actual concert and notice, not shared views or notice alone; nine; no automatic join in operational neutrality |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II-A(2) | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Rejects the present categorical challenge | Neutral operation as authoritatively confined; six |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II-B and Parts III-A through III-E | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Souter, Ginsburg | Governing standard; sustains public access/noise and reverses private extension/images/initial consent | Heightened neutral-injunction rule and each distinct application; five |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III-F | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Reverses residential territory | Narrow residential fit holding; six; Stevens does not join II-B |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV-A | O'Connor | Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Leaves independent (7)–(9) merits undecided | Rule 14 nonreach; eight; Stone-Zsela agrees only in disposition |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV-B | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Ensures effective relief on reversed components | No invalidated predicate or evasion through another clause; nine at this precise level |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV-C | O'Connor | Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Remand permits, but does not command, new lawful conduct relief | No mandatory personal-separation replacement or judicially selected distance; eight; other component disagreements remain |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins Parts I, II, III and IV-B; agrees in IV-A's disposition; dissents from IV-C's refusal to require post-refusal replacement | Complete bounded post-refusal order and conditionally proposed eight-foot measure |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Joins II-A, III-F and IV; would sustain initial-approach restriction; declines distinct geography/noise/images merits | Individual equitable complete-relief standard and question-presentation limits; no join in II-B or III-A through III-E |
| Concurrence | Souter | No other Justice | Joins the Court in the scopes above | Actual concert remains an individual issue; preliminary appearance statements do not establish every stranger's liability |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas throughout | Joins I, II-A(1), IV; concurs in reversal of private extension, images, initial consent and residences; would also reverse public exclusion and noise | Strict direct-restraint review, operational viewpoint objection and adequate-unlawful-predicate objection; assumed noise fit is not affirmance |

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in part and dissents in part. He joins the closer injunction-specific standard and the Court's component merits dispositions. He would require a replacement conduct-based post-refusal protection on remand, proposing eight feet subject to site-specific proof and a narrower supported implementation if that measure cannot be justified. This preferred mandatory remedy differs from the Court's permission to consider lawful relief. He agrees only in the disposition leaving the independent validity of the particular disputed terms and applications of paragraphs (7)–(9) undecided; that disposition-only agreement supplies no additional rationale.

Outside the fixed access zone, an enjoined person must remain free to make a peaceful initial approach, offer literature or request conversation. Protection begins only after words or clear conduct convey that further individualized contact is unwanted, a reasonable person would understand that refusal, and the enjoined speaker has notice. Silence, disagreement with the viewpoint and entry into the vicinity do not automatically trigger it. Once triggered, that speaker must stop following, shadowing, accompanying, repeated reapproaches, surrounding and close-range individualized speech, including coordinated substitution of another participant to continue the same pursuit. His proposed order would require at least eight feet of separation while the person proceeds toward or away from the facility within a finite, ascertainable, route-related clinic vicinity.

The refusal ends a particular unwanted encounter, not all exposure to a viewpoint. Lawful stationary signs, speech to the public, offers to willing listeners and communication from outside the separation remain protected. An unavoidable passing encounter, a patient voluntarily approaching a stationary protester and momentary inability to separate safely must not be punished. The speaker must stop targeted pursuit and take reasonable safe steps once able to comply; a listener cannot force a speaker from an otherwise lawful stationary location. Renewed voluntary engagement can reopen communication, but repeated unwanted approaches cannot be used to solicit that permission.

Carroll's precision requirement, Claiborne Hardware's protection of advocacy and association, Yamasaki's remedial limitation, Ward's content distinction and Frisby's focused protection support his proposed conduct-based response. The practical aim is to make the termination of a close encounter observable after conduct that defeated earlier protection. Yet a moving personal zone may burden leafleting, be difficult to measure on a narrow sidewalk, or turn aversion to speech into suppression. The stated safeguards answer those dangers without proving the number necessary. The state court would have to define the vicinity, support space and necessity with record-specific findings, and consider concrete less burdensome means. If eight feet cannot lawfully be supported, it must implement a supported narrower protection, rather than an unsupported numerical floor. Eight feet is a proposed operational measure, not a constitutional number or an already established rule.

Legally bound defendants and persons shown to act in concert with notice are the only subjects of that order. Touching, grabbing, intentional obstruction, threats, vehicle interference, surrounding and coordinated physical intimidation may independently be prohibited when the record and governing law support them, without waiting for refusal. These express conduct positions are not blanket approval of every disputed term in paragraphs (7)–(9). Casey protects choice against state interference and creates no direct private-liability right here; Bray preserves independently grounded state relief without weakening speech protection. Contempt requires notice and the process appropriate to the actual sanction. The separate writing decides no contempt-classification question and rests on no later personal-zone authority.

Justice Stevens concurs in part and dissents in part. He rejects categorical content discrimination and the association challenge, joins the residential reversal, and agrees with the Court's nonreach and limited remand. He declines separate geographic tailoring of the 36-foot zone and the noise and images merits. Under Izumi, later argument does not alone enlarge the petition. His earlier willingness to reach an indispensable intervention issue does not require resolving additional provisions unnecessary to these selected questions. Leaving those provisions intact on that limited review is not a merits endorsement.

His injunction standard is individualized equitable relief, not the Court's heightened speech-injunction formula or a universal adoption of ordinary Ward review. Yamasaki requires no more burden than necessary to give complete relief; Professional Engineers, Hudson and Paradise explain why a remedy for identified wrongdoers can reach beyond the precise past act when needed to prevent recurrence or eliminate its effects. The trial court's familiarity matters. Those principles do not insulate every remedy or presume that the entire decree is necessary.

He would sustain paragraph (5)'s physical-approach restraint for the bound actors in this established setting. It is not a prohibition on every unsolicited word or all advocacy within 300 feet. Distant expression, offered verbal or written counsel that does not become prohibited approach, and invited nonthreatening conversation remain distinct. The documented approaches and interference support this individualized constraint even though a similar general rule for all speakers would pose a different issue. He therefore disagrees with the reversal of the initial-invitation condition, but does not endorse a general right to suppress unwelcome ideas.

He joins the narrower Frisby-based conclusion that the residential radius exceeds supported protection. Advance invitation does not cure suppression of general neighborhood activity. His agreement there does not join the Court's distinct standard or the private-geography, noise and images merits. He would permit lawful consideration of further precise conduct relief on remand; he does not require a new post-refusal injunction or endorse a numerical substitute. His equitable theory and clinic-approach position remain noncontrolling.

Justice Souter concurs. He emphasizes that actual concert is a matter for individual determination, not a synonym for opposition to the clinic. Regal Knitwear allows an injunction to reach actual assistance to a bound party while preserving a stranger's right to deny that relation. The preliminary appearance exchanges included troubling references to abortion opposition, but also reserved nonassociation and concert defenses for later proceedings and distinguished arrest reports from formal charges. They cannot be treated as a final finding against every absent person.

His concurrence accepts the written order and Florida's authoritative limiting construction without declaring every later enforcement neutral. A person must have notice and must actually fall within the lawful bound category; receiving the order alone supplies neither concert nor guilt. His position differs from the dissent's operational inference, yet grants no immunity to an unconstitutional application. The named petitioners' categorical challenge and Cheffer's independent federal preliminary-relief litigation remain separate. He joins the provision-specific speech review and derivative relief; his additional explanation neither approves a new distance nor imposes a mandatory replacement decree.

Justice Scalia concurs in part and dissents in part, joined throughout by Justices Kennedy and Thomas. They would also reverse the public expressive exclusion and the noise restriction. Their first sufficient objection is that a judicial command directly forbidding selected persons' future protected advocacy demands the rigorous protection for prior restraints even if nominally content neutral. Carroll, Keefe, Vance and Claiborne Hardware support that distinction. Ordinary conduct orders and incidental consequences of neutral sanctions are different. Alexander's distinction between postconviction effects and a direct future-speech prohibition does not shelter this order.

They separately reject the decree's asserted neutral operation. Turner requires attention to what a rule does, not merely a benign description of its aims, while preserving the proposition that speaker identity alone is not content discrimination. Here the already reported preliminary exchanges connecting coverage to opposition to the clinic supply the additional operational concern. The three accept the abstract legality of actual concert with notice and the bar on ideological guilt; they do not treat that legal category as proof of neutral implementation. They determine no absent person's concert or contempt.

The public-zone objection also concerns the legal predicate for this expansion. They accept the facts found but dispute whether incidental slowing and momentary hesitation, without the necessary relation to obstruction forbidden by the predecessor, legally justify the whole exclusion of peaceful expression. This is a legal sufficiency objection, not a retrial through selected video. Precisely directed road, driveway or conduct controls may address genuine interference; none is found sufficient or ordered as a compulsory substitute. The crossing and shoulder permissions do not themselves establish the necessity of the full exclusion.

For noise, they accept that the qualified clause fits the Court's test if that test is assumed. That concession does not affirm it or join the governing standard. The separate objection is that medically desirable restraint is not an identified adequate prior or threatened violation of statute, common law or the predecessor justifying this personalized speech order. They do not find that Florida has no applicable noise law, deny medical harm or invent a decibel threshold. Strict direct-restraint review independently remains applicable. They concur in reversing private-side exclusion, images, initial-consent approach and residential territory under their stricter grounds, without a right to trespass, threaten, pursue a declining person or invade a home.

They join the confined review-scope ruling and decline independent paragraphs (7)–(9) merits despite actual briefing. Every predicate they would reverse must fall from dependent enforcement; at minimum they join removal of the predicates this judgment actually sets aside. Independently lawful obstruction, assault, threats and conduct remedies survive. Their position does not command a replacement personal zone, approve Stone-Zsela's proposed distance, decide an unreported contempt or dissolve the entire predecessor injunction.

### Schenck - published source

Source: `terms/OT1996/output/OT_1996CHUNK4.md`.


#### Separate Writings

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the Court's fixed/mobile distinction and removal of the automatic retreat. In his view the supported repeated close pursuit permits a narrower provision on remand: preserve an initial peaceful approach, but after an objectively clear verbal or nonverbal refusal require the enjoined defendants to stop following and maintain a modest, precisely defined separation supported by the record. Direct conduct rules can address obstruction, touching, threats and harassment without making every speaker calculate an automatic moving fifteen-foot boundary. He would permit, not require, that carefully fitted provision. It would restrict neither nonparties, willing conversation, the initial peaceful approach, stationary advocacy nor speech addressed generally, and would create no undefined personal-space right or general listener's veto.

**Justice Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justices Kennedy and Thomas.** Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join the Court's independent conclusion that the moving restraints fail Madsen and that their dependent distance must fall. They would invalidate the fixed expressive restrictions as well. Their stricter approach requires a legally supported remedial predicate and direct justification for restraining protected expression. The existing conduct prohibitions and substantial TRO compliance make the added expressive exclusion doubtful; appellate assumptions about what access relief might justify cannot replace the trial court's actual assessment. In particular, treating cessation as a discretionary counseling privilege does not establish its actual necessity when the lower rationale relies on unwilling listeners' personal space. General safety interests cannot by themselves establish entitlement to a private judicial remedy. They preserve lawful obstruction and physical-interference restraints and do not claim a right to threaten or coerce. Their disagreement with fixed relief is consistent with joining the sufficient Madsen ground against the moving restraints.

**Justice Breyer, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He joins the qualified fixed access and counseling rulings but would leave the lower judgment intact pending the District Court's interpretation and possible modification of the disputed mobile terms. The lower court expressly left their geographic reach and particular applications unresolved and retained an ordinary route for clarification, including problems arising from escorts or overlapping encounters. In his view contextual construction and first-instance administration should precede invalidating the disputed operation. He does not approve an unlimited radius, supply a travel cap himself or conclude that every literal floating zone is constitutional. His disagreement concerns the Court's decision to invalidate now on this posture.
