# Hill — narrow entering-law refresh for City of Erie v. Pap's A.M.

**Scope and cutoff:** This supplement adds the completed March 29, 2000 public law and published Justice positions in City of Erie v. Pap's A.M., No. 98-1161, to the neutral Hill packet for June 28, 2000. It revises no fact, statutory term, question, source limit or judgment below in that original packet. The appended complete Erie Public Projection is copied verbatim from the authorized preparation artifact. This is public earlier current-term law, not the historical Supreme Court decision or a private current position in Hill.

**Materiality:** Erie materially supplements the classification and justification inquiry already identified in Hill and supplies recent, differentiated positions for every Associate. It does not displace Ward's legislative rule or transplant the heightened Madsen/Schenck injunction rule into this statute. Its actual five-Justice merits proposition concerns a public-nudity ordinance whose justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting; it rejects incidental-burden treatment and finds Erie's resulting scrutiny unsatisfied while preserving genuinely general conduct rules and supported regulation of nonexpressive harms. That proposition bears on how a court distinguishes genuine control of noncommunicative harms from suppression of expression. It does not decide whether Colorado's specified approach rule has that character or invalidate every statute using communicative-purpose terms.

**Authority limits:** Stevens's full Part II explanation has four joins; the common precise merits proposition has five direct supporters across Part II and the Chief Justice's separate concurrence, without a fifth full join or Marks synthesis. Part III's independent intermediate-scrutiny insufficiency ground has only three votes. Souter's separate conclusion of insufficiency and different remand cannot be added to Part III as another vote for its final disposition. Erie creates no controlling universal local-study requirement, mandatory hearing, strict least-restrictive-means rule, or automatic invalidity of general nudity regulation. Its mootness holding concerns the particular continuing state judgment and adversity after a business closure; no analogous closure or new jurisdictional fact is supplied in Hill.

**Current public positions relevant to fresh modeling:**

- Stevens and Ginsburg joined the fuller finding of actual communicative selection and the independently insufficient advancement/fit ground. Their conclusions rested on Erie's record, including attributed governmental interpretations; they do not supply a Hill result.
- Scalia joined the fuller content-selection ground but not the independent intermediate-scrutiny alternative. Thomas joined both. Their threshold dissent did not remove their conditional merits participation. Their distinct grounds must not be flattened into a shared alternative holding.
- O'Connor found the particular adult-establishment application justified under O'Brien based on the general conduct scope, identified noncommunicative harms, relevant outside inference and limited retained burden. Her approach still required affirmative justification and actual scrutiny; benign purpose or lack of contrary proof did not suffice alone.
- Kennedy and Breyer favored a nonvalidating remand for the actual classification and, if appropriate, advancement and fit. They reserved present evidentiary sufficiency and did not choose final invalidation or validation. They rejected both a conclusive general-text defense and a conclusive inference from a single nonenforcement episode.
- Souter joined that common classification inquiry and nonvalidating remand, but separately found the existing demonstration insufficient even assuming O'Brien. His proposed opportunity to justify remained conditional on unresolved state-law or overbreadth routes; he prescribed neither an unconditional study nor a hearing.

**Neutral application:** Hill's model must compare the statute's actual operation and asserted access/treatment interests with its burdens on peaceful initiation, leafleting and conversation, retaining the binding no-retreat construction and the difference between a generally applicable law and an injunction. Erie strengthens the need to identify the actual noncommunicative harm, the asserted means and any contrary evidence, without deciding that comparison. Both the supported neutral-regulation route and the supported communicative-selection objection remain to be assessed independently. Erie does not eliminate the original packet's separate vagueness, overbreadth, preservation and remedy questions or turn a source limit into litigant default.

**Readiness:** The omission is material to entering law and the individual prior-position inquiry, so the revised packet is the handoff for fresh affected modeling before a Hill commitment is frozen. No current Hill commitment, comparator, reconciliation, Stone material or target historical opinion was inspected. This validation decides no vote or coalition and supplies no newly reconstructed event.


## Exact earlier current-term public law: Erie

## Event

City of Erie v. Pap's A.M., No. 98-1161. Merits decision, March 29, 2000. Argued November 10, 1999. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania, reviewing its October 21, 1998 judgment, 719 A.2d 273. The grant concerns the application of Erie's minimal-clothing requirement to adult-entertainment dancers and the threshold question whether the Court can reach that federal dispute after Pap's ceased operating Kandyland.

Erie enacted Ordinance 75-1994 on September 28, 1994. Section 1(c) prohibits knowingly or intentionally appearing nude in a public place. Section 2 defines nudity through specified insufficiently covered anatomy and devices or coverings that simulate specified anatomy; those distinct terms are reproduced below. Section 3 extends public place to outdoor and enclosed venues, including adult or membership clubs, theaters and invited events with or without admission charges. Section 4 excepts children under ten and an individual exposing a breast while breastfeeding an infant under two. Section 6 directs the least constitutional infringement consistent with the ordinance's purposes and requires severance when a court finds a portion, application or enforcement excessively restrictive of the specified rights. It contains no enacted exception for expression judged artistically superior.

Pap's operated Kandyland, a commercial establishment with nude female dancing and no alcohol sales, though patrons could bring alcohol. The Pennsylvania majority invalidated the nudity restriction under the First Amendment and severed sections 1(c) and 2, leaving the separate prohibitions on sexual intercourse, deviate sexual intercourse and genital fondling intact. The majority did not decide the Pennsylvania Constitution, substantial overbreadth or the objection to standing to assert overbreadth. A two-Justice state constitutional concurrence did not provide the majority judgment's independent ground.

The submitted record includes the Council video, the parties' agreed facts and the City's repeated litigation interpretation that theatrical expression such as Equus, Hair and Oh! Calcutta! could receive different treatment as higher protected expression through section 6 and the preamble. Counsel attributed that interpretation to the City and Council, acknowledged its subjectivity, and sought judicial narrowing; the trial court did not adopt a binding theatrical exemption. The parties agreed that Equus featured frontal nudity for several weeks in October and November 1994 without enforcement efforts during a run in which the prohibition became effective. They deleted the proposed characterization of the production's artistic and social merit. The admitted episode does not establish the sole reason for nonenforcement, a complete enforcement policy or official awareness of every performance. The early temporary restraint and differing effective-date descriptions prevent treating every day of the run as an available enforcement opportunity.

Pap's closed Kandyland and sold the property in 1998. Its June 1999 representation disclaimed an interest in the premises or another nude-dancing business; a September affidavit confirmed cessation and the owner's retirement. Continued incorporation establishes no plan to reopen. No surviving damages claim, citation, penalty, substitute claimant or fee-based merits controversy is established.

### Operative ordinance terms

```text
The Ordinance states in relevant part:
      1. A person who knowingly or intentionally in a public place:
      a. engages in sexual intercourse;
      b. engages in deviate sexual intercourse as defined by the Pennsylvania Crimes Code;
      c. appears in a state of nudity, or
      d. fondles the genitals of himself, herself or another person commits Public Indecency, a Summary Offense.
      2. "Nudity” means the showing of the human male or female genital (sic), pubic area or buttocks with less than a fully opaque covering; the showing of the female breast with less than a fully opaque covering of any part of the nipple; the exposure of any device, costume, or covering which gives the appearance of or simulates the genitals, pubic hair, natal cleft, perineum anal region or pubic hair region; or the exposure of any device worn as a cover over the nipples and/or areola of the female breast, which device simulates and gives the realistic appearance of nipples and/or areola.
      3. "Public Place” includes all outdoor places owned by or open to the general public, and all buildings and enclosed places owned by or open to the general public, including such places of entertainment, taverns, restaurants, clubs, theaters, dance halls, banquet halls, party rooms or halls limited to specific members, restricted to adults or to patrons invited to attend, whether or not an admission charge is levied.
      4.The prohibition set forth in subsection 1 (c) shall not apply to:
      a. Any child under ten (10) years of age; or
      b. Any individual exposing a breast in the process of breastfeeding an infant under two (2) years of age.
      6. CONSTRUCTION AND SEVERABILITY— It is the intention of the City of Erie that the provisions of this ordinance be construed, enforced and interpreted in such a manner as will cause the least possible infringement of the constitutional rights of free speech, free expression, due process, equal protection or other fundamental rights consistent with the purposes of this ordinance. Should a court of competent jurisdiction determine that any part of this ordinance, or any application or enforcement of it is excessively restrictive of such rights or liberties, then such portion of the ordinance, or specific application of the ordinance, shall be severed from the remainder, which shall continue in full force and effect.
```

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated at argument and decision. All nine participate in the threshold decision. Once the Court reaches the merits, Scalia and Thomas participate in the merits judgment despite their threshold dissents.

## Public Action

The Court reaches the granted federal merits, 7–2, and affirms the Pennsylvania judgment, 5–4. The existing state severance remains in place.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Operative consequence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Continuing controversy and federal review | Reach the merits, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Federal merits review proceeds; the two dissenters would dismiss this review as moot and leave the state judgment undisturbed. |
| Granted nudity application | Affirm, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg | O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | The Pennsylvania judgment and its severance of sections 1(c) and 2 remain in place. |

O'Connor would reverse the federal invalidation of the granted adult-establishment application and remand the unadjudicated issues after finding that application valid under O'Brien. Kennedy and Breyer would vacate and remand for classification and, if appropriate, advancement and fit, without a final validity or insufficiency determination. Souter would vacate and remand without validation, joining that common inquiry but independently concluding that the existing demonstration is insufficient even assuming O'Brien applies. His proposed opportunity for justification remains subject to prior resolution under Pennsylvania constitutional law or properly presented federal overbreadth.

The affirmance issues no new federal severance or reinstatement order. The state court's existing excision of sections 1(c) and 2 stands; the other three conduct prohibitions are undisturbed. The Court neither inserts a theater exemption nor adjudicates the claims of absent theaters, every simulated-anatomy application, state constitutional protection or substantial overbreadth. No new hearing, study, ordinance, penalty, damages, fees or reopening of the business is ordered.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Stevens | O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Reach federal merits | ASARCO state-review rationale and its limits; six rationale votes |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Part II | Stevens | Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg | Affirm the Pennsylvania judgment | Content selection and failure of strict justification; four joins in this complete rationale |
| Lead opinion, Part III | Stevens | Thomas, Ginsburg | Independently supports affirmance | Even assuming content neutrality, the present showing fails O'Brien/Ward advancement and fit; three votes, noncontrolling |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Reach the merits and affirm | Case is live; incidental-burden treatment is unavailable when justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting; Erie fails the resulting scrutiny; no portion of the lead joined |
| Dissent from the threshold disposition | Scalia | Thomas | Would dismiss federal review as moot | The state judgment would remain; both expressly participate in the merits once review proceeds, with their different Part II and III joins shown above |
| Dissent from the merits judgment | O'Connor | No other Justice | Would reverse federal invalidity and remand unadjudicated issues | Final O'Brien validity for the granted adult-establishment application; joins Part I of the lead |
| Dissent from the merits judgment | Kennedy | Breyer throughout; Souter only in the common classification/fit inquiry and nonvalidating remand | Would vacate and remand without final validation or invalidation | Kennedy and Breyer reserve present evidentiary sufficiency; Souter does not join that reservation and states his additional ground separately; all three join Part I of the lead |
| Further dissent from the merits judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Would vacate and remand without validation | Existing evidence is insufficient even assuming O'Brien; opportunity to justify if still necessary after possible state-law or federal-overbreadth resolution |

Stevens delivers one signed lead with the three stated parts. Six Justices adopt Part I's rationale; Stone separately supplies the seventh threshold vote. Five Justices directly agree across Part II and Stone's concurrence on the precise content-selection rule and its application stated in the Holdings block. Stone does not join Part II's complete explanation, and no fifth full join or Marks inference is asserted. Part III has only three votes. Souter's distinct evidentiary conclusion and remand do not enlarge that affirmance coalition or turn its alternative into a holding. Scalia's and Thomas's conditional merits positions are operative because the Court has reached the merits; neither abstains.

## Holdings

### 1. The existing state judgment presents a live federal controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** In this state-review posture, the judgment's continuing withdrawal of the City's enforcement authority, coupled with the respondent's continuing adverse defense of that judgment, supplies a live federal controversy despite Pap's cessation of the regulated business. This rule depends on the actual legal disability and adversity; abstract governmental interest, incorporation, fees or an imagined return to business does not suffice.

**Authority:** Stevens's Part I, joined by O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six direct rationale votes. Stone-Zsela separately votes to reach the merits without joining this rationale; Scalia and Thomas dissent.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that this state-review controversy remains live because the judgment presently withdraws the City's enforcement authority and the respondent continues to defend that concrete alteration of legal rights. ASARCO permits the party first invoking federal jurisdiction to establish its own injury from the state judgment; it does not dispense with a case or controversy. Here reversal would change the City's actual legal disability, rather than answer an abstract question about desirable legislation.

Pap's closure, sale and retirement are credited. Continued incorporation is not proof of future dancing, and no manipulation, prospective reopening or surviving monetary claim supplies jurisdiction. The objection that only one party retains a business interest is substantial, but the operative state judgment and continued adverse defense preserve this particular dispute over municipal authority. Arizonans for Official English involved an expired individual federal claim and supplies no automatic answer erasing this distinct state-review posture. Laidlaw's defendant-cessation burden is not transferred to a plaintiff leaving its business. The Court recognizes no general exception for public-law litigation or fee disputes; without the actual legal disability and continuing adversity, abstract importance would be insufficient.

**Precedent treatment:** ASARCO supplies the state-review injury principle while retaining the case-or-controversy requirement. Arizonans's actual-expiration rule and Laidlaw's defendant-cessation doctrine retain their distinct predicates.

### 2. Communicative targeting cannot receive incidental-burden treatment

**Holding and operative rule:** A public-nudity ordinance cannot receive incidental-burden treatment when its justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting; Erie's record does not satisfy the scrutiny that follows. The granted application therefore cannot be sustained, while genuinely general nudity rules and evidence-based regulation of nonexpressive harms remain available.

**Authority:** Stevens, Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg in Part II, together with Stone-Zsela's express agreement in his concurrence, directly support this precise rule, its application and its stated boundary: five Justices across writings. Stone joins no portion of the lead. This is direct proposition-level agreement, not a Marks inference or five votes for every Part II sentence.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a general public-nudity label cannot give incidental-burden treatment to an application whose justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting. O'Brien requires an interest unrelated to suppressing expression before its incidental-burden analysis can sustain the restriction. The content-neutrality inquiry concerns what the government actually regulates and why, not only the breadth of its words.

Erie's defense of this application through harms attributed to the message-bearing setting does not satisfy that requirement or the scrutiny that follows. A modest clothing requirement does not by itself cure this defect. The rule leaves genuinely general nudity laws and supported regulation of crime, obstruction, sanitation, zoning or other nonexpressive harms available. It neither establishes that every adult-business effect is communicative nor resolves a different properly justified regulation. The more detailed evidentiary account and strict-scrutiny analysis in Part II have four complete joins; the independent intermediate-scrutiny alternative has three. Neither is attributed in full to the five supporters of this common rule.

**Precedent treatment:** O'Brien's nonsuppression requirement is applied rather than displaced. Existing content-neutrality doctrine continues to test operation and justification. No categorical invalidity of nudity rules, new theater exemption or independently controlling O'Brien record-insufficiency holding follows.

### Scope of the affirmance

Affirmance leaves the Pennsylvania judgment, including its existing severance of sections 1(c) and 2, intact. The common as-applied reason does not replace that state remedy with a newly narrowed federal decree or restore other applications of the severed provisions. The Court does not decide the Pennsylvania constitutional or preserved overbreadth grounds, every simulated-anatomy application or unpresented theatrical claims.

## Precedent Treatment

- **ASARCO Inc. v. Kadish, 490 U.S. 605 (1989):** Applied in Part I to concrete injury from a state judgment when the injured party first invokes federal review; the continuing case-or-controversy requirement remains. The threshold dissent distinguishes original standing from subsequent mootness.
- **Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona, March 3, 1997:** Its requirement of a continuing personal controversy remains; Part I distinguishes its expired federal claim from this existing state judgment. Its corrective treatment of a federal judgment does not authorize automatic vacatur of a state judgment.
- **Friends of the Earth, Inc. v. Laidlaw Environmental Services (TOC), Inc., January 12, 2000:** Its injury, redressability and defendant-cessation predicates remain; plaintiff closure does not automatically invoke that cessation burden or create damages here.
- **U.S. Bancorp Mortgage Co. v. Bonner Mall Partnership, 513 U.S. 18 (1994):** Its equitable approach supplies no automatic right or power to erase a state judgment upon dismissal of federal review.
- **United States v. O'Brien, 391 U.S. 367 (1968):** Its requirement that the asserted interest be unrelated to expressive suppression governs the common merits holding. Its distinct advancement-and-fit inquiry remains available for genuinely incidental regulation; Part III's adverse application has three votes only.
- **Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, June 27, 1994:** The existing distinction between actual content selection and benign purpose remains. Part II applies that principle in its fuller account; the congressional predictive-deference rule and four-Justice present-record application are not transplanted wholesale.
- **Ino Ino, Inc. v. City of Bellevue, September 28, 1998:** Its supported physical-control holding and its separate protection of visible nonobscene expression retain their actual coalitions and limits. Part II's four Associates apply the distinction between operational controls and suppression of communication; Stone's Erie concurrence does not acquire a join in Ino Ino's separate visibility holding.
- **Barnes v. Glen Theatre, Inc., 501 U.S. 560 (1991):** Its judgment and distinct rationales remain; no single rationale is made binding through unshown logical nesting. The writings here disagree over its application to this record, and no general prohibition on public nudity is invalidated.
- **City of Renton v. Playtime Theatres, Inc., 475 U.S. 41 (1986):** Relevant noncommunicative effects and reasonably relevant outside evidence remain permissible; a crime label does not itself establish fit or convert objection to a message into a noncommunicative harm.
- **Ward v. Rock Against Racism, 491 U.S. 781 (1989):** Advancement and adequate fit remain distinct from a least-restrictive-means requirement. The several intermediate-scrutiny writings preserve that distinction, without a majority adopting Part III's present insufficiency ground.
- **Edenfield v. Fane, April 26, 1993; Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, March 31, 1997; Nixon v. Shrink Missouri Government PAC, January 24, 2000:** Their actual justification and evidence principles inform the separate evidentiary positions. None establishes a universal local-study requirement, shifts the government's burden to the challenger or decides this clothing requirement's efficacy in advance.
- **Forsyth County v. Nationalist Movement, June 19, 1992:** Its audience-hostility principle remains; it does not equate every adult-business consequence with persuasion. No precedent is overruled.

## Law After Decision

Effective March 29, 2000, the state-review threshold rule rests on six rationale votes. The merits establish the five-Justice rule against incidental-burden treatment when a public-nudity ordinance's justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting, with Erie failing the resulting scrutiny. The judgment leaves Pennsylvania's existing severance of sections 1(c) and 2 intact and the three other public-indecency prohibitions undisturbed.

There is no controlling independent finding that this record fails intermediate scrutiny, no blanket invalidation of general public-nudity laws, no constitutional entitlement to every form of adult entertainment and no new evidentiary or hearing duty. The full Part II account has four joins; Part III has three. Existing rules for genuinely general conduct regulation and supported control of nonexpressive harms remain. State constitutional law, substantial overbreadth, other simulated-anatomy applications and the claims of absent theatrical producers are not adjudicated.

## Separate Writings

**Justice Stevens's Part II, joined by Justices Scalia, Thomas and Ginsburg: the fuller content-selection rationale.** These four Justices find communicative selection from the City's repeated hierarchy of protected expression, its message-control explanation, the intended Kandyland application and the mixed Council record. The agreed Equus episode corroborates that combined inference but proves neither its own motive nor a comprehensive enforcement policy. Counsel's account is attributable governmental interpretation, not an enacted exception or authoritative judicial construction. The deleted artistic-merit characterization remains deleted.

Turner's operation-based classification and Ino Ino's distinction between supported physical controls and suppression of protected anatomy answer the general-text defense. Barnes does not remove the need to identify communicative targeting, and the reservation for general nudity rules remains. The alternative account—constitutional accommodation of theatrical settings with different noncommunicative effects—is substantial, but these Justices reject it as an adequate explanation of the combined municipal account here. The restriction therefore requires content-based scrutiny; the asserted covering means lacks the required narrow fit. They do not equate every secondary effect with persuasion or adjudicate a nonparty theater's claim. Stone agrees with the common operative rule and result but does not join this complete account.

**Justice Stevens's Part III, joined by Justices Thomas and Ginsburg: an independent intermediate-scrutiny alternative.** Even assuming the application is content neutral, these three Justices would affirm because the submitted record does not establish material advancement of the asserted harms through the covering requirement. O'Brien and Ward require a supported relation between means and harm; Edenfield, the actual Turner decisions, Ino Ino and Nixon illustrate the need to evaluate that relation rather than accept a label. The absence of a new local study alone is not the defect, and relevant outside experience remains available under Renton.

They confront Barnes's judgment, the general text, the small retained expressive burden and the constitutional-accommodation account. The combined assertions still do not establish why this alteration of the performance materially reduces the asserted harms. They require neither a completed crime, a formal administrative record, numerical certainty nor the least restrictive conceivable regulation. This is an independently sufficient ground for their affirmance, but has only three votes and is not a holding of the Court. Scalia does not join it; his doubt about efficacy supplies no unexpressed intermediate ground. Souter's different insufficiency conclusion supports a remand opportunity and does not add a join here.

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in the judgment.** The case is live. A public-nudity ordinance cannot receive incidental-burden treatment when its justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting, and Erie's record does not satisfy the scrutiny that follows. Government cannot invoke incidental regulation while defending the law through harms attributed to the message-bearing setting. He therefore affirms and leaves the state judgment invalidating enforcement against Pap's in place, without a separate remedial instruction.

His reasoning preserves genuinely general nudity rules and evidence-based regulation of crime, obstruction, sanitation, zoning or other nonexpressive harms. He joins no portion of the lead. His concurrence supplies direct support for the common content-selection proposition, but neither adopts the lead's complete threshold and evidentiary accounts nor adds an independent O'Brien insufficiency ground. His as-applied reasoning does not order new severance or reinstate applications of the provisions the state court severed.

**Justice Scalia, dissenting from the threshold disposition, joined by Justice Thomas.** Pap's complete departure from the regulated business ends the prospective controversy. ASARCO's original-standing problem differs from later mootness: the City's adverse judgment cannot alone supply the missing opposing personal stake. Arizonans requires a real continuing dispute, and Laidlaw's defendant-cessation rule does not automatically govern this plaintiff's closure. Continued incorporation, fees, retirement or an imagined future business does not cure that defect.

They would dismiss federal review, leaving the state judgment undisturbed. Article III does not require the state judiciary to dismiss its own proceeding; federal supervisory vacatur and Bancorp supply no automatic erasure of the state decree. Once the Court rejects their objection and reaches the merits, both expressly participate and affirm. Both join Part II's finding that this actual application selects communication, which defeats the general-conduct and absence-of-targeting defenses. Only Thomas joins Part III's independent O'Brien alternative. Scalia retains a genuine general-law category and does not turn doubt about covering's efficacy into a second merits ground.

**Justice O'Connor, dissenting from the merits judgment.** She joins Part I but would reverse the federal invalidation of the granted adult-establishment application under O'Brien and remand the unadjudicated matters. The general public-conduct scope, identified noncommunicative adult-establishment harms, reasonably relevant Barnes/Renton inference and limited retained expressive burden together justify this application. Ino Ino and Turner still require actual scrutiny, and Nixon requires affirmative justification; a preamble, small burden or lack of contrary proof would not suffice alone.

She reads the City's theater account as constitutional accommodation in contexts potentially lacking the relevant adult-establishment effects, rather than sufficient proof that the Kandyland application selects art by taste. She acknowledges the agreed nonenforcement, repeated subjective interpretations, message-control argument and thin local record. She nevertheless accepts the bounded analogy and preventive inference, without finding a Kandyland crime, adopting a binding theater exemption or making morality alone sufficient. Her proposed remand follows final federal validation within the grant; it is not the nonvalidating remand of Kennedy, Souter and Breyer. Pennsylvania constitutional protection, substantial overbreadth and separate simulated-anatomy applications remain open.

**Justice Kennedy, dissenting from the merits judgment, joined by Justice Breyer; Justice Souter joins only the common classification/fit inquiry and nonvalidating remand.** They would vacate and remand to decide whether the City's actual account selects expression by perceived worth or rests on relevant differences in noncommunicative effects. An artistic hierarchy cannot supply a nonsuppressive justification, but neither general wording nor the single Equus episode conclusively decides classification. If the justification is content based, strict scrutiny applies; if independent of expression, O'Brien and Ward require real advancement and fit without least-restrictive-means review.

Ino Ino's actual operational analysis, Edenfield and Turner require connecting the specific harm, means and contrary evidence. Nixon's actual evidentiary treatment does not permit the City's repeated hierarchy and message-control account to disappear behind generic deference. The state majority's categorical equation of effects with persuasion is too broad, while immediate validation leaves the City's actual interpretation unanswered. Kennedy and Breyer also reject a final conclusion that this application is necessarily content selective. They reserve whether the existing record satisfies intermediate scrutiny; Souter does not join that reservation.

The proposed remand would not compel a new local study, hearing or rewritten ordinance. The state court could use the submitted record or resolve preserved state constitutional and federal-overbreadth issues. Common experience and relevant outside evidence remain available; no completed offense, precise numerical proof or inevitable business closure is required. Kennedy and Breyer's possible later outcomes are not present alternative judgment votes.

**Justice Souter, further dissenting from the merits judgment.** In addition to joining the common classification inquiry and nonvalidating remand, he concludes that the existing demonstration is insufficient even assuming O'Brien applies. Erie must establish a germane basis for both the asserted harms and their alleviation by this restriction if that federal justification remains decisive. He reconsiders the evidentiary leniency of his Barnes inference rather than treating citation to Barnes as proof. Relevant outside evidence and current justification may suffice; the amount of proof varies where fit is obvious, but covering's efficacy is not self-evident here.

Edenfield, Turner, Ino Ino and Nixon reinforce independent examination of advancement and fit. Renton permits relevant outside experience without repeated local studies. Reliance must be real and pertinent; an opinion accepting evidence for a similar regulation can supply a relevant basis. The existing zoning alternative and its nonenforcement require an answer, not a command to use zoning. No formal administrative record, completed crime or numerical certainty is demanded.

He leaves open a genuine nonsuppressive distinction between venues and would permit the City an opportunity to justify it. First, however, the state court may resolve Pennsylvania constitutional law or properly presented federal overbreadth, making further evidentiary development unnecessary. Thus he neither orders an unconditional new study or hearing nor promises eventual validation. Kennedy and Breyer join the common inquiry and remand but not his independent conclusion that the present showing is insufficient. His remedy also differs from Part III's final affirmance.

## Procedure After Action

The Supreme Court's review is complete. The Pennsylvania judgment is affirmed, leaving its existing severance of sections 1(c) and 2 in force. The other conduct prohibitions remain undisturbed. No additional federal remedial proceeding, evidentiary reopening, local study or ordinance revision is ordered. The Court does not decide the preserved Pennsylvania constitutional or substantial-overbreadth issues, confer immunity from independent valid laws, impose a penalty, award damages or fees, or determine a claim belonging to an absent theater.

## Source Notes

The [Pennsylvania Supreme Court opinion, 719 A.2d 273](https://static.case.law/a2d/719/html/0273-02.html) supplies the quoted ordinance, the federal majority grounds, existing state severance and separately unadjudicated claims. The [joint appendix for No. 98-1161](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0258) supplies the agreed facts, Council record and October and December 1994 hearing material, including the City's theatrical interpretation and the agreed Equus nonenforcement fact. The agreed episode excludes the proposed artistic-merit characterization; the City's legal interpretation is not an enacted exception or an authoritative judicial construction.

The differing effective-date accounts and early restraint do not establish that enforcement was available throughout the production's run. The Court determines no penalty provision or completed offense. The clothing agreement states what the ordinance required, not actual compliance; the liquor-premises comparison was deleted. Refusal of an erotic-message stipulation is not waiver of expression. Anticipated theatrical chill does not establish a canceled production or a claim by a theater party. Admission of pleadings for statements and admissions does not prove every allegation.

---

# Frozen neutral packet — Mitchell v. Helms

Fresh neutral validation completed November 3, 2026. This packet supplies no modeled vote, assignment, Stone position or target historical Supreme Court outcome. It corrects the generic/stale framing of the approved runtime Section I without changing that input. Exact saved substantive handoffs follow.

The nine seated Justices are Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No case-specific nonparticipation is identified. Independent modeling concerns the eight Associates. The law cutoff is immediately before June 28, 2000. The four uncoordinated June 28 cases share entering law and do not govern one another. No unfinished June 26 position is law. If a completed earlier event adds materially relevant law before modeling, provide a sanitized update; the specifically identified Jones/Dale dependency does not predetermine another case.

## Independent framing, qualifications and readiness

The question concerns the federal Chapter 2 equipment/materials program, considered June 28, 2000 after December 1, 1999 argument, on review of the Fifth Circuit. The lower Part III disposition is categorical as to equipment and library-book loans to sectarian schools in Jefferson Parish; it is not merely a quantified judgment about a proved devotional item. Textbooks and the separate programs within the larger litigation retain their actual scope. The federal grant does not automatically bring every Louisiana program or unrelated docket before the Court.

The exact approved operational premise is copied unchanged below. It establishes actual financed religious instruction or demonstrated religious use under recipient control for this application. It is not inferred from religious identity, classroom control, fungibility or savings; it is not a recovered historical finding, and supplies no specific school, number, incident, nationwide prevalence or chosen remedy. A model may not reduce it to a mere hypothetical risk or enlarge it to all materials at all schools. Its constitutional significance, proper application and remedy remain for independent adjudication.

Public title, public administration, secular/neutral/nonideological services, independent providers and supplement-not-supplant requirements are statutory commands. The described classroom control, actual monitoring practices, recalled titles and assurances are separate facts or judicial recitations. Neither nominal public title nor neutral eligibility conclusively establishes lawful use. Conversely, a religious provider and a possible institutional saving alone cannot establish a forbidden financed function under the current law.

Agostini's unified Lemon inquiry and the actual Jackson and Strout funded-function holdings govern. The independent old Lemon prongs do not return. The exact relationship and controls in Rosenberger, Southworth and safeguarded secular instruction retain their lawful protection; a label of private expression does not alone resolve what this expenditure buys. The Court must address both the categorical premise below and the adopted actual-use premise. Rejecting a categorical equipment rule does not approve every administered use, while identifying a genuine defect does not justify exclusion of every religious provider or independently lawful loan.

Competing paths include confined invalidity/remedial correction under the actual funded-function rule, or a narrower disposition correcting the categorical lower rationale while preserving adjudication of the established financed-use application. A departure from binding doctrine requires the ordinary precedent analysis rather than recharacterizing the adopted fact. No route is selected. Relief must explain treatment of the actual lower declaration, any genuinely dependent state provision and lawful severable applications. No new administrative audit system, school closure, across-the-board exclusion, recoupment or damages is presumed.

Ready for independent modeling. Source-specific quantity, particular financed activities beyond the premise, exact reach of a distinct state program and the final remedial implementation are not supplied by inference. If one becomes essential to a proposed broader remedy, obtain that source or confine the holding/remand to established scope. No nonexistent simulated filing is required to re-prove the approved premise. The copied prior public writings preserve the different Agostini, Jackson and Strout grounds of each Associate, including Breyer's limited Agostini joins; they do not generate a present commitment.

## Validated objective source extraction

## Mitchell v. Helms - No. 98-1648

**Event, question and adopted premise.** Merits consideration June 28, 2000; argued December 1, 1999. Certiorari to the Fifth Circuit. The parties' question concerns federal Chapter 2's publicly purchased, loaned instructional materials and equipment for students in public and private schools, with allocations independent of religious identity. The approved neutral packet separately adopts an operational premise that the program as administered actually finances religious instruction or demonstrated religious use under recipient control. Its exact text is copied in the source extracts. That premise is neither an inference from affiliation/fungibility nor a recovered historical finding; it supplies no school, quantity, incident, nationwide prevalence or remedial scope beyond its words.

**Statute and operation.** The lower opinion reproduces 20 U.S.C. sections 7351(b)(2), 7372(a)(1), 7371(b), 7372(c)(1)-(2) and distribution provisions. Chapter 2 provides block grants, including materials, library resources, computer hardware/software and equipment. Private-school services/materials must be secular, neutral and nonideological; federal aid must supplement rather than supplant nonfederal funds; a public agency retains control of funds and title to property and administers them. Service providers must be public agencies or independent contractors. Public title and administration are statutory requirements; whether particular classroom operation complies and what government finances are separate inquiries.

The lower court described local requests, review of book titles, equipment loans, state/local monitoring, secular-use assurances and the absence of direct cash payments to nonpublic schools. It reported changes in monitoring after a federal review and a recall of approximately 191 book titles found inconsistent with Chapter 2 guidelines. These descriptions do not establish that all materials were devotional, that all safeguards were ineffective, or that the adopted premise is limited to those recalled titles. They also do not erase the adopted actual-use premise.

**Judgment and reasons below.** Helms v. Picard, 151 F.3d 347 (5th Cir. Aug. 17, 1998), Part III, reversed the district court's summary judgment for defendants and rendered a declaration that federal Chapter 2 and its Louisiana counterpart were unconstitutional as applied in Jefferson Parish insofar as they permitted educational/instructional equipment loans to sectarian schools, including library books. The court regarded Meek and Wolman as directly controlling and rejected the argument that later cases had implicitly displaced their equipment/textbook distinction. It preserved the textbook category and did not rest this categorical disposition on a particular quantified finding of actual devotional use. The current Court must evaluate the stated lower ground against actual simulated Agostini, Jackson and Strout; a changed operational premise does not silently narrow the lower decree.

The litigation also included special education, transportation and a Louisiana state program. Those distinct programs and their dispositions cannot all be imported into No. 98-1648 merely because they share the Fifth Circuit opinion. The federal Chapter 2 question is the supplied grant; identify any genuinely dependent state provision before directing relief beyond it. A separate historical Supreme Court action in another docket is not part of this event.

**Competing arguments.** Petitioners and the Secretary of Education support neutral access to secular educational resources. Public title, secular-content restrictions, neutral per-student allocations and controls can preserve a lawful benefit; religious location, institutional savings and potential divertibility alone cannot establish religious sponsorship. They challenge categorical reliance on Meek/Wolman and preserve genuinely segregated secular services and independent private benefits. Under the adopted premise, they must address the actual financed use rather than simply deny that fact; the scope of violations and appropriate severable remedy remain available arguments.

Helms and the plaintiff taxpayers argue that equipment controlled and used by a religious school's teachers for its own integrated instruction differs from the separately supervised public service in Agostini or the independently edited printing benefit in Rosenberger. Actual religious instructional financing is not cured by neutral eligibility, public title or routing. Their historical merits brief also relies on potential diversion and institutional support; those broader theories must be tested against the actual current rule rather than accepted as controlling. No requirement that every religious school be excluded follows merely from proving a defective use.

**Entering-law forks and remedy.** Agostini's updated Lemon inquiry asks what governmental act and function are performed, not whether every act of a recipient is government speech. It permits identifiable secular work with enforceable controls and rejects religious-location and incidental-benefit presumptions. Jackson establishes that genuine parental choice does not itself cure demonstrated financing of integrated devotional instruction; Strout applies the funded-function distinction in tuition's actual public-substitute setting. Rosenberger remains an actual controlled financing benefit for independently edited private expression, not a general declaration that all public payments are forums. Southworth's independent university-expression program and Santa Fe's official religious-choice mechanism retain their distinct applications.

Apply the adopted premise, then identify the actual scope of relief it supports. An as-administered violation may require correction while independently lawful loans remain; reversal of an overbroad categorical rationale does not establish that every administered use is constitutional. Conversely, actual religious financing cannot be treated as only a risk merely because the historical record is disputed. Vacatur and a targeted remedial remand, narrower affirmance if the lawful reviewed scope permits it, or another disposition must explain both the legal ground and treatment of the actual lower declaration. No new audit regime, all-provider exclusion, recoupment or school closure is assumed.

**Justice-specific sources.** O'Connor's Jackson opinion and Stevens/Souter/Ginsburg/Breyer joins adopt the actual funded-function rule and confined remedial inquiry; their earlier Agostini positions are separately preserved. Kennedy's Jackson dissent, joined by Scalia and Thomas, treats the genuine child-selected benefit as important while denying that neutrality or a signature alone is universally sufficient. The three later joined Kennedy's Strout dissent distinguishing capped ordinary tuition from provider operating/debt costs. Breyer did not join Souter's affirmative direct-aid objection in Agostini, although he joined its precedent and procedural objections. Souter and Ginsburg authored those separate Agostini grounds. These distinctions, not a generic church/state grouping, support individual analysis. Current Santa Fe positions are copied for attribution/official-function continuity.

**Sources and limits.** [Fifth Circuit opinion](https://static.case.law/f3d/151/html/0347-01.html); [IA record, No. 98-1648](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0270), especially petition and appendix, petitioners' brief, Secretary of Education brief, plaintiffs' brief and joint appendix. The full Part III was read, with selected briefing passages. The adopted premise resolves the actual-use fact for this application but does not resolve source-specific quantity or remedy. Do not require nonexistent simulated findings or treat unavailable historical detail as litigant default.

## Mitchell - petitioners merits question

Source: [mitchell_08.txt](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0270).

QUESTION PRESENTED 


Whether a program under Chapter 2 of Title I of the 
Elementary and Secondary Education Act of 1965, 20 U.S.C. 
§ 7301 et seq., which provides federal funds to state and local 
educational agencies to purchase and lend secular, neutral, and 
nonideological materials such as computers, software, and library 
books to public and nonpublic schools for use by the students 
attending those schools, and which allocates the funds on an equal 
per-student basis, regardless of the religious or secular character 
of the schools the students choose to attend, violates the 
Establishment Clause of the First Amendment. 


il

## Mitchell - statutory terms reproduced in Part III.A

Source: [mitchell_lower.txt](https://static.case.law/f3d/151/html/0347-01.html).

Chapter 2 provides financial assistance through “block grants” to state and local educational agencies to implement eight “innovative assistance programs.” See 20 U.S.C. §§ 7311(b); 7312(a),(c)(1); 7351. The challenged innovative assistance program describes
    programs for the acquisition and use of instructional and educational materials, including library services and materials (including media materials), assessments, reference materials, computer software and hardware for instructional use, and other curricular materials which are tied to high academic standards and which will be used to improve student achievement and which are part of an overall education reform program.
    20 U.S.C. § 7351(b)(2).
    Chapter 2 services are to be provided to children enrolled in both “public and private, nonprofit schools.” 20 U.S.C. § 7312. Section 7372 provides that a.local educational agency shall equitably provide “secular, neutral, and nonideological services, materials, and equipment” to students who are enrolled in private nonprofit elementary and secondary schools within its boundaries. 20 U.S.C. § 7372(a)(1). Chapter 2 funds for the innovative assistance programs must supplement, and in no case supplant, “funds from non-Federal sources.” 20 U.S.C. § 7371(b). The control of Chapter 2 funds, as well as title to all Chapter 2 “materials, equipment, and property,” must be in a public agency, “and a public agency shall administer such funds and property.” 20 U.S.C. § 7372(c)(1). In addition, any services provided for the benefit of private school students must be provided by a public agency or through a contractor who is “independent of such private school and of any religious organizations.” 20 U.S.C. § 7372(c)(2).

## Chronology and reading scope

Cutoff: immediately before June 28, 2000. All four June 28 matters use the same entering-law baseline. The June 26 Jones decision must be added after its completed public projection is available; no pending position or historical result supplies it. This copy contains no target-case outcome or private position. Authority remains in the cited trackers and completed events.

## Standards and Tests selection: Lemon test

### Lemon test

**Governing question:** How the Lemon test governs Establishment Clause claims.

**Current rule:** Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test, modified so that purpose, effects and entanglement are evidence within one inquiry: a specific prohibition or distinct doctrine controls when it resolves the conduct; an established permissible relationship defeats the claim on that ground; otherwise a legally sufficient unresolved allegation is decided through attribution and function under ordinary procedure. These are three routes within the updated Lemon test, not freestanding prongs, exemptions from review or an alternative test for uncertain cases. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method. Neutral eligibility, parental selection and restrictive endorsement do not cure public payment for demonstrated devotional instruction integrated into the financed educational work without reliable separation. The inquiry concerns the instructional function purchased; religious identity alone does not establish the defect, and genuinely independent private benefits, private forum access and reliably separated secular services remain distinct.

**Authority by component:**

- General inquiry and operative qualifications: Agostini v. Felton — I. Lemon governs through one inquiry into governmental religious authority and function, 1997-06-23. Part I of the Opinion of the Court; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join this modification in full, supplying five direct joins at this level of generality.
- Object of the inquiry, evidence role and Holding I limits: Agostini v. Felton — I. Lemon governs through one inquiry into governmental religious authority and function (controlling explanation and precedent treatment), 1997-06-23; the same five Justices.
- Safeguarded secular instruction: Agostini v. Felton — II. Separately administered secular instruction is not forbidden because of its religious-school location, 1997-06-23. Part II of the Court's opinion; the five-Justice coalition stated above directly joins the rule, its secular-control qualifications and its precise treatment of precedent.

- Jackson v. Benson — Canonical Decision Record, Holdings, “Parental routing does not justify the demonstrated purchase of integrated devotional instruction”, 1999-03-16; O’Connor Part I with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Present operation effective:** Jackson v. Benson, March 16, 1999, for integrated provider-cost instructional payments; Agostini’s general inquiry retains its June 23, 1997 effective date.

**Object of the inquiry:** Lemon addresses governmental sponsorship of religion, financing of religious functions and the improper union of civil and religious authority. Its method must identify those acts rather than condemn an independently secular service because officials supervise it to remain secular. Purpose and effects help identify the governmental function; institutional relationships show who directs it.

**Operative qualifications:** The court identifies the governmental act—an official message, organized event, exercise of supervisory power or expenditure—and the function it performs. For financing, the question is what government purchases or performs; all religious conduct of the recipient need not become government speech. Private-access authorization, religious identity and physical location are relevant in context but are not individually decisive.

Government may not organize worship as its own activity, prescribe religious profession or observance, adopt doctrine as governmental truth, solicit religious adherence through public authority, govern theology or confer civil authority on a religious basis. Denominational classifications retain their distinct strict-scrutiny rule. Independently identifiable secular work in a religious setting may be purchased with enforceable controls against devotional use and diversion. Restoration of private exercise restricted by custody or comparable governmental control remains subject to the applicable accommodation inquiry, not an automatic financing prohibition. Those distinct doctrines retain their own sources and burdens.

**Safeguarded secular instruction on religious-school premises:** The Establishment Clause permits the described supplementary Title I service for children independently selected under neutral public eligibility criteria, delivered by public employees under public supervision with public secular materials, without religious instruction, team teaching or other cooperative instructional activity with religious-school teachers, or participation in worship, and with effective controls against diversion. Religious-school location, presumed inculcation arising from that location, incidental benefit to the school, and ordinary monitoring of that separately administered public service do not by themselves establish unconstitutional sponsorship, devotional financing or institutional control; Aguilar's categorical prohibition and Ball's corresponding Shared Time rationale are displaced at that scope.

**Exceptions and limits:** Once prohibited conduct is established, actual conversion, religious success or a completed penalty is unnecessary. A secular objective cannot justify prohibited religious means. Conversely, a provider's religious motivation does not make its secular work devotional. Ordinary monitoring of secular performance is not itself establishment, even when repeated; civil control of doctrine and religious control of public authority remain forbidden.

This method requires real operational separation and reliable accountability for funded work, but not invariably separate institutional governance or wholly distinct personnel. Shared structures cannot excuse devotional financing, and they do not automatically defeat a genuinely severable public service. An institutional-support objection warrants scrutiny of the service, not a presumption that its secular and religious functions are inseparable. Neutral eligibility or public employment alone does not excuse devotional instruction or diversion; independent prohibitions on governmental worship, devotional financing and improper institutional control remain.

**Former-rule note:** Lemon's independent purpose, primary-effect and excessive-entanglement prongs ceased to govern as separate or fallback tests on June 23, 1997 (Agostini v. Felton, Holding I); those considerations now serve as evidence within the updated Lemon test. Aguilar v. Felton is overruled as authority for categorically excluding this safeguarded public Title I service from religious-school premises (Agostini v. Felton, Holding II, June 23, 1997); no reopening of completed claims or authorization of religious instruction follows. School District of Grand Rapids v. Ball loses its contrary Shared Time rationale at the same scope; its Community Education judgment and unaddressed grounds remain undisturbed.

**Open question:** Jackson resolves only the demonstrated provider-cost arrangement purchasing integrated devotional instruction; it adopts no universal voucher prohibition or exclusion of religious providers and no constitutional entitlement to subsidy. Other voucher arrangements, monuments, legislative prayer, general accommodation and other aid retain their actual governing rules. Witters and Mueller remain effective for their actual private-benefit relationships.

**Preserved specific holdings:** Existing specific holdings continue to govern their established relationships: Kiryas Joel restricts denominational allocation of civil power; Rosenberger permits its controlled publication benefit; Pinette and Chabad distinguish private expression and governmental attribution. Zobrest supplies no Establishment Clause merits holding.

**Related current rules:** Denominational selection for general governmental authority; Private religious viewpoints in public forums and printing benefits. Each states existing specific holdings that continue to govern their established relationships under this test.

**Holdings navigation:** Agostini v. Felton (1997-06-23) — I. Lemon governs through one inquiry into governmental religious authority and function; II. Separately administered secular instruction is not forbidden because of its religious-school location. [Jackson v. Benson — Parental routing does not justify the demonstrated purchase of integrated devotional instruction](HOLDINGS.md#parental-routing-does-not-justify-the-demonstrated-purchase-of-integrated-devotional-instruction-1).

## Standards and Tests selection: Private religious viewpoints in public forums and printing benefits

### Private religious viewpoints in public forums and printing benefits

**Governing question:** Viewpoint-neutral access for private religious expression and the distinct establishment inquiry in the forums and benefits actually opened.

**Current rule:** When a public school admits private family-life and child-rearing discussion to an after-hours forum, it cannot exclude an otherwise eligible presentation solely for its religious viewpoint, even assuming a nonpublic forum. Neutral restrictions outside the forum's actual subjects remain possible. Equal access to voluntary, publicly accessible after-hours films without school sponsorship does not establish religion. That establishment holding concerns private speech, not an official devotional program; it supplies no replacement for the inherited establishment framework or universal ruling on worship restrictions. A State may not categorically exclude an otherwise eligible private unattended religious display from a forum actually opened to comparable expression merely because its message is religious or its location adjoins or lies within a capitol. Pinette’s access holding includes the proposal’s offered private-attribution safeguard; it establishes neither adequacy of a later sign nor a comprehensive establishment test. Chabad separately holds that an interior capitol location does not establish religion when actual context makes the expression clearly private. Actual selection, financing, control, adoption, preference or obscured private sponsorship can alter that conclusion; no forum label or unspecified sign decides attribution. In a generally available, privately edited student-publication program, a religious editorial viewpoint cannot exclude an otherwise eligible publication from printing support. Its secular expression purpose, broad neutral availability, independent attribution and controlled vendor payments together permit that benefit; neutrality alone does not authorize devotional financing or governmental worship.

In a broad after-hours educational and community forum, an otherwise comparable private meeting cannot be excluded solely for its worship form; exact secular sacramental counterparts are not required. Genuine reasonable, viewpoint-neutral operational and activity limits remain available. An admitted instructional subject likewise cannot exclude comparable religious instruction merely while permitting religious discussion. Independent private worship in rented school space does not by itself establish religion; actual governmental adoption must be shown rather than inferred from religious identity or location.

**Authority by component:**

- A religious perspective cannot be excluded from an admitted subject: Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District — A religious perspective cannot be excluded from an admitted subject, 1993-06-07. White’s speech portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.
- Equal private access does not establish religion here: Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District — Equal private access does not establish religion here, 1993-06-07. White’s establishment portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

- 2. Ohio’s categorical Establishment Clause defense cannot sustain this exclusion: 2. Ohio’s categorical Establishment Clause defense cannot sustain this exclusion, 1995-06-29. Stone, Parts II–III, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer: seven votes for this bounded speech and access holding. Those seven do not adopt one comprehensive Establishment Clause test.
- 2. Georgia’s categorical defense cannot sustain summary judgment excluding the private religious display: 2. Georgia’s categorical defense cannot sustain summary judgment excluding the private religious display, 1995-06-29. Souter, Part II, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: eight votes for this bounded judgment and speech holding; Stevens dissents from its application.
- 3. Interior location does not itself make clearly private expression governmental religion: 3. Interior location does not itself make clearly private expression governmental religion, 1995-06-29. Souter, Part III, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: five votes for this contextual rule. The additional decree-specific inquiry proposed in Part IV has only O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; it is not a five-Justice mandate.
- Religious viewpoint in the private publication program: Religious viewpoint in the private publication program, 1995-06-29. Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas; five Justices adopt the program-specific viewpoint rule.
- Permissible printing support on the actual program terms: Permissible printing support on the actual program terms, 1995-06-29. Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas join this concrete program conclusion. Lemon remains governing law; no new universal framework is adopted.

- Private-worship attribution, religious instruction and otherwise comparable worship access in a broad community forum: Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10 — Holdings 1–3, 1998-05-11.

**Present operation effective:** Bronx Household of Faith, 1998-05-11, for the bounded private-worship access rule and attribution clarification. Earlier components retain their own dates: June 29, 1995: Pinette’s bounded access holding, Chabad’s expressly subsequent contextual interior-forum holding, and Rosenberger’s independent same-day publication-benefit holding. Lamb’s Chapel retains its original school-forum components. Agostini v. Felton, June 23, 1997, for the operation of these establishment components within the Lemon test as updated in Agostini's Holding I.

**Holdings navigation:** Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District, 508 U.S. 384 (1993); 2. Ohio’s categorical Establishment Clause defense cannot sustain this exclusion; 2. Georgia’s categorical defense cannot sustain summary judgment excluding the private religious display; 3. Interior location does not itself make clearly private expression governmental religion; Religious viewpoint in the private publication program; Permissible printing support on the actual program terms; Agostini v. Felton (1997-06-23) — I. Lemon governs through one inquiry into governmental religious authority and function. Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10 — Private worship in a broad school community forum.

**Exceptions and limits:** No universal disclaimer duty, unconditional right to unattended displays, obligation to fund every religious activity, duty to keep a forum open or automatic immunity for nominal private sponsorship is created. Neutral identification rules may be considered; they are not ordered. The four-Justice additional attribution inquiry in Chabad is not a majority mandate. Pinette supplies no universal forum immunity or automatic attribution from location. Rosenberger's controlled vendor-funded printing benefit concerns financing, not merely facility access; religious editorial viewpoint there is not automatically purchased devotional instruction, and Rosenberger did not authorize every devotional expenditure.

**Material development:** Lamb's Chapel decided its establishment component under Lemon's then-independent purpose, primary-effect and entanglement prongs; effective June 23, 1997, those considerations serve as evidence within the Lemon test as updated in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I, not as separate or fallback prongs.

**Related current rules:** Lemon test states the governing Establishment Clause inquiry; under it Rosenberger's controlled publication benefit and Pinette's and Chabad's distinction between private expression and governmental attribution continue to govern their established relationships.

## Mitchell - controlling prior holdings


Source: `state/holdings/first-amendment.md`. Copied sections.


#### Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-329.
**Decided:** June 29, 1995.

##### Religious viewpoint in the private publication program

**Holding:** The University may not exclude an otherwise eligible private student publication from its generally available printing benefit because its editorial viewpoint is religious. The rule applies to this privately edited, independently attributed publication program on its otherwise lawful eligibility, payment and administrative terms; it does not require funding every religious activity or every subject.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas; five Justices adopt the program-specific viewpoint rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Recognition, facilities, distribution and money remain distinct; not every public expenditure creates a speech forum. The publication remains independently edited and attributed under required disclaimers and approved vendor-payment terms.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Perry Education Association v. Perry Local Educators’ Association: preserves limited-forum definition while forbidding viewpoint discrimination.
- Lamb’s Chapel v. Center Moriches; Widmar v. Vincent: apply private religious-viewpoint equality without assuming that facility access decides every subsidy.
- Regan v. Taxation With Representation: distinguishes definition of a government financing program from discriminatory exclusion within this opened private-publication arrangement.

##### Permissible printing support on the actual program terms

**Holding:** Providing this neutral printing benefit to the otherwise eligible private religious publication does not itself violate the Establishment Clause. Its secular student-expression purpose, broad neutral availability, independent editorial attribution and controlled vendor payments matter together; neutrality alone is not a universal authorization of devotional financing or governmental sponsorship of worship.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas join this concrete program conclusion. Lemon remains governing law; no new universal framework is adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The actual purpose, effect and entanglement inquiry remains; neutral availability alone neither authorizes unrestricted devotional financing nor governmental worship. Compelled-fee challenges, unrestricted religious aid and particular awards are outside review.

The denial concerned religious activity, defined by the Guidelines as primarily promoting or manifesting belief in or about deity or ultimate reality, rather than an established finding of religious-organization ineligibility. The organization rule separately concerns devotion as organizational purpose. Political restrictions separately exclude principal-purpose lobbying and candidate electioneering while permitting incidental or insubstantial advocacy, nonpartisan study/research and ideological views; those permissions are not exceptions to the religious-activity rule. Permitted ability-to-perform membership limits likewise remain distinct. Abandoned free-exercise/state individual-rights claims and unchallenged equal-protection intent findings are not reopened.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Lemon v. Kurtzman: retains the existing purpose, effect and entanglement inquiry; no universal replacement is adopted.
- Witters v. Washington Department of Services for the Blind; Mueller v. Allen: apply neutral-benefit and private-direction distinctions within the actual program, not neutrality alone.
- Board of Education of Kiryas Joel v. Grumet: distinguishes denominational governmental authority and retains religious-neutrality limits.
- Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills: preserves the actual antecedent-law remand; supplies no religious-aid merits holding here.
- Lee v. Weisman: preserves limits on governmental religious participation and coercion; this private printing benefit compels no student worship.
- Church of Scientology v. Clearwater: distinguishes RFRA burdens and exemptions from constitutional financing authority.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for removal of the religious-viewpoint exclusion under otherwise lawful eligibility, payment and administrative terms. Examine any properly available independent Virginia funding defense consistently with the federal viewpoint holding, without declaring applicability, validity or revival of forfeited defenses. No full payment, damages, immunity, fee or compelled-fee refund is determined.

**Current-force modification:** Effective June 23, 1997, Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test as modified in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform a single inquiry with three routes: controlling specific prohibition or distinct doctrine, established permissible relationship, and residual attribution and function. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method. Rosenberger's actual controlled vendor-funded printing benefit remains permissible on its program terms. It concerns financing, not merely facility access; it neither decided Agostini's public-teaching case nor authorized every devotional expenditure.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553, June 23, 1997, Holdings I–II; First Amendment — Public instruction on religious-school premises.




#### Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 96-552 and 96-553.
**Decided:** June 23, 1997.

**Material judgment:** Both judgments vacated and remanded, 5–4, for tailored prospective Rule 60(b)(5) relief; Lemon modified as the governing test; Aguilar's categorical rule overruled and Ball's corresponding Shared Time rationale displaced at the stated scope.

**Proposition-level authority:** All four holdings are delivered by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela in the Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Each has five direct joins at its stated level of generality, including the modification of Lemon in Holding I. No aggregation of separate opinions supplies controlling authority.

##### I. Lemon governs through one inquiry into governmental religious authority and function

**Holding:** Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test, modified so that purpose, effects and entanglement are evidence within one inquiry: a specific prohibition or distinct doctrine controls when it resolves the conduct; an established permissible relationship defeats the claim on that ground; otherwise a legally sufficient unresolved allegation is decided through attribution and function under ordinary procedure. These are three routes within the updated Lemon test, not freestanding prongs, exemptions from review or an alternative test for uncertain cases.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join this modification in full, supplying five direct joins at this level of generality.

**Operative qualifications:** The court identifies the governmental act—an official message, organized event, exercise of supervisory power or expenditure—and the function it performs. For financing, the question is what government purchases or performs; all religious conduct of the recipient need not become government speech. Private-access authorization, religious identity and physical location are relevant in context but are not individually decisive.

Government may not organize worship as its own activity, prescribe religious profession or observance, adopt doctrine as governmental truth, solicit religious adherence through public authority, govern theology or confer civil authority on a religious basis. Denominational classifications retain their distinct strict-scrutiny rule. Independently identifiable secular work in a religious setting may be purchased with enforceable controls against devotional use and diversion. Restoration of private exercise restricted by custody or comparable governmental control remains subject to the applicable accommodation inquiry, not an automatic financing prohibition. Those distinct doctrines retain their own sources and burdens.
