**Controlling explanation:** Lemon addresses governmental sponsorship of religion, financing of religious functions and the improper union of civil and religious authority. Its method must identify those acts rather than condemn an independently secular service because officials supervise it to remain secular. Aguilar exposes that defect: location and repeated contact become substitutes for examining what the public program actually does. The Court therefore modifies Lemon's operation while retaining its constitutional concerns.

Purpose and effects help identify the governmental function; institutional relationships show who directs it. Once prohibited conduct is established, actual conversion, religious success or a completed penalty is unnecessary. A secular objective cannot justify prohibited religious means. Conversely, a provider's religious motivation does not make its secular work devotional. Ordinary monitoring of secular performance is not itself establishment, even when repeated; civil control of doctrine and religious control of public authority remain forbidden.

This method requires real operational separation and reliable accountability for funded work, but not invariably separate institutional governance or wholly distinct personnel. Shared structures cannot excuse devotional financing, and they do not automatically defeat a genuinely severable public service. Here public selection, staffing, materials and supervision identify the remedial service to be examined. The school's entire religious mission is not the governmental act.

The dissent's institutional-support objection warrants scrutiny of the service, not a presumption that its secular and religious functions are inseparable. Existing specific holdings continue to govern their established relationships: Kiryas Joel restricts denominational allocation of civil power; Rosenberger permits its controlled publication benefit; Pinette and Chabad distinguish private expression and governmental attribution. The Court does not decide absent voucher, monument, legislative-prayer or accommodation programs, or declare every future application compatible with every preserved precedent.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no tuition-voucher or provider-redemption scheme, monument, legislative-prayer program, general accommodation rule or all forms of direct aid. Parental selection does not itself answer what a particular expenditure purchases; no conclusion about an absent program displaces Witters or Mueller. Religious editorial viewpoint in Rosenberger is not automatically purchased devotional instruction. Private speech, free exercise, institutional religious autonomy, denominational scrutiny and accommodation retain their distinct sources and burdens. No controlling rationale is drawn from separate opinions under Marks.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lemon v. Kurtzman remains governing, with its independent purpose, primary-effect and excessive-entanglement prongs modified into the three routes of this single inquiry. The actual holdings of Kiryas Joel, Rosenberger, Pinette, Chabad, Witters and Mueller remain in force; Zobrest supplies no Establishment Clause merits holding. Kiryas Joel's denominational-allocation and accommodation holdings remain intact; its preservation of Aguilar and Ball established the rule entering this decision, not a bar to the Court's express overruling. Zobrest's antecedent-law remand remains; it supplied no Establishment Clause merits holding and did not already undermine Aguilar by such a holding. Rosenberger's actual controlled vendor-funded printing benefit remains permissible on its program terms; it concerns financing, not merely facility access, and neither decided this public-teaching case nor authorized every devotional expenditure. Pinette's bounded private-expression and access holding remains; it supplies no universal forum immunity or automatic attribution from location. Chabad's contextual-attribution and access holding remains; location alone does not establish governmental sponsorship. Witters's actual benefit holding remains in force; no absent tuition-voucher or provider-redemption scheme is decided. Mueller's actual tax-benefit holding remains in force; the Court does not decide an absent funding program or silently enlarge or displace that holding.

##### II. Separately administered secular instruction is not forbidden because of its religious-school location

**Holding:** The Establishment Clause permits the described supplementary Title I service for children independently selected under neutral public eligibility criteria, delivered by public employees under public supervision with public secular materials, without religious instruction, team teaching or other cooperative instructional activity with religious-school teachers, or participation in worship, and with effective controls against diversion. Religious-school location, presumed inculcation arising from that location, incidental benefit to the school, and ordinary monitoring of that separately administered public service do not by themselves establish unconstitutional sponsorship, devotional financing or institutional control; Aguilar's categorical prohibition and Ball's corresponding Shared Time rationale are displaced at that scope.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the Court's opinion; the five-Justice coalition stated above directly joins the rule, its secular-control qualifications and its precise treatment of precedent.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the constitutional inquiry must distinguish the public remedial service from the religious school's own instruction. Lemon, as updated in Part I, identifies the governmental act and its religious or secular function, using purpose, effects and institutional relationships as evidence within that inquiry. Here the remedial purpose, independent selection of eligible children, public staffing and supervision, secular materials and restrictions on religious participation identify a supplementary public service. Those safeguards establish this program's severable secular operation; operational separation and reliable accountability do not invariably require wholly distinct personnel or separate institutional governance. Ordinary consultation about a child's secular educational needs is distinct from joint teaching. Neutral eligibility or public employment alone would not excuse devotional instruction or diversion.

Aguilar treated the supervision needed to preserve secular instruction as a reason to prohibit the instruction on religious premises. That categorical inference is unsound: supervising public teachers' secular work is not, without more, governing the school's religious affairs. The absence of a finding of indoctrination does not prove every possible defect absent, but location cannot substitute for the connection between the challenged public service and an actual constitutional objection. Nor does an incidental institutional benefit alone turn this separately controlled service into religious teaching.

These safeguards existed when Aguilar was decided. The legal defect is the categorical inference that confuses secular supervision with religious sponsorship and control, not a newly discovered factual transformation. Continuing expense and impaired access make the consequence substantial; they are not the constitutional rule. Property, contractual, completed-transaction and educational-service interests receive their actual weight. No identified concrete property or completed-transaction entitlement requires retaining the categorical ban; established obligations and service continuity remain protected in implementation. Ball's Shared Time program implicated the corresponding inference about supplementary public teaching. Its distinct Community Education program predominantly used religious-school teachers and principals, and today's holding neither approves that arrangement nor reopens its judgment. The onsite rule applies Lemon as modified in Part I; the Court does not approve every program considered in Ball or reopen its completed judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Independent prohibitions on governmental worship, devotional financing and improper institutional control remain; public employment and neutral eligibility do not immunize supported devotional conduct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Aguilar v. Felton is overruled as authority for categorically excluding this safeguarded public Title I service from religious-school premises; no reopening of completed claims or authorization of religious instruction follows. School District of Grand Rapids v. Ball loses its contrary Shared Time rationale at the same scope, insofar as religious location, presumed inculcation, incidental school benefit or ordinary secular monitoring categorically condemns the separately administered public service; its Community Education judgment and unaddressed grounds remain undisturbed. Lemon v. Kurtzman governs through the updated inquiry stated in Holding I.

For the significant-change inquiry and injunction remedy in Holdings III–IV, see Federal Courts, “Modification of continuing injunctions,” Agostini, June 23, 1997, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553.




#### Jackson v. Benson

**Docket or dockets:** No. 98-376
**Decided:** 1999-03-16.

##### Parental routing does not justify the demonstrated purchase of integrated devotional instruction

**Holding:** Wisconsin may not justify public payment for the demonstrated devotional instructional function merely by neutral eligibility, parental selection and restrictive endorsement of its provider-cost payment. Where the financed educational work integrates devotional instruction without reliable separation, those features do not cure the expenditure; religious identity alone does not establish that defect, and genuinely independent private benefits and reliably separated secular services retain their distinct protection.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Six direct joins control the rule and its application; the remedy has the separate coalition stated below.

##### The corrective judgment must preserve the actual limits of the federal conflict

**Holding:** The federal approval and its dependent blanket injunction-dissolution direction are vacated, and the lower courts must determine appropriate provider/payment relief and state-law severability under the funded-function holding. Relief must prevent the established federal conflict while preserving lawful severable applications; the judgment neither rewrites state law nor commands an immediate ban on every payment or religious provider.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II is joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Those five directly support this disposition and remedy. Stone-Zsela seeks the different reversal and mandatory direction stated separately; the three affirming dissenters' conditional acceptance of remedial limits supplies no extra vacatur vote.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Agostini v. Felton:** Applied to the actual instructional function purchased and the need for reliable separation of secular work. Its modified Lemon inquiry remains governing; its reserved voucher question is answered only for this supported cost/instruction arrangement.
- **Lemon v. Kurtzman:** Continues through Agostini's unified inquiry. Its former independent prongs are not restored.
- **Witters v. Washington Department of Services for the Blind; Mueller v. Allen:** Preserved for their actual individual vocational-benefit and general tax-deduction relationships; neither supplies automatic approval of this provider-cost payment.
- **Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia:** Preserved as an actual controlled printing benefit for independently attributed expression; it does not equate all spending with a forum or authorize every devotional expenditure.
- **Bronx Household of Faith; Pinette; Chabad:** Preserve genuinely private expression and contextual attribution. Private access does not itself resolve governmental purchase of instructional work.
- **Committee for Public Education v. Nyquist:** Its aid limitations are not categorically displaced by parental routing; the present application rests on the actual funded function under Agostini.
- **School District of Grand Rapids v. Ball:** Agostini's displacement of its corresponding Shared Time rationale remains confined; the distinct Community Education judgment is not reopened.
- **Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills:** Supplies no Establishment Clause merits holding; its antecedent-law remand does not validate this program.
- **Dalton v. Little Rock Family Planning Services; Leavitt v. Jane L.:** Applied to conflict-specific relief and preservation of independently supported protection while state-law severability is resolved.
- **American Manufacturers v. Sullivan:** Preserves review of government's own conduct despite private-carrier nonattribution. It supplies no Establishment Clause financing exemption.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The actual provider-cost payment cannot receive categorical federal approval solely because eligibility is neutral and a parent selects and endorses the payment. Demonstrated devotional instruction within the financed work must be addressed under the governing expenditure rule. Genuine private-benefit relationships, private forum access and reliably separated secular services remain distinct. The decision supplies no all-provider exclusion, new general facial-challenge formula, compulsory school participation or constitutional subsidy entitlement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** **Vacated and remanded, 5-4 on that disposition.**

**Supporting:** Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing the Court's disposition:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela would reverse the federal approval and remand with the payment-preventing direction described in his separate opinion. Justices Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would affirm the federal approval. Stone-Zsela joins the Court's substantive financing rule but not its vacatur or remedial disposition.

The lower courts must apply the actual funded-function holding to provider/payment scope, appropriate relief and state-law severability. They may use the existing record where sufficient; no new trial or submissions are automatically required. Lawful secular applications and the actual enrollment-status qualification remain protected. No blanket statewide funding cutoff, school closure, pupil transfer, recoupment or damages award follows from this judgment.

## Effective current-term public law and positions

Source: `terms/OT1999/workspace/neutral-projection.md`. Every matching public section is copied; no raw Record or audit annex was read.


### Strout v. Albanese — 2000-02-28

[Record](../records/strout-v-albanese_merits_2000-02-28.md)

#### Event, question and statutory setting

**Strout v. Albanese**; review of 178 F.3d 57 (1st Cir. 1999). Certiorari granted November 1, 1999; argued January 24, 2000; decided February 28, 2000. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit, affirming the District of Maine's judgment at 13 F. Supp. 2d 112.

The granted questions concern whether excluding otherwise eligible sectarian schools and their students from a generally available tuition-assistance program solely because the schools are religious violates free exercise, and whether establishment principles require, permit or forbid that exclusion when assistance reaches schools through independent parental choice. Three families in Minot, where no secondary school was maintained, sought ordinary St. Dominic's tuition. Their application concerns integrated sectarian instruction, not a separately identified secular service or expression forum. The case was resolved on cross-motions for summary judgment without a genuine dispute of material fact.

Section 2951 retains basic school approval, nonsectarian status in accordance with the First Amendment, incorporation under Maine or United States law, reporting and auditing under sections 2952 and 2953 and requirements adopted under section 2954. Schools with at least 60 percent publicly funded students, measured by the previous year's October and April average enrollment, must participate in statewide assessment. Upon a school unit's request, the private school must release copies of all student records for students transferring from that private school to the requesting unit. The repealed fourth condition is not restored. Section 5204(4) applies when the resident unit neither maintains a secondary school nor contracts for secondary-school privileges. It permits attendance at an approved private school, a public school in an adjoining unit accepting tuition students, or an approved school in another state or country, subject to receiving-school permission. The resident unit must pay tuition up to the legal rate. The Department's acceptance of schools' self-identification without a classification dispute is a lower-court finding about administration, not a statutory command.

#### Maine may refuse the integrated devotional tuition presented here

**Holding and operative rule:** The Religion Clauses permit Maine to refuse public payment for the integrated devotional instructional function presented by this ordinary-tuition application. Independent parental choice and neutral student eligibility do not themselves justify public purchase of demonstrated devotional instruction integrated into the financed educational work without reliable separation.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies six direct joins to this holding.

**Controlling explanation:** Jackson requires attention to the instructional function purchased; Agostini applies that inquiry within the governing, unified Lemon test. The parents selected St. Dominic's, and the requested ordinary tuition of approximately $3,850 was below the $4,900 statutory ceiling. That distinguishes the amount formula from Jackson's provider operating-and-debt costs, but does not separate the devotional work in the integrated program for which payment is sought. Maine is not buying an identified interpreter, secular remedial service, or controlled printing benefit. Rosenberger preserves its independently directed publication program; it does not make every publicly financed activity a speech forum. The actual funded function therefore supports refusal of this tuition without prohibiting the families' private educational choice. Neither a school's religious name nor its affiliation alone establishes the character of its work. The Court does not approve a universal religious-provider exclusion, decide all voucher arrangements, or create a constitutional entitlement to every education subsidy.

#### Free exercise receives its own inquiry

**Holding and operative rule:** Refusing this public purchase of integrated devotional instruction does not, in this application, violate the Free Exercise Clause. The conclusion does not depend on whether attending the school is a central religious tenet and does not authorize religious hostility, exclusion from a neutral private-expression forum, or categorical rejection of genuinely severable secular work solely because its provider is religious.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies six direct joins to this holding.

**Controlling explanation:** Lukumi requires scrutiny of religious targeting; Shahar preserves an independent free-exercise inquiry and rejects theological compulsion as a prerequisite to sincere exercise. Calling a program secular cannot by itself answer that inquiry. Here the challenged benefit is public purchase of the ordinary integrated instructional package, and the State may refuse that financed devotional function while the parents remain free to choose it privately. The lower court's observation that no parent described attendance as a central tenet does not defeat the claim, and the Court does not adopt that reasoning. Agostini protects its separately administered secular service despite religious location, and the private forum decisions protect genuinely private religious expression on their actual terms. Those distinct relationships remain intact; this tuition request presents neither one. The Court therefore affirms on the actual financing ground rather than an affiliation-based presumption or an independent-prong Lemon test.

#### The affirmance is limited to the granted claims and this application

**Holding and operative rule:** The judgment sustaining the challenged refusal of integrated tuition is affirmed within the granted Establishment and Free Exercise questions. The decision does not reopen the ungranted speech, equal-protection or parental-liberty claims, the unchallenged dismissals of local defendants, or distinct applications for reliably separated secular services.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies six direct joins to this holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The cross-motions were decided on an agreed absence of genuine material factual dispute. No remand is required to adjudicate a different benefit application not before the Court or to restructure the school. The statutory payment duty for eligible schools remains subject to its independent approval, receiving-school permission and tuition-rate conditions. Affirmance leaves the challenged denial in place; it awards no tuition, damages or recoupment, orders no transfer or school closure, and imposes no statewide funding cutoff.

#### Precedent treatment

- **Jackson v. Benson:** Applied to the actual integrated instructional function; its provider-cost formula is distinguished in amount, without making capped tuition sufficient to separate devotional work. Its limits preserving private benefits and secular services remain.
- **Agostini v. Felton:** Applied as the governing unified Lemon inquiry and to the distinction between integrated devotional work and separately controlled secular instruction; no categorical inference follows from religious location.
- **Lemon v. Kurtzman:** Continues only through Agostini's unified inquiry; the former independent purpose, effect and entanglement prongs are not revived.
- **Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia:** Its controlled printing benefit for independently directed expression remains valid; that relationship does not determine the ordinary tuition purchase here.
- **Church of the Lukumi Babalu Aye, Inc. v. City of Hialeah:** Its neutrality and religious-targeting inquiry is preserved; refusal of the demonstrated devotional purchase is not a license for hostile religious discrimination.
- **Shahar v. Bowers:** Applied to the independent free-exercise inquiry and the irrelevance of theological compulsion to sincere exercise.
- **Witters v. Washington Department of Services for the Blind:** Its actual individual vocational-benefit relationship remains distinct; it supplies no automatic answer for integrated tuition.
- **Mueller v. Allen:** Its general tax-deduction relationship remains distinct and is not displaced by this application.
- **Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills School District:** Its antecedent-law remand supplies no Establishment Clause merits holding or general tuition entitlement.
- **Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10:** Its bounded private-expression and worship-access protection remains intact; forum access differs from purchasing integrated instruction.

#### Resulting law and limits

Effective February 28, 2000, Jackson's funded-function rule applies to the integrated ordinary-tuition application presented here despite the tuition cap and parental choice. Agostini's unified Lemon inquiry continues; its former independent prongs do not return. The separate Free Exercise inquiry rejects theological centrality as a prerequisite, but does not require public purchase of this devotional instructional function. Religious identity alone remains insufficient to establish the financing defect. Genuinely independent private benefits, neutral private-expression forums and reliably separated secular services retain their actual protections. The decision settles this application, not every permitted or required tuition arrangement.

#### Source and record limits

The [petition and appendices, opposition and reply](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40386005_1011) supply the program terms, statutory conditions and record limits. The [First Circuit opinion](https://openjurist.org/178/f3d/57/strout-v-albanese) supplies the judgment under review; the petition reproduces both lower opinions and the historical statutory appendix. The stated grant, argument and decision dates are the case's governing merits calendar. No finding about the devotional content of every course or every religious provider is made. The Department's self-identification practice is described as a record finding, not enacted statutory text.




### Strout v. Albanese — 2000-02-28

[Record](../records/strout-v-albanese_merits_2000-02-28.md)

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the Court's application and emphasizes the distinction between paying for devotional instruction and allowing genuinely private religious expression. Parental designation does not turn a public expenditure into private money. At the same time, an otherwise eligible, genuinely severable secular service cannot be denied payment solely because its provider is religious, and religious expression in a genuinely neutral forum involving no governmental financing, control, selection or attribution must receive equal treatment. Agostini supplies the secular-service distinction; the private-forum decisions protect private expression. These qualifications preserve lawful separate relationships, not a general right to tuition. No such distinct service or forum application is before the Court.

**Justice Kennedy, dissenting, joined by Justices Scalia and Thomas.** They would reverse the exclusion as applied and remand for otherwise lawful eligibility and payment proceedings. The actual benefit is ordinary tuition, capped by statute and here below that cap, selected through independent parental choice at a private school. They distinguish that individual educational benefit from Jackson's provider operating-and-debt-cost formula, while maintaining their disagreement with Jackson's attribution analysis. Rosenberger protects genuinely independent private activity within an available benefit; Witters supports the distinction between a private beneficiary's choice and the State's devotional direction; Lukumi requires scrutiny of excluding religious choices from that benefit. Maine's public-substitute characterization does not, for these Justices, overcome the actual independent direction and capped tuition. They would not excuse sham parental choice, governmental devotional direction, or other independent statutory ineligibility, and would order no immediate subsidy or entitlement to every tuition program.

Supreme Court merits review of the granted Religion Clause questions is complete. The First Circuit's judgment remains in force at the stated scope. No new lower-court factfinding, statutory redesign or subsidy proceeding is directed by the affirmance.




### Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth — 2000-03-22

[Record](../records/board-of-regents-v-southworth_merits_2000-03-22.md)

#### Event and question

Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth, No. 98-1189. Merits decision, March 22, 2000. Argued November 9, 1999. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, 151 F.3d 717, decided August 10, 1998, Nos. 97-3510 and 97-3548.

The question is whether a public university violates the First Amendment by requiring students to pay an activity fee supporting organizations engaged in political, ideological and religious expression. The live dispute concerns speech and association and the objectors' allocable fees. The general fee power, nonallocable services, ordinary student government, nonideological organizations, nonobjectors' money, the student newspaper and Distinguished Lecture Series are outside the challenge.

In 1995–96 a full-time Madison student paid a mandatory $165.75 segregated fee each semester; nonpayment prevented receipt of grades and graduation. About 80 percent supported unchallenged nonallocable services. The challenged allocation program supported registered student organizations and qualifying services through student-government processes subject to University approval. The ordinary grant and General Student Services Fund routes were stipulated to operate without viewpoint discrimination or University advocacy of a particular view. Student organizations controlled their own messages, which included lobbying and other political advocacy; the University disclaimed their expression. Most funding reimbursed expenses. WISPIRG received lump-sum funding, and all uses of that money are not established.

The stipulation expressly excludes the referendum. Student government treated referendum outcomes as binding, permitting allocation, termination or veto of a group's funding. The record contains a WISPIRG funding vote and an October 1996 termination vote, without establishing a constitutional finding about each ballot's operation. The lower court had already confined relief to objectors' allocable fees and removed the district court's all-students injunction and prescribed detailed administrative mechanism. It nevertheless sustained the general compelled-subsidy liability rule challenged here.

#### The genuine educational private-expression program may be supported by a general fee

**Holding and operative rule:** A public university may require a generally applicable student activity fee to support a broad program of independent extracurricular student expression serving its educational mission when eligibility, allocation and administration are objective and viewpoint neutral. Within that program, a student's disagreement with a recipient's political, ideological or religious viewpoint does not require an ideological opt-out or pro rata refund.

**Authority:** Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg, supplies six direct rationale votes for this holding and its limits. Stevens, Souter and Breyer support the judgment on the distinct ground described below.

**Controlling explanation:** Compelled financing implicates expression even without personal attribution. Abood and Keller protect against compulsory ideological support outside the representative duties that justify union and integrated-bar charges; Lehnert applies those distinct union principles. Here the common educational function is maintaining opportunities for independent and competing student expression. It does not appoint one organization to speak for every payer. Requiring courts to sort student ideas by agreement with an official educational orthodoxy would defeat that function.

Glickman recognizes expressive burdens while permitting a specified common expense within collective agricultural regulation. Its nonideological product-promotion holding does not itself authorize student ideological subsidies; this different program supplies its own justification. Rosenberger protects viewpoint equality in its controlled private-publication benefit and reserved the compulsory-fee question now presented. Independent student control, broad educational opportunity and viewpoint-neutral administration together answer that question here. Lack of attribution, common benefit, majority approval or enrollment alone would not suffice.

The ordinary SGAF/GSSF allocation and approval process is stipulated neutral, and the University does not advocate a particular viewpoint through it. Students need not adopt recipients' views, join their groups or surrender their own expression. The fee therefore supports a forum, not governmental endorsement of each message. The University may choose optional contributions or refunds, but the Constitution does not require ideological exemptions on these facts. No finding that opt-outs are impossible or every subsidy indispensable is made.

#### Neutrality governs the actual program, not every university expenditure

**Holding and operative rule:** The protection for a compulsory independent-expression program requires viewpoint-neutral eligibility, allocation and administration; neither an educational label nor popular approval permits selection by viewpoint. Reasonable neutral limits on program scope, financial accountability, performance and fraud prevention remain available; the rule does not require equal grants or funding every activity.

**Authority:** The same six Justices join Justice Thomas's opinion on this holding and its limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Rosenberger distinguishes independently attributed student expression from official advocacy and forbids viewpoint exclusion within the benefit actually opened. A neutral program may define the work it supports and require an account of its performance; those conditions cannot conceal approval or rejection of ideology. The decision addresses the actual broad student-expression program and establishes no constitutional requirement that curricular choices, faculty instruction or governmental speech follow the same forum rule. It does not immunize those activities from other constitutional review.

This case establishes no particular devotional purchase. Agostini's unified Lemon inquiry, Jackson's funded-function rule and Strout's integrated-tuition application remain distinct from protected religious editorial viewpoint in Rosenberger. Neutrality alone does not authorize governmental worship or devotional instruction as such. The Court neither decides nor revives an Establishment, free-exercise or RFRA claim, and does not conclusively approve a particular external payment without its facts and proper claim.

#### The referendum requires separate examination

**Holding and operative rule:** A university cannot distribute access to its compulsory private-expression program according to majority approval of a group's viewpoint. The binding referendum is outside the ordinary-allocation neutrality stipulation, so the Seventh Circuit's general compulsory-subsidy judgment must be reversed and the case remanded only to determine the referendum's actual operation and appropriate relief.

**Authority:** The same six Justices join Justice Thomas's complete referendum rule. Stevens, Souter and Breyer independently support the referendum remand without adopting the complete necessity framework.

**Controlling explanation:** The referendum can allocate, terminate or veto funding, and the student government treats its result as binding. Ordinary committee neutrality does not establish referendum neutrality. Minority expression cannot depend on majority ideological consent. Yet the record does not decide every ballot's operation or establish that every possible voting arrangement is unconstitutional. The Court assumes no neutral final override and orders examination of the existing procedure.

The lower court already removed the district court's all-students reach and prescribed administrative scheme. Neither is restored. The late partisan-policy challenge is not reopened by a general reference to unresolved questions; the operative remand concerns the referendum alone and permits only relief its actual defect warrants. No damages, fixed refund, specified WISPIRG payment, universal opt-in procedure or order to fund every recipient is entered.

#### Limits and questions not reached

The operative remand does not reopen a late partisan-policy challenge foreclosed by the reiterated ordinary-allocation stipulation, and no written restriction is assumed rescinded, amended or universally valid. The separate limits on GSSF lobbying or primarily political groups are not exceptions to a different religious-activity provision. The decision does not adjudicate every outside expenditure or every written exclusion. Off-campus location alone supplies no categorical constitutional disposition of an otherwise eligible student expressive activity; the actual program relationship and proper claim remain necessary.

No Establishment, free-exercise, RFRA or state claim is revived or decided. Religious editorial viewpoint is not identical to governmental worship or devotional instruction. No actual devotional purchase is established here, and the ruling does not authorize one. The two reported WISPIRG sums are not treated as the same accounting measure or used to calculate relief. Unchallenged program components remain outside the judgment.

#### Precedent treatment

- **Abood v. Detroit Board of Education; Keller v. State Bar of California:** Their protection against compelled nongermane ideological support remains effective in exclusive-representation and integrated-bar settings. Their representative-duty inquiry does not itself decide this different educational expression program.
- **Lehnert v. Ferris Faculty Association:** Its union chargeability framework and divided applications remain intact; the statutory-duty separate account is not converted into a universal Court rule for universities.
- **Glickman v. Wileman Brothers & Elliott, Inc., June 25, 1997:** Its specified permission for germane nonideological promotion within collective agricultural regulation is distinguished, not expanded into a prior approval of ideological student subsidies. Its recognition that financing can burden expression remains controlling at that scope.
- **Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, June 29, 1995:** Its private-publication viewpoint equality and controlled printing-benefit holdings remain intact. The compulsory-fee question it reserved is resolved here for the defined broad educational expression program, with separate safeguards for payers and recipients.
- **Gibson v. Florida Bar, December 4, 1991:** Its advance-protection, chargeability-burden and impartial-review requirements remain for established compulsory nongermane charges. It does not itself establish a violation in the ordinary program upheld here, so those procedures are not imposed on that program.
- **Air Line Pilots Association v. Miller, May 26, 1998:** Its distinction between offering impartial arbitration and requiring nonconsenting objectors to exhaust it remains unchanged. No new mandatory arbitration mechanism is created.
- **Agostini v. Felton, June 23, 1997; Jackson v. Benson, March 16, 1999; Strout v. Albanese, February 28, 2000:** Their distinct rules concerning the actual governmental religious function remain effective. This private-expression decision does not replace Agostini's unified Lemon inquiry, authorize a devotional purchase or treat religious identity alone as governmental religious work.

#### Resulting law and limits

Effective March 22, 2000, a genuine broad educational program for independent extracurricular student expression may be financed through generally applicable student fees without ideological opt-outs when its eligibility, allocations and administration are objective and viewpoint neutral. This is a justification for the defined program, not a conclusion that compulsory financing is nonexpressive or that every useful subsidy is permissible. The protection extends to political, ideological and religious viewpoints within the program's lawful scope. Neutral accountability and program limits remain available; popularity cannot replace viewpoint neutrality. The ordinary-allocation stipulation does not validate the separate referendum. The earlier compelled-representation, private-publication and religious-function rules retain their actual fields.

#### Source and record limits

The [Seventh Circuit opinion, 151 F.3d 717](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/151/151.F3d.717.97-3548.97-3510.html) supplies the program, lower-court reasoning, narrowed decree and claim history. The [published record recitations, 529 U.S. 217](https://www.govinfo.gov/app/details/USREPORTS-529/USREPORTS-529-217) supply the ordinary-allocation stipulation and its express referendum exclusion. [Lehnert, 500 U.S. 507](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep500/usrep500507/usrep500507.pdf) supplies the preexisting union framework and separate accounts. No exact WISPIRG sum or finding about all uses of its lump-sum support is necessary to the disposition; no policy revision or particular devotional payment is assumed.




### Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth — 2000-03-22

[Record](../records/board-of-regents-v-southworth_merits_2000-03-22.md)

Effective March 22, 2000, a genuine broad educational program for independent extracurricular student expression may be financed through generally applicable student fees without ideological opt-outs when its eligibility, allocations and administration are objective and viewpoint neutral. This is a justification for the defined program, not a conclusion that compulsory financing is nonexpressive or that every useful subsidy is permissible. The protection extends to political, ideological and religious viewpoints within the program's lawful scope. Neutral accountability and program limits remain available; popularity cannot replace viewpoint neutrality. The ordinary-allocation stipulation does not validate the separate referendum. The earlier compelled-representation, private-publication and religious-function rules retain their actual fields.

Supreme Court review of the general compelled-subsidy claim is complete. The case returns to the Seventh Circuit for proceedings concerning only the referendum's actual operation and appropriate relief consistent with the opinion. The general liability rule against the stipulated ordinary program is reversed. No revived policy challenge or religious claim, mandated administrative design, new evidentiary hearing, filing deadline or monetary award follows.

#### Published noncontrolling position — Southworth, March 22, 2000

**Justice Stevens, concurring in the judgment, joined by Justices Souter and Breyer.** They support reversal and referendum remand. The fee reaches an independent distribution program advancing no single ideology and supporting many competing speakers. That actual program, its educational purpose, the attenuated payer-message connection and stipulated neutral administration defeat the claimed ideological exemption. The recipients' political advocacy remains real; the conclusion does not depend on classifying the money as nonspeech, enrollment as waiver, taxation as immunity, or lack of attribution alone.

They would decide that the actual stipulated neutrality and program structure suffice, without declaring a newly necessary viewpoint-neutrality framework for every compulsory university-expression arrangement. They preserve Rosenberger's existing viewpoint-access rule and create no academic-autonomy immunity. They agree that the referendum falls outside the stipulation and requires further proceedings. The same unpresented religious-function and remedial limits apply. They do not join the Court's opinion; agreement with its judgment does not adopt its complete constitutional framework.

This concurrence is noncontrolling and supplies no extra join in the Court's necessity framework. No other separate position is published. No roster, allotment or standing-practice change occurs, and no next Supreme Court act or condition is ordered. The referendum remains for lower-court disposition; no separate pending Court case is decided.




### Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe — 2000-06-19

[Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe](../records/santa-fe-independent-school-district-v-doe_merits_2000-06-19.md)

#### The school may not delegate its official religious choice to a student majority

**Holding and operative rule:** A public secondary school may not organize an invocation as a representative component of its own football pregame ceremony and delegate to a student-majority election whether that official component will include religious exercise. The challenged board-created election mechanism violates the Establishment Clause under both its unrestricted and nonsectarian/nonproselytizing versions; relief reaches that mechanism while preserving genuinely voluntary private prayer, independent student expression, lawful secular messages and genuine neutral access.

**Authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, supplying seven direct votes for the rule, explanation and limited affirmance. The particular government-created representative religious choice is essential to all seven joins; the Court adopts no independent religious-pressure holding.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini requires identification of the governmental act and its function, using purpose, effect and institutional relationships as evidence within the modified Lemon inquiry. Here the board creates an invocation or message slot within its own ceremony; the student council conducts a majority election under the principal's advice and direction; the electorate selects a single volunteer; and the speaker must serve the board's prescribed purposes. This is an official representative component, not generally available access for independently chosen private speakers. Lee forbids official school organization of worship, and Ingebretsen explains why student initiation or delivery cannot alone make that exercise private. A majority vote cannot privatize the board's delegated religious choice.

The District stresses secular purposes, student authorship, no required prayer, optional attendance and the possibility of silence or a secular message. Those features do not remove the existing governmental mechanism. The Court assumes no official textual preapproval and no delivered October prayer; it does not rest on a finding that every secular purpose is a sham. The ability to decline use of the delegated religious authority does not transform it into neutral access. Pinette and Bronx Household protect actually private expression, where government has not adopted the devotion as its own event component. Tanford likewise distinguishes official organization from private composition, without making its adult setting identical to this school policy.

The backup's religious-content limits retain the same official mechanism and do not cure it. The Court does not direct officials to edit private religion into acceptable language. Nor does it prohibit every election, invited student speaker or religious message. The existing mechanism suffices to affirm; Lee and Ingebretsen's separate protection against substantial practical religious pressure remains intact without requiring legal compulsion or compulsory attendance. No additional pressure finding, universal facial-challenge rule, new forum entitlement, graduation ruling or damages award is necessary.

- **Agostini v. Felton:** Applied as the governing governmental-act/function inquiry; purpose, effects and entanglement remain evidence within that inquiry, not independent surviving prongs.
- **Lemon v. Kurtzman:** Continues in the form Agostini adopted; the Court revives neither an independent purpose test nor an independent endorsement test.
- **Lee v. Weisman:** Its official-organization rule applies to this school-created invocation mechanism. Its independently sufficient substantial-pressure rule remains, without a compulsory-attendance prerequisite; no new pressure holding is made here.
- **Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District:** Its rejection of automatic insulation by student delivery and its protection of lawful private expression apply. Its provision-sensitive interim ruling is not recast as a universal rule invalidating every future religious message.
- **Tanford v. Brand:** Preserved for its established official devotional program and its distinction between independent wording and private access; the Court does not transplant every feature of university commencement to football.
- **Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette:** Its actual generally available private forum is distinguished from this selective representative event component; religious content and government location remain insufficient by themselves to establish attribution.
- **Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10:** Its independently organized private use remains protected; the school's own representative ceremony presents a different governmental function.
- **Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District:** Its protection of religious viewpoints within genuine comparable private access remains intact; no schoolwide private forum is inferred here.
- **Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia:** Its independently directed private-expression relationship remains intact; it does not authorize government-organized worship.
- **Chabad of Southern Ohio v. City of Cincinnati:** Its contextual attribution inquiry remains; neither a forum label nor location alone supplies the answer.
- **Strout v. Albanese:** Its distinction between genuine private expression and governmental performance or purchase of a religious function is preserved; no football-specific rule is imported from its tuition setting.

The official character of the challenged representative religious-choice mechanism supplies the constitutional defect even without a delivered October prayer or official editing of its words. Government cannot make its own religious choice private by delegating it to a student majority. Agostini's unified governmental-act/function inquiry governs; no independent purpose or endorsement test returns. Genuinely private religious expression retains its protection. The independent substantial-pressure rules of Lee and Ingebretsen continue without requiring legally compulsory attendance; this decision need not decide that additional ground.




### Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe — 2000-06-19

[Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe](../records/santa-fe-independent-school-district-v-doe_merits_2000-06-19.md)

**Justice Scalia, dissenting, joined by Justice Thomas.** They would reverse the facial invalidation of the unrestricted October policy and remand for bounded adjudication of any actual official-adoption or unconstitutional-pressure application. Agostini requires examination of what government actually does, and they accept its prohibition on government-organized worship. But the present wording permits a secular message, a religious message or no message and leaves the selected volunteer the content choice. In their view, the election and prior prayer history do not sufficiently establish that every implementation of this policy is official devotion. Their Ingebretsen objection distinguishes anticipated official participation from supported implementation; Pinette and Bronx Household preserve genuine private access. They do not require a government-written script before attribution is possible, treat student authorship as conclusive, or deny Tanford's established official-attribution holding. Tanford's adult ceremonial dispute supplies no general permission for official secondary-school worship.

For the conditional backup, they would vacate and remand its football disposition with the unrestricted-policy proceedings so that the lower courts can determine the lawful reach and severability of any needed relief. They would not restore mandatory screening of private speech for nonsectarian and nonproselytizing content, and they do not hold the backup independently valid. It becomes operative only if the unrestricted version is enjoined; no independent continuing application is presumed. Their component remedies preserve genuine voluntary expression while leaving supported actual official worship and substantial practical pressure open to adjudication. They would neither reopen graduation or damages questions nor authorize suppression of private religious viewpoints.




### Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe — 2000-06-19

[Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe](../records/santa-fe-independent-school-district-v-doe_merits_2000-06-19.md)

**Affirmed as to both football-policy versions, 7–2.**

| Component | Supporting affirmance | Opposing | Opposing disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unrestricted October policy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Scalia and Thomas | Reverse and remand for bounded adjudication of supported actual official adoption or unconstitutional pressure |
| Conditional nonsectarian/nonproselytizing backup | Same seven Justices | Scalia and Thomas | Vacate and remand for lawful scope and severability of any needed relief, without restoring compulsory religious-content screening |

The judgment restrains the challenged official representative invocation mechanism. It preserves genuinely voluntary private prayer, independent student expression, lawful secular messages and genuine neutral access, and requires neither a new forum nor censorship of otherwise lawful private religious viewpoints. It does not decide graduation, damages or later incidents outside this record.

The Fifth Circuit's football-policy judgment remains in force at the stated official-mechanism scope. Supreme Court merits review of the granted question is complete. No new policy, compulsory content screen, forum entitlement, damages award, evidentiary hearing or Supreme Court return date is ordered. The separate graduation and damages dispositions remain outside this review.

### Agostini - published source

Source: `terms/OT1996/output/OT_1996CHUNK11.md`.


#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–IV | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas in full | Vacate both judgments and remand for tailored prospective relief | Part I: modification of Lemon; Part II: onsite secular-service rule and precise overruling; Part III: present legal change under Rule 60(b)(5); Part IV: tailored removal with retained safeguards and lawful obligations |
| Dissent, Part I | Souter | Stevens and Ginsburg | Would affirm both judgments | Affirmative substantive objection to this public instructional support; Breyer does not join |
| Same dissent, Part II | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer | Would affirm both judgments | Retention of precedent and absence of sufficient intervening erosion or factual change |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter and Breyer | Would affirm both judgments on an independent procedural ground | Rule 60 review cannot supply this means of rehearing the original constitutional ruling |

#### Separate Writings

##### Justice Souter, dissenting

Stevens and Ginsburg join the dissent in full. Breyer joins Part II only.

In Part I, Souter, Stevens and Ginsburg would retain Aguilar's and Ball's applicable substantive rules. Public employees conducting education within a religious school's ordinary instructional setting supply governmental educational support, not independently attributable private speech. Public staffing, neutral eligibility and safeguards do not alone resolve the institutional subsidy and entanglement concerns. Rosenberger's private publication and the private-forum decisions therefore do not decide this case. They reject the Court's modification of Lemon because its treatment of purpose, effects and entanglement within attribution and function understates the constitutional significance of continuing public instructional support. Their objection concerns this governmental program rather than a claim that every private religious act must become government speech. These Justices do not find proved proselytizing and do not require it for their structural objection. Breyer does not join this affirmative direct-aid rationale.

In Part II, all four dissenters would adhere to the direct precedents. The safeguards and anticipated costs did not newly arise. Kiryas Joel preserved Aguilar and Ball, Zobrest supplied no establishment merits holding, and the bounded expression decisions did not erode the public-instruction rule sufficiently to justify its abandonment. They oppose adopting the general methodological change as a basis for abandoning these precedents in this case. They would affirm denial of relief and preserve the injunction.

##### Justice Ginsburg, dissenting; joined by Stevens, Souter and Breyer

The four dissenters would also affirm on an independent procedural ground. Browder limits an appeal from denial of Rule 60 relief; it does not reopen the original judgment for indefinite rehearing. The district court followed still-binding Aguilar, and Rufo requires a qualifying change rather than an appeal whose own desired outcome creates the prerequisite for reopening. This Court may examine the continued force of its precedents in a proper case, but that power does not establish this vehicle's propriety. Their position does not turn an adjudicated injunction into a negotiated consent promise, deny Rule 60 relief after an actual qualifying change, or treat costs alone as sufficient. The Court's adoption of an updated Lemon test does not, in their view, cure that procedural defect. This writing addresses the route to relief independently of the substantive merits disagreement.

### Jackson v. Benson - published source

Source: `terms/OT1998/output/OT_1998CHUNK5.md`.


#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Part I: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Part II: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. | Vacatur and confined remand | I: funded-function rule and actual cost/instruction application. II: formal disposition and limited remedy. |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stone-Zsela | Author only | Would reverse and remand with a more definite payment direction | Joins Part I; preserves his broader stated prohibition and required relief; does not join Part II. |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Program-specific private educational benefit; no universal neutrality or parental-choice safe harbor. |

#### Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela concurs in Part I and dissents from Part II.** He would reverse the federal approval and require relief preventing public payment for religious-school tuition or integrated devotional and inseparably sectarian instruction, including payment through checks, vouchers, tax credits, reimbursements, provider-redemption instruments or another third-party route. His objection is to the function purchased with restricted public value, not the religious identity of the school. Neither the parent's selection nor the instrument's form makes the purchased religious formation privately financed.

He distinguishes Agostini's publicly controlled secular service and Rosenberger's independently edited publication benefit. He preserves genuine neutral forums, genuinely severable publicly controlled secular services, and the distinct arrangements in Witters and Mueller. Actual operative payment terms, instruction and severability determine the reach of relief. He would direct application of state severability law to the secular-school portion, permitting preservation of genuinely severable publicly controlled secular services; ungranted state-law judgments remain untouched. His required reversal and payment direction are not the Court's more limited vacatur.

**Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, dissents.** The combined program facts establish a genuine individual educational benefit: faith-neutral child eligibility, real family selection among supported public and private choices, independently operated schools and payment bounded by enrollment and educational cost. In their view, cost verification defines the amount of the child's benefit rather than making the chosen curriculum a governmental religious undertaking. They acknowledge the integrated instruction and possibility of payment beyond tuition; they do not treat a parent signature or neutrality alone as conclusive.

Witters shows that a genuine individual benefit may support privately chosen religious training. Rosenberger, Agostini and the private-access decisions preserve the difference between governmental activity and independently directed religious use. The dissenters would affirm this program while leaving actual governmental direction, religious preference, sham choice or other independently prohibited administration for a properly supported claim. They recognize no universal voucher rule, constitutional duty to subsidize or right to compel a school to participate. If corrective relief is entered, it must remain confined; that conditional limit does not join the Court's financing premise or vacatur.

# Stenberg v. Carhart — validated neutral framing

**Stage and cutoff:** Fresh neutral validation for the June 28, 2000 merits event; argued April 25, 2000; No. 99-830. Cutoff: immediately before decision. This packet supplies no vote, coalition or disposition. All uncoordinated June 28 matters use the same entering-law baseline. The runtime's stale statement that no current-term event has been completed is superseded; no new reproductive-liberty rule is supplied by the other authorized material. Jones's June 26 political-nomination law supplies no automatic abortion-method rule.

**Review channel, questions and participation:** Certiorari to the Eighth Circuit's judgment in 192 F.3d 1142 (1999). The approved question includes reach to ordinary D&E and the absence of a health exception. The exact two questions from Nebraska's merits brief are copied below; the petition's additional fetal-personhood and wholesale-reconsideration questions do not enlarge this grant. Respondent's supported health, bodily-integrity, purpose and vagueness arguments may support the protection under review within their proper preservation and decisional scope. No additional jurisdictional or participation defect is identified. The seated nine-Justice Court is Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; no intervening participation change is supplied. Ordinary quorum and appellate-majority rules apply.

## Enacted terms and completeness correction

The official five-page LB23 enactment was approved June 9, 1997; section 10 makes it effective when passed and approved according to law. All five saved images were visually inspected. The exact statute is preserved in those images and the official saved PDF, linked below. **The fresh modeling context must inspect page 2 and the linked continuation pages before relying on the complete licensing or administrative provisions.** Embedded PDF text omits much of the underlined new language. It is not a complete statutory quotation. The earlier brief extract's license ellipsis is not silently filled or treated as the statute. No source passage has been manually reconstructed into a purported exact transcript.

The following is a qualified description, not a substitute transcription:

- Section 2 adds §28-326(9). It defines the procedure through partial vaginal delivery of a living unborn child before killing and completing delivery; the elaboration requires deliberately and intentionally delivering a living unborn child, or a substantial portion, into the vagina for a procedure the performer knows will kill the child and that does kill it. These are act, intent, knowledge, life and sequence elements; a clinical label alone does not establish them. The definition has no viability limitation.
- Section 3(1), codified as §28-328(1), prohibits that procedure unless necessary to save the mother's life endangered by a physical disorder, physical illness or physical injury, including a life-endangering physical condition caused by or arising from the pregnancy itself. It supplies no independent health exception. The broader emergency-situation definition in amended §28-326(7), spanning pages 1–2, addresses immediate abortion to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial impairment of a major bodily function, on the physician's good-faith clinical judgment. Its presence does not insert those broader terms into section 3(1), which does not invoke that definition.
- Section 3(2) makes intentional and knowing performance of an unlawful procedure violating subsection (1) a Class III felony. Section 3(3) forbids prosecution of the woman upon whom an unlawful procedure is performed under this section or for conspiracy to violate it. It is a distinct patient immunity, not a physician defense.
- **Section 3(4):** Intentional and knowing performance of an unlawful procedure results in automatic suspension and revocation of the attending physician's Nebraska medical license by the Director of Regulation and Licensure pursuant to §§71-147 through 71-161.20. The actor, conduct condition and statutory process cross-reference qualify the provision; it must not be shortened to an unexplained automatic loss of license without the enacted procedure.
- **Section 3(5):** When the Attorney General or a county attorney files criminal charges under this section, the Attorney General shall also file a petition under §71-150 to suspend and revoke the attending physician's license. A hearing shall be set under §71-153. At that hearing the physician shall have an opportunity to present evidence that the conduct was necessary to save the mother's life under the same physical-disorder, illness or injury terms, including the pregnancy-caused life-endangering condition. A criminal defendant may bring a motion to delay the beginning of the trial until after the Director's §71-155 order. The Director's findings on that life-necessity question shall be admissible in the criminal proceeding. The statute does not make a motion an automatic delay, make the findings conclusive, or create a health exception or preprocedure permission requirement.
- Section 5 adds the corresponding intentional-and-knowing procedure violation to §71-148(15)'s unprofessional-conduct categories, expressly retaining the same life-saving exception. Section 6 amends §71-155: the proceeding is summary and tried as an equity action before the Director or designated hearing officer; affidavits may be received at their discretion, and subpoena and deposition procedures are preserved. Where the petition concerns §71-148(15), the Director shall make findings on life necessity under the same specified terms. The general authority to enter orders, irrespective of the petition, includes censure/reprimand, suspended judgment, probation, practice limitations, a civil penalty up to $10,000 based on severity, suspension, revocation, or dismissal. The relation of these general powers to section 3(4)'s mandatory wording is not resolved by inventing an exception or an unreported state construction.
- Under §71-155, after finding guilt the Director may consult the board of examiners on sanctions or conditions; if the Director does so, the licensee receives the request and the board recommendation and an opportunity to respond in the manner the Director determines. Suspension lasts a definite period set by the Director, with the specified reinstatement conditions; revocation is for all time, except that an application for reinstatement may be made after two years under §71-161.04. Section 7's amended §71-171.02 separately permits the board, after Attorney General referral, to send an approved letter of concern, advise on a voluntary assurance of compliance, or recommend a §71-150 petition. The assurance is signed and public; it is not an admission of violation. These are not an invented medical-board veto over treatment or an additional automatic defense.
- Section 4 adds section 3 to §28-340's employment-protection remedy for a person whose employment or position is altered, impaired or terminated in violation of the listed provisions. It does not by itself create a patient damages award in this case. Section 8 expressly preserves the validity and constitutionality of remaining portions if a section or part is declared invalid or unconstitutional. This severability direction requires attention to independently lawful provisions and applications; it does not authorize judicial rewriting of the prohibited act or silently nullify dependent sanctions. Section 9 repeals the superseded sections identified there.

The complete enactment includes older findings and other professional-discipline provisions. Their exact text remains in the linked images. Legislative disapproval of abortion alone does not establish the prohibited obstructive purpose under current Casey; any purpose holding must address objective text, design, context and operation.

## Record, judgments and competing arguments

Carhart performs abortions before viability. The supplied lower-court recitations distinguish ordinary D&E, which can draw a living fetus's limb through the cervix before dismemberment, from intact variants and from instances where fetal demise precedes removal. That distinction matters under the enacted living-fetus element. The district court's credited evidence supports specific safety advantages for Carhart's intact method in the circumstances of roughly 10–20 annual cases, including reduced instrumentation, retained parts, trauma, blood loss, operating time and infection risk. It does not establish universal superiority in every patient. The State disputes comparative superiority, notes the absence of controlled comparisons and invokes available alternatives and professional ethics. The credited testimony, its qualified scope, and the district court's credibility assessments must be retained; competent disagreement is not automatically a physician veto or categorical legislative immunity from review.

**Actual relief below:** The District Court expressly declined whole-statute facial invalidation. It declared LB23 unconstitutional as applied to Carhart, his patients and similarly situated persons regarding his D&E and D&X methods on nonviable fetuses, and enjoined the defendants and those bound under Rule 65(d) to that extent, with service on county attorneys. The exact operative directions are copied below. Its grounds concerned the health/access effect of restricting Carhart's intact method, reach to ordinary D&E and vagueness of substantial portion. The Eighth Circuit affirmed on the ordinary-D&E reach and substantial-obstacle ground; it did not decide the separate intact-only burden or vagueness grounds. Its separate county-attorney fee appeal is not independently before the Court under the supplied merits question. An affirmance cannot be casually described as a new whole-statute, nationwide or all-gestation invalidation.

**Nebraska's supported position:** The enacted sequence, context and purpose can reasonably distinguish intact delivery from dismemberment D&E. Constitutional avoidance and respect for state law favor that construction; the Attorney General's D&E nonenforcement position supports its practical operation. Alternatives preserve choice, and the State may regulate a distinct method for potential-life and medical-ethics reasons without allowing an unsupported preference to defeat the law. The strongest counterargument is that the enacted substantial-portion language may reach deliberate limb extraction in ordinary D&E despite those purposes; a litigation assurance is not a binding state supreme-court construction or established control over every county prosecutor. Even a permissible narrowing must answer necessary-care and actual-burden claims.

**Carhart's supported position:** The text and described ordinary procedure satisfy the enacted elements, and the penalty risk restricts the predominant second-trimester method. Separately, prohibiting the intact method without usable necessary-health protection forces clinically significant risk in supported applications. The strongest counterargument is that procedure overlap alone does not establish notice failure, coverage of every D&E, universal method necessity, or a remedy beyond the proved applications. The State's contextual construction and actual alternatives need independent consideration. The evidence must establish interference, causation and an affected population under actual Casey, not a presumption against every abortion regulation.

## Law, independent paths and remedial limits

The full supplied Casey, Fargo and Voinovich standards, holdings, public joins and material separate positions are mechanically copied below. Casey's **complete six-step framework** controls: enacted duty and baseline; actual function and purpose; concrete attributable interference and stable affected population; independent baseline/purpose/obstacle bars followed by residual severe-interference scrutiny and meaningful-interference justification and fit; clinical and communication safeguards; and violation-specific relief. The State's justification burden follows the appropriate established trigger, not abortion's subject matter alone. Usable care and clinical necessity are distinct from every preference. No numerical facial threshold or independent no-valid-applications barrier displaces this rule.

Voinovich rejects categorical invalidity merely because statutory and medical labels overlap or every clinical judgment lacks an express fault term. It equally requires independent substantive method, necessary-care, alternative-care and remedy analysis. Nebraska's enactment is not Ohio's: Ohio's particular criminal and civil alternative-method defenses and burdens cannot be imported. Fargo likewise separates intelligibility, actual fault and substantive necessary-care protection. The words intentional and knowing do not automatically answer uncertainty about every element, establish good faith as a universal immunity, or cure a substantive exclusion of necessary care.

- A construction-and-access path may sustain the existing bounded protection if the enacted prohibition reaches ordinary D&E and the supported affected population loses practical timely, clinically appropriate care. It must explain actual reach and interference, rather than rely on the label D&E or only on rejected categorical vagueness reasoning. An independently sufficient coverage/access ground need not manufacture an additional medical holding.
- A separately supported health path may sustain protection for circumstances in which the prohibition denies timely necessary care, even if a legally available narrowing distinguishes ordinary D&E. It must identify the actual clinical trigger, credible evidence, usable alternatives and limited applications. No universal finding that the intact method is safest follows; no narrow life-only exception becomes a health exception through a favorable label or post-charge hearing.
- A State-favoring construction and application may permit reversal or narrower relief only if that construction is legally available and the record supports lawful care and the relevant justification under the governing framework. It must confront the existing independent grounds and injunction's actual scope. A construction cannot be invented from the desired outcome or borrowed from Ohio.
- A bounded remand may address a genuinely unresolved material construction, clinical application or remedy, with its legal source and ordinary interim consequences specified. No new hearing, automatic dissolution, automatic blanket enforcement or permanent all-applications injunction follows from remand alone. If a proposed path depends on certification, a binding Nebraska construction or additional severability doctrine, validate the narrow missing source before relying on it.

Notice/fault, health necessity, practical access, prohibited purpose and severability are separable questions. The distinct administrative mechanism must be evaluated for the work it actually does; an evidentiary hearing after charges does not itself authorize treatment or supply every substantive protection. Section 8's severability text must be applied without simply importing Ohio's state-law rules or deciding unpresented professional-discipline claims.

**Justice-specific continuity:** Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer joined Souter's Voinovich opinion completely. Kennedy joined its notice rule, grave necessary mental-health protection, bounded protective-construction inquiry and ordinary severability rule, while rejecting residual lesser-burden review and favoring reversal of the method injunction under his distinct regulation account. Scalia and Thomas joined notice/severability but dissented from constitutional abortion and mental-health protections. The full prior publications below preserve those differences. None supplies a vote on Nebraska's materially different wording, absent Ohio defenses and developed medical findings. The enhanced issue-specific comparison remains for a separate fresh modeling context.

**Readiness and source limits:** Ready for independent modeling on these supported paths after inspection of the exact official source images specified above. The objective facts rely on supplied published recitations and sanitized control extraction under Engine §14; this validator does not claim exhaustive reading of all briefs and appendix volumes. The image omission in searchable text is a retrieval limitation, not absence of enacted law or a litigant failure. No new fact, litigant default, waiver, filing, procedural clock, reconstruction premise or judicial act is adopted. An additional quantitative medical assertion, specific clinical condition, authoritative construction or precise preservation claim requires narrow verification before use. No target historical Supreme Court opinion, private Stone material, commitments or comparator was accessed.

## Exact official source, preserved as images

The linked images are source images, not a reconstructed transcript. Page 2 contains all of section 3; page 3 begins the amended discipline procedure; page 4 completes it and contains severability; page 5 contains the effective-date provision.
