# Frozen neutral packet — United States v. Alaska, implementing decree

Fresh neutral validation completed November 3, 2026. No vote, assignment, Stone position or present Court action is supplied. The June 7 joint filing is now admitted as a party act through the copied sanitized filing update. It changes the stage from awaiting a proposal to having one available; it does not itself fix a boundary, issue an injunction, trigger payment or grant leave to plead.

## Event and independent completeness assessment

No. 84, Original; scheduled consideration June 29, 2000. This is original-jurisdiction implementation of the actual June 19, 1997 decision. It is not appellate review of a judgment below. No new argument or case-specific nonparticipation is established. The seated Court consists of Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Independent modeling concerns the eight Associates, with their prior positions supplying context rather than a present vote.

The joint motion, full memorandum and proposed A–G provisions have been checked against the actual predecision filing. The requested terms conform in legal structure to the prior baseline, Dinkum, Petroleum Reserve and Refuge holdings. The State does not recover a Dinkum-generated belt. The memorandum expressly identifies the jointly agreed westward extension to Point Hope, unexcepted Harrison Bay/Arco matters and uncontroverted baseline principles; those representations support the proposed intersovereign settlement without creating new adjudicated historic-title findings.

The offshore state/federal line would be fixed under section 1301(b); the reserve/refuge coastal boundaries would remain ambulatory. Those are different boundaries. The Petroleum Reserve uses highest highwater and the named lagoon/barrier descriptions; the Refuge uses extreme low water and its described bars, reefs, islands and lagoon closures. The descriptive 1960 order does not replace the controlling 1957 legally operative segregation or import the 1980 enlargement. No absent survey or new title finding is inferred.

The proposed seaward federal right preserves section 1337(g) and Paragraph C; the shoreward state right preserves the reservations, section 1313 and Paragraph C. Reciprocal noninterference relief is subject to those actual qualifications. Statutory revenue sharing is not unqualified state title or a uniform award over all revenues. The exact statutory terms, including the Alaska partial-tract and disputed-escrow provisions, are copied below.

The three lease categories must remain separate: section 7 agreements control their leases and escrow; nonagreement leases intersected by the fixed line stay with their original lessor for administration and past/future lease revenue until expiration, relinquishment or termination; and agreement leases also intersected by the line retain agreement administration and distribution. Paragraph B controls subsequent area rights after those terminating events. Pre-entry expired leases' distributions are preserved at each applicable category. The 180-day escrow deadline runs only from a decree actually entered. Third-party and lessee rights, and present/future unitization, operating, enhanced-recovery, commingling and similar agreements, remain preserved by C(2).

Leave to file the counterclaim formally completes the existing pleading posture; no merits reopening follows. Retained jurisdiction permits implementation and supplementation, and the proposed decree is otherwise final. Neither agreement nor the absence of a presently filed objection substitutes for conformity review. Entry of the conforming joint proposal and withholding or requiring correction of a genuinely nonconforming provision are lawful paths; no new hostile contention or current accounting dispute is invented.

## Readiness and source limits

Ready for independent modeling of the proposed implementing decree. The only supported additional event is the admitted June 7 filing; no reconstructed response, waiver, submission deadline or default is used. Research cutoff is June 29, 2000. All June 28 matters are uncoordinated and their unrelated substantive decisions supply no assumed title change here; any actual intervening relevant change would require freshness review.

The complete coordinate appendix remains part of the exact original filed proposal. This validator has read the substantive joint motion, memorandum and A–G terms and checked relevant source-page images, but has not independently recomputed or certified every coordinate or performed a survey. No coordinate discrepancy is asserted or used. If a decision depends on changing a particular coordinate, the actual schedule, datum and survey basis must be examined before that change; current entry analysis preserves the submitted schedule without alteration.

The earlier authorized operative handoff contained later-reporter page headers. The frozen packet instead supplies a direct predecision-file extraction, with isolated image-verified OCR repairs. No later decree, historical target disposition, lineup or reasoning is opened or adopted as a Court action.

## Validated objective facts

# Alaska decree consideration - objective neutral facts

## Identity, source and lawful posture

United States v. Alaska, No. 84, Original, is scheduled for consideration on June 29, 2000. It is an original action, not appellate review. The operative prior law is the June 19, 1997 decision in this timeline, copied in entering-law/OT_1999CHUNK9_ALASKA.md. Its implementing decree remains awaited in Standing State and the current manifest. The 1997 merits result is not reopened.

The official Supreme Court original-jurisdiction collection lists a June 7, 2000 Joint Motion for Entry of Decree, accompanying memorandum, and proposed decree. The actual document is saved at [joint motion](../sources/chunk9/alaska/joint-motion-2000-06-07.pdf). The memorandum states the parties jointly seek the attached terms. This filing is an objective party act compatible with the earlier order requiring a proposed decree. Its presentation is not a Court grant or entered decree. No new oral argument is identified. No case-specific nonparticipation is identified.

Public source: https://www.supremecourt.gov/pdfs/recordsandbriefs/1000370914/1000370914_001.pdf . Docket collection: https://www.supremecourt.gov/casedocuments/original_jurisdiction_cases.aspx . Research cutoff for operative events is June 29, 2000. The modern hosting date supplies no later law.

## The complete proposal and distinctions that matter

Read [the copied operative terms](OT_1999CHUNK9_ALASKA_PROPOSED_TERMS_FROM_FILING.md), the original proposal's Exhibit A, and [statutory source provisions](OT_1999CHUNK9_ALASKA_STATUTORY_SOURCE.md). The following is a neutral summary of requested relief, not an entered order.

A. Alaska seeks formal leave to file its counterclaim. The motion was referred to the Master in 1980; the memorandum explains that jurisdiction over its title questions was necessarily exercised in the prior merits decision. This provision formally completes that pleading step and supplies no fresh merits reopening.

B. The proposed intersovereign offshore boundary is fixed by Exhibit A, from Point Hope in the Chukchi Sea to the United States-Canada border in the Beaufort Sea. The parties' memorandum states that they agreed to extend the continuous line westward to Point Hope using the resolved issues and uncontroverted principles. It identifies Harrison Bay and the Arco pier as unexcepted Master recommendations. These are representations supporting the joint proposal, not newly adjudicated historic-title findings. The 1997 decision supplies the governing normal baseline and rejects an entitlement based on Dinkum Sands; the proposal cannot restore that entitlement.

The United States would have exclusive exploration and natural-resource rights seaward of the line against Alaska and those claiming under it, with a reciprocal noninterference injunction. The exception preserves Alaska's section 8(g), 43 U.S.C. 1337(g), revenue rights and Paragraph C's lease-administration rules. Alaska would have the corresponding rights shoreward against the United States and those claiming under it, except within the Petroleum Reserve and Arctic Refuge, subject to 43 U.S.C. 1313 and Paragraph C. The reciprocal injunction has that same qualified scope.

Section 1313 preserves lawfully and expressly acquired federal parcels and their accretions, resources and improvements; lands lawfully held under state law; lands expressly retained or ceded at statehood, excluding a merely general marginal-sea retention; lands acquired in a proprietary capacity by eminent domain, purchase, cession, gift or otherwise; lands filled, built up or reclaimed for federal use; existing federal rights in lands actually occupied under claim of right; lands or interests held for tribes, bands, groups or individual Indians; and structures or improvements constructed under the federal navigational servitude. No new parcel-specific exception finding is requested.

Section 1337(g) concerns federal leasing and coastal-state revenue sharing, including its qualified Alaska treatment for tracts partly within the three-mile band, escrow treatment during boundary disputes, later adjustment at settlement, and specified interest and payment rules. It does not give Alaska the seaward title the proposed decree otherwise denies. The decree preserves the section's operation without calculating a dollar award or adjudicating every statutory application. Its exact text is supplied so that the preservation is not mistaken for an unqualified 27-percent award over all offshore revenues.

Section 1301(b) authorizes an offshore boundary fixed by coordinates in a final Supreme Court decree to remain immobilized despite subsequent coastal change. Exhibit A uses NAD 83/WGS 84 as authoritative. NAD 27 coordinates are conversion aids, not equal alternative authority. The schedule is preserved in the original PDF; no coordinate should be retyped or silently changed from OCR. The proposal does not ask the Court to conduct a new survey.

C. Three distinct lease categories govern accounting and administration. For leases subject to section 7 agreements, escrow funds are to be distributed under those agreements within 180 days after decree entry, and their applicable terms govern administration. Existing leases outside section 7 agreements but intersected by the new fixed line remain administered by their original lessor, who keeps all past and future lease revenues; after expiration, relinquishment or termination, Paragraph B governs the area's exploration and exploitation rights. The fixing does not change distribution from former leases in that category that expired before decree entry. Where an existing lease both falls under a section 7 agreement and is intersected by the line, the agreement controls escrow distribution and administration; Paragraph B governs the area after expiration, relinquishment or termination. Fixing the line does not change distribution from former intersected agreement leases that expired before entry.

Paragraph C(2) independently preserves each sovereign's rights and obligations toward lessees and third parties, whether arising under section 7 agreements or otherwise. Present or future unitization, operating, enhanced-recovery, commingling and similar agreements are also preserved. No damages award, lease cancellation or new third-party obligation is proposed by implication.

D. The Petroleum Reserve's coastal boundary runs from the western bank of the Colville River along the highest highwater mark westward, closing the small lagoons and estuaries named in the proposed text, following the ocean side of barrier islands and sandspits within three miles and the Plover Islands, to Icy Cape. E. The Arctic Refuge's coastal boundary begins at the international boundary near Monument 1 and follows extreme low water westward, closing lagoon entrances so that the identified offshore bars, reefs, islands and separating lagoons lie within the Refuge, ending at Brownlow Point. The descriptive reference to the 1960 land order does not confer retroactive legal effect on that instrument: the prior controlling retention premise is the legally effective 1957 segregation and section 6(e). No 1980 enlargement supplies this boundary.

F. These two reservation coastal boundaries remain ambulatory and migrate with relevant physical features. That is distinct from the fixed federal-state offshore line. The parties may resolve resulting disputes through negotiation, alternative dispute resolution, or retained jurisdiction, and may jointly submit a boundary agreement for a supplemental decree. G. Jurisdiction would be retained to effectuate and supplement the decree and the parties' rights; in other respects it would be final.

## Neutral alternatives and source limits

The live question is conformity and lawful implementation. Entry of the jointly proposed terms is available if they faithfully implement the settled title rules and do not exceed lawful intersovereign settlement authority. If a specific provision materially changes the settled rights or reaches an unsupported area, the Court may withhold that provision or require a conforming proposal; the parties' agreement does not authorize silently changing prior law. There is no current adverse litigant argument or newly filed objection established by these materials. Do not invent one. The unresolved status of a hypothetical future accounting or boundary dispute supplies no present default or objection.

All eight Associates participated in the prior decision. O'Connor's baseline and Dinkum portions had all nine joins; the reserve/refuge portions had Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Thomas, joined by Scalia, disputed federal retention but accepted the implementing-decree mechanism subject to their merits position. Their present response to a conforming final decree must be modeled independently; their earlier disagreement does not itself establish a current objection or vote.

The full joint motion and memorandum are recovered, not reconstructed. The proposed coordinate appendix is preserved as supplied; the Court's legal conformity inquiry need not pretend to be an independent land survey. No numerical coordinate correction is proposed. Exact figures are available in the source PDF, which controls imperfect OCR. Only the source's actual A-G terms and schedule are presented; the later Court action is excluded from this neutral packet.

## Exact entering law

# Focused entering-law copy — ALASKA

Derived reading copy; cited authorities remain controlling. Event dates: June 26, 2000 for Jones, Mobil/Marathon and Henerey; June 29, 2000 for Alaska. No current-case historical outcome is included.

Source: `state/holdings/property-and-economic-rights.md`; exact heading: United States v. Alaska

#### United States v. Alaska

**Docket or dockets:** No. 84, Original.
**Decided:** June 19, 1997.

##### The normal baseline governs without a proved general ten-mile island-fringe rule

**Holding:** Alaska's statutory three-mile submerged-land grant is measured from the Convention's normal low-water baseline, including applicable river and bay closing rules and separate belts of qualifying islands. The evidence establishes no firm and continuing general federal rule converting all waters behind fringing islands with entrances under ten miles into inland waters; the earlier Mississippi Sound judgment does not preclude that conclusion.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Parts I–II, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Article 4 permits but does not require straight baselines; the United States made no selection here. The international twelve-mile territorial sea does not enlarge the statutory three-mile State grant. Alaska asserted general practice rather than specific historic title to Stefansson Sound; no different proved inland-water claim or general power to contract existing state territory is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** California (1965) supplies Convention definitions. The 1985 Alabama/Mississippi decision established particular historic title, not a universal fringe rule; preclusion fails even assuming its availability, without expanding Mendoza or creating an original-action exception. Nome’s artificial-extension rule and the later Alabama/Mississippi and Massachusetts decrees retain their actual geographic scopes.

##### Dinkum Sands’ frequent seasonal submergence defeats island status

**Holding:** A naturally formed feature must ordinarily remain above the agreed mean-high-water datum to qualify as an island under Convention Article 10(1). Dinkum Sands' frequent seasonal submergence below that datum defeats island status and a separate three-mile grant; isolated emergence does not create alternating island entitlement, and abnormal temporary inundation of an otherwise enduring island remains distinct.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Parts I–II, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No percentage-of-days test is adopted. Natural accretion or erosion of an established island is distinct. Article 11 supplies no low-tide-elevation entitlement at the reported distances exceeding three miles from mainland and nearby islands.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Borax and the California decree support the approximately nineteen-year mean-high-water datum, not an absolute physical-permanence condition. Measurements and the survey support the formation’s frequent submergence despite occasional emergence or maps derived from earlier observations.

##### The Petroleum Reserve instruments satisfy separate clear-inclusion and title-defeat requirements

**Holding:** Federal retention requires clear inclusion of the submerged lands and clear intent to defeat future state title for an appropriate public purpose; geography or general purpose alone does not suffice. The 1923 Petroleum Reserve order's boundary and petroleum function, together with Congress's specific Statehood Act treatment of that reserve, satisfy both requirements and retain the disputed submerged estate, under the same demanding inquiry for equal-footing and statutory territorial-sea lands.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Parts III–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Material application:** The 1923 order’s ocean-side barrier boundary, small-lagoon closures and express three-mile limit identify the estate. Ownership of the submerged petroleum deposits serves the national-defense purpose. Statehood Act §11(b)’s specific recognition of the reserve, read with that estate and function, confirms retained ownership; legislative jurisdiction alone would not establish title.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 1313(a) is not diluted by a general federal-grant presumption. Congressional confirmation makes it unnecessary to decide independent Pickett Act authority; no broad Executive retention power is announced.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Shively preserves congressional retention for appropriate public purposes. Utah Division and Montana require distinct clear inclusion and title-defeating intent; their insufficient reservoir-control and unnecessary-riverbed showings differ from this defined petroleum estate.

##### The legally operative prestatehood wildlife segregation retained the described submerged lands

**Holding:** The 1957 application and legally operative temporary-segregation regulation set the specifically described submerged lands apart for the proposed wildlife refuge before statehood. Statehood Act §6(e)'s exclusion of lands withdrawn or otherwise set apart as wildlife refuges therefore retained those lands, although the application was not a completed final reservation; lands outside its boundaries or lacking comparable legal effect are not decided.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Parts III–IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Material application:** The 1957 application’s extreme-low-water boundary, coastal features and identified aquatic habitat establish inclusion. The regulation immediately barred settlement, location, sale, selection, entry, lease and other disposals, including mining and mineral leasing, only insofar as the proposed reservation would prevent them; administrative jurisdiction remained unchanged. Those conditions nevertheless gave the specific segregation present legal effect.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 6(e) supplies title-defeating intent, separately from §6(m)’s incorporation of the Submerged Lands Act. Neither general ownership nor wildlife purpose alone suffices. The 1960 reservation receives no retroactive effect; the 1980 enlargement does not set the original boundary. Scalia and Thomas do not join the geographic determination.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Alaska’s baseline, Dinkum and Petroleum Reserve exceptions are overruled; the United States’ original Range exception is sustained. The parties must propose a conforming implementing decree; jurisdiction is retained and each bears its own costs. No final coordinate schedule, new survey, damages or oil-accounting award is entered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Utah Division and Montana’s distinct inclusion and retention requirements govern. The statutory wildlife exclusion reaches this legally effective temporary segregation without treating every pending application as a completed reservation.

Source: `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`; exact heading: Ordinary high-water emergence for natural island status

### Ordinary high-water emergence for natural island status

**Governing question:** Whether a naturally formed coastal feature qualifies as an island for a separate submerged-land belt.

**Current rule:** Convention Article 10(1) requires a naturally formed feature ordinarily to remain above the agreed mean-high-water datum. Frequent ordinary seasonal submergence defeats island status; intermittent emergence does not create alternating island entitlement. Abnormal temporary inundation of an otherwise enduring island is different. Mean high water, averaged over approximately nineteen years, is the datum, not absolute permanence or an adopted percentage-of-days test.

**Authority by component:**
- Island definition and seasonal boundary: United States v. Alaska, United States v. Alaska — II. Dinkum Sands does not satisfy the island definition, O'Connor Parts I–II, all nine, 1997-06-19.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Alaska, 1997-06-19.

**Exceptions and limits:** Natural accretion or erosion of an established island and independently qualifying low-tide elevations retain their distinct questions. This rule does not enlarge the statutory three-mile State grant to the international twelve-mile territorial sea.

**Holdings navigation:** United States v. Alaska (1997-06-19) — Property and Economic Rights / Submerged lands and fixed coastal baselines.

Source: `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`; exact heading: Coordinate fixation of state-federal submerged-land boundaries

### Coordinate fixation of state-federal submerged-land boundaries

**Governing question:** Whether a state-federal submerged-land boundary fixed by coordinates in a final Supreme Court decree moves with the coast.

**Current rule:** Under 43 U.S.C. §1301(b), a state-federal boundary fixed by coordinates in a final decree of this Court remains immobilized at the decreed coordinates and does not move with later coastline change. The decree’s complete coordinate schedule, including its datum, zones, precision, sequence, segments and arcs, controls; an illustrative map does not replace it.

**Authority by component:**

- Coordinate-fixation consequence and schedule priority: Canonical Decision Record `united_states_v_louisiana_supplemental_decree_1998-10-13.md`, Public Projection / Holdings / The complete coordinates fix and immobilize the boundary, 1998-10-13.

**Present operation effective:** 1998-10-13.

**Exceptions and limits:** This rule does not allocate an unpresented State’s territory, decide reserved property interests or change the entitlement used to select the decreed line.

**Holdings navigation:** [United States v. Louisiana (Texas Boundary Case) — The complete coordinates fix and immobilize the boundary](HOLDINGS.md#the-complete-coordinates-fix-and-immobilize-the-boundary).

## Exact admitted filing update

# Alaska pending-filing update

Effective June 7, 2000, the United States and Alaska jointly filed the verified motion, memorandum and proposed decree in No. 84, Original. The original action therefore has the requested implementing proposal available for consideration rather than awaiting its submission. The filing creates no Court action, vote, holding, final boundary, payment obligation or implementation deadline. The June 19, 1997 decision remains controlling. The proposal awaits the scheduled June 29 consideration. Source: the actual June 7 joint motion in the Supreme Court Library original-jurisdiction collection. This change has no material effect on another completed matter.

3. 
The boundary described in Exhibit A shall remain 
fixed for purposes of the Submerged Lands Act. 
C. 
Distribution of Revenues in Escrow and Admini- 
stration of Leases 
1. 
The United States and the State of Alaska shall 
resolve accounting and administration issues arising 
from the past issuance of offshore oil and gas leases in 
disputed areas based on the following principles: 
a. 
Existing and Former Leases That Are Subject 
to Section 
7 Agreements. During the course 
of 
this litigation, the United States and the State of 
Alaska entered into agreements under Section 7 of 
the Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act, 43 U.S.C. 
1336, and Alaska Stat. §§ 38.05.020 and 38.05.137, to 
allow mineral leasing 
of submerged 
lands 
in 
disputed areas. Under the terms of those “Section 7 
Agreements,” lease revenues are held in income-

4a 
producing escrow accounts for distribution based on 
the outcome of the litigation. 
No later than 180 
days after entry of this Decree, the funds held in 
escrow accounts shall be distributed in accordance 
with the distribution provisions contained in the 
Section 
7 Agreements. The United States and the 
State of Alaska shall carry out all applicable pro- 
visions and terms of the Section 
7 Agreements and 
shall administer the leases in accordance with the 
provisions therein. 
b. Existing 
and Former Leases 
That 
Are 
Affected by the Fixed Federal-State Boundary 
Described in Exhibit A. The United States and the 
State 
of Alaska have issued mineral leases 
in 
offshore areas that were not in dispute on the date 
of lease issuance and are therefore not subject to 
Section 
7 Agreements. Those leases may be inter- 
sected, however, by the fixed federal-state boun- 
dary described in Exhibit A, which is based upon 
surveys conducted after the lease dates. 
Leases 
existing on the date of this Decree and not covered 
by Section 
7 Agreements, but intersected by the 
fixed federal-state boundary described in Exhibit 
A, shall continue to be administered by the original 
lessor, who shall have the exclusive right to all past 
and future revenues from the lease. Following the 
expiration, relinquishment, or termination of such 
leases, the rights to explore and exploit the natural 
resources within the area that was leased shall be 
determined solely in accordance with Paragraph B 
of this Decree. 
The distribution of revenues from 
former leases that expired before the date of this 
Decree, that were not covered by Section 7 Agree- 
ments, but that would have been intersected by the

5a 
fixed federal-state boundary described in Exhibit 
A, shall not be affected by the fixing of the federal- 
state boundary. 
c. 
Existing and Former Leases That Are Both 
Subject to Section 7 Agreements and Affected by the 
Fixed 
Federal-State Boundary 
Described 
in 
Exhibit A. 
In the event that an existing lease is 
subject to a Section 7 Agreement and is intersected 
by the fixed federal-state boundary described in 
Exhibit A, the funds held in escrow shall be distrib- 
uted, and the lease shall be administered, in ac- 
cordance with the provisions of the Section 7 Agree- 
ment. Following the expiration, relinquishment, or 
termination of the lease, the rights to explore and 
exploit the natural resources within the area that 
was leased shall be determined solely in accordance 
with Paragraph B of this Decree. The distribution 
of revenues from former leases that expired before 
the date of this Decree, that were subject to Section 
7 Agreements, but that would have been inter- 
sected by the fixed federal-state boundary de- 
scribed in Exhibit A, shall not be affected by the 
fixing of the federal-state boundary. 
2. 
This Decree shall not affect the rights or obli- 
— 
gations of the United States or the State of Alaska with 
respect to its lessees or third parties, whether arising 
from the Section 
7 Agreements or otherwise. This De- 
cree shall not affect any rights or obligations arising 
under present or future unitization, operating, en- 
hanced recovery, commingling, or other similar agree- 
ments between the parties or with others.

6a 
D. 
The Coastal Boundary of the National Petroleum 
Reserve-Alaska 
The coastal boundary of the National Petroleum 
Reserve-Alaska is a continuous line, as described in 
Executive Order No. 3739-A (1923), that begins at the 
western bank of the Colville River and follows the 
highest highwater mark westerly, extending across the 
entrances 
of small lagoons, including Peard Bay, 
Wainwright Inlet, the Kuk River, Kugrua Bay and 
River, and other small bays and river estuaries, and 
following the ocean side of barrier islands and sandspits 
within three miles of shore and the ocean side of the 
Plover Islands, to the northwestern extremity of Icy 
Cape, approximately 70° 21’ N., 161° 46’ W. 
E. 
The Coastal Boundary of the Arctic National 
Wildlife Refuge 
The coastal boundary of the Arctic National Wildlife 
Refuge is a continuous line, as described in Public Land 
Order No. 2214, 25 Fed. Reg. 12,598 (1960), that begins 
at the intersection of the International Boundary line 
between the State of Alaska and Yukon Territory, 
Canada, with the line of extreme low water of the 
Arctic Ocean in the vicinity of Monument 
1 of said 
International Boundary line, and follows the line of ex- 
treme low water westerly, extending across the en- 
trances of lagoons such that all offshore bars, reefs and 
islands, and lagoons that separate them from the 
mainland, are part of the Refuge, to Brownlow Point, at 
approximately 70° 10° N., 145 51° W.

Ta 
F. 
Resolution of Disputes Respecting the Coastal 
Boundaries of the National Petroleum Reserve- 
Alaska and the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge 
The coastal boundaries of the National Petroleum 
Reserve-Alaska and the Arctic National Wildlife Re- 
fuge are ambulatory and will therefore migrate as a 
result of changes in relevant physical features. 
The 
United States and the State of Alaska may resolve 
disputes arising from those changes through negotia- 
tion, through alternative methods of dispute resolution, 
or through invocation of this Court’s retained juris- 
diction in accordance with Paragraph G. 
The United 
States and the State of Alaska may jointly submit to 
this Court, for entry as a supplement to this Decree, 
any agreement respecting the location of the coastal 
boundary of the National Petroleum Reserve-Alaska or 
of the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge. 
G. 
Retention of Jurisdiction 
The Court retains jurisdiction to entertain such fur- 
ther proceedings, enter such orders, and issue such 
writs as from time to time may be determined neces- 
sary or advisable to effectuate and supplement this 
Decree and the rights of the respective parties. 
In all 
other respects, this Decree is final.


## Exhibit A

The complete proposed Exhibit A is the original PDF, printed pages 8a onward. Its NAD 83/WGS 84 coordinates are authoritative; NAD 27 conversions are convenience values. The schedule is preserved verbatim as part of the original source, not reconstructed or independently surveyed here. No coordinate is changed by this handoff.
